================================================================================
    STAGE 5: UPPER PRIMARY — GRADES 5-6 (Age ~10-12) — QUESTIONS AND ANSWERS
    Fractions, decimals, ratio
================================================================================

How to use this file (note for the parent / teacher)
----------------------------------------------------
- There are 35 topics (5.1 to 5.35, the same as in topic.txt). Each topic
  has:
    * WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN   the checklist of skills in that topic
    * PART A — DESCRIPTIVE        12 questions (D1-D12): real-life word
                                  problems, worked examples and "explain
                                  why" questions, each with the answer and
                                  "Teacher explains" (the method step by
                                  step as a teacher shows it on the board,
                                  common mistakes, and a "Now you try"
                                  question for the child)
    * PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY  12 questions (M1-M12): pure number,
                                  symbol and diagram drills with answers.
                                  Most have many parts (a, b, c ...) so
                                  that EVERY case of this stage is
                                  practised — every sign combination with
                                  integers, every kind of fraction and
                                  decimal operation, every common
                                  fraction-decimal-percent conversion,
                                  every binary number to 31, and so on —
                                  not just one example.
    * TEACHING TIPS               what to do if the child struggles, and
                                  how to make it harder
- In total: 420 descriptive + 420 mathematical questions, then a mixed
  revision test (equal descriptive and mathematical parts) and a readiness
  checklist for Stage 6.
- What is new in Stage 5: very large numbers and powers of ten; HCF and
  LCM; all four operations with FRACTIONS and DECIMALS; PERCENTAGES,
  RATIO and RATE; NEGATIVE NUMBERS; the first ALGEBRA (variables,
  expressions, equations, nth term); coordinates; angle facts,
  constructions, circles, area of triangles and trapezia, volume and nets;
  averages and probability; money maths (profit, loss, discount, interest,
  budgets), speed, problem-solving strategies, puzzles and number bases.
- Order: roughly follow the topic numbers. Suggested strands to run side
  by side through the year:
    Number              5.1-5.4, 5.35
    Fractions/decimals  5.5-5.10
    Ratio & proportion  5.11-5.13, 5.30-5.32
    Integers & algebra  5.14-5.19
    Geometry & measure  5.20-5.27
    Data & chance       5.28-5.29
    Thinking skills     5.33-5.34
- Method: concrete → pictorial → abstract still matters. Use fraction
  strips and area models, 10×10 percent grids, double number lines, bar
  models, number lines with negatives, algebra tiles or balance pictures,
  compasses and protractors, and nets cut from paper.
- Write working neatly: one step per line, "=" signs lined up, units
  written with every answer. Good layout prevents most mistakes.
- Do 40-45 minutes a day: 2-3 descriptive questions and 2-3 drills per
  session, plus 5 minutes of mental arithmetic. Repeat a topic over
  several days with new numbers.
- Always ask: "How did you work it out?", "Can you check it another
  way?" and "Is your answer reasonable?" (estimate first).
- Revise Stage 4 (A4.txt) whenever the child finds something difficult.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.1 — LARGE NUMBERS (LAKHS/CRORES AND MILLIONS/BILLIONS SYSTEMS)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Read and write numbers up to billions (international system) and up to
    hundreds of crores (Indian system), in numerals and in words.
  - International system: the digits are grouped in threes from the right, each
    group a PERIOD: ones, thousands, millions, billions. Commas after every
    three digits: 4,305,120,000 is "four billion three hundred five million one
    hundred twenty thousand".
  - Indian system: the first comma comes after three digits from the right, then
    a comma after every TWO digits: 43,05,12,000 is "forty-three crore five lakh
    twelve thousand". The places are ones, tens, hundreds, thousands, ten
    thousands, lakhs, ten lakhs, crores, ten crores, and 100 crore is one arab.
  - Every place is worth 10 times the place on its right, in BOTH systems. The
    place value of a digit = the digit × the value of its place: in 6,47,25,803
    the 4 is worth 40,00,000 (forty lakh = 4,000,000).
  - Convert between the systems: 1 lakh = 100 thousand; 10 lakh = 1 million; 1
    crore = 10 million; 10 crore = 100 million; 100 crore = 1 arab = 1 billion.
  - Compare and order large numbers: more digits means bigger; with the same
    number of digits compare from the left. Rewrite numbers in the same system
    before comparing.
  - Round to the nearest lakh, million or crore by looking at the digit just to
    the right of that place (5 or more: round up).
  - Use large numbers from real life: populations, distances in space, budgets
    and prices, and say them sensibly ("about 143 crore", "about 1.43 billion").

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. The census of a state counted 3,05,48,217 people. Read the number aloud in
    the Indian system and write it in words.
         C  TL  L  TTh  Th  H  T  O
         3  0   5   4   8   2  1  7
    Answer: "three crore five lakh forty-eight thousand two hundred seventeen".
    Teacher explains: Put the digits in an Indian place value chart (C = crores,
    TL = ten lakhs, L = lakhs, TTh = ten thousands, Th = thousands). The Indian
    commas split the number into groups: 3 | 05 | 48 | 217. Read each group and
    say its name: 3 crore, 05 lakh, 48 thousand, and then the last three digits
    as usual, 217. The 0 in the ten lakhs place is not spoken. A common mistake
    is to say "three crore zero five lakh" or to forget the word "lakh". Now you
    try: read 7,20,05,300 in words. (seven crore twenty lakh five thousand three
    hundred)

D2. Write the same number, 3,05,48,217, with international commas and read it in
    the international system.
         TM  M  HTh  TTh  Th  H  T  O
         3   0   5    4   8   2  1  7
    Answer: 30,548,217, "thirty million five hundred forty-eight thousand two
    hundred seventeen".
    Teacher explains: Remove the Indian commas and put commas after every THREE
    digits from the right: 30548217 becomes 30,548,217. The periods are: 30
    millions, 548 thousands, 217 ones. Read each period as a number up to 999
    and then say the period name: "thirty million", "five hundred forty-eight
    thousand", "two hundred seventeen". Notice that one crore is ten million, so
    three crore is thirty million. The number is the same; only the grouping and
    the names change. Now you try: write 7,20,05,300 in the international way
    and in words. (72,005,300, seventy-two million five thousand three hundred)

D3. A town spent nine crore six lakh fifty thousand rupees on a new bridge.
    Write the amount in numerals with Indian commas and with international
    commas.
    Answer: ₹9,06,50,000 (Indian) and ₹90,650,000 (international).
    Teacher explains: Build it group by group from the left: nine crore gives 9
    and a comma; six lakh must fill TWO places, so write 06 and a comma; fifty
    thousand fills two places: 50 and a comma; there is nothing more, so the
    last three places are 000. Answer 9,06,50,000. For international commas
    regroup in threes from the right: 90,650,000, that is ninety million six
    hundred fifty thousand. The common mistake is 9,6,50,000 or 9,60,50,000:
    every Indian group after the first must have exactly two digits, except the
    last group of three. Now you try: write "twelve crore forty thousand nine"
    in numerals. (12,00,40,009 = 120,040,009)

D4. Write the place and the place value of every digit in 6,47,25,803.
        digit  Indian place   international place      value
        6      crores         ten millions        6,00,00,000
        4      ten lakhs      millions             40,00,000
        7      lakhs          hundred thousands     7,00,000
        2      ten thousands  ten thousands           20,000
        5      thousands      thousands                5,000
        8      hundreds       hundreds                   800
        0      tens           tens                         0
        3      ones           ones                         3
    Answer: The 6 is worth 6 crore = 60 million; the 4 is worth 40 lakh = 4
    million; the 7 is worth 7 lakh = 700 thousand; the 2 is worth 20 thousand;
    the 5 is worth 5 thousand; the 8 is worth 800; the 0 is worth 0 and the 3 is
    worth 3.
    Teacher explains: The FACE value of a digit is the digit itself; its PLACE
    value is the digit × the value of its place. Go from the right: ones, tens,
    hundreds, thousands, ten thousands, lakhs, ten lakhs, crores. So 4 in the
    ten lakhs place is 4 × 10,00,000 = 40,00,000, which is 4,000,000 in
    international commas: 4 million. Adding all the place values gives the
    number back: 6,00,00,000 + 40,00,000 + 7,00,000 + 20,000 + 5,000 + 800 + 3 =
    6,47,25,803. Now you try: what is the place value of 9 in 2,19,08,450?
    (9,00,000 = 900,000, nine lakh)

D5. Explain why 10 lakh = 1 million, 1 crore = 10 million and 100 crore = 1
    billion.
        Indian commas    Indian name   International
        1,00,000         1 lakh        100 thousand = 100,000
        10,00,000        10 lakh       1 million = 1,000,000
        1,00,00,000      1 crore       10 million = 10,000,000
        10,00,00,000     10 crore      100 million = 100,000,000
        1,00,00,00,000   100 crore     1 billion = 1,000,000,000
    Answer: 1 lakh = 100,000, so 10 lakh = 10 × 100,000 = 1,000,000 = 1 million.
    1 crore = 100 lakh = 10,000,000 = 10 million. 100 crore = 100 × 10,000,000 =
    1,000,000,000 = 1 billion (also called 1 arab).
    Teacher explains: Line up the two systems on one place value chart. Both
    have ones, tens, hundreds, thousands and ten thousands. Then the names
    differ: the 6th place from the right is a LAKH in India and a HUNDRED
    THOUSAND internationally; the 7th place is TEN LAKH and ONE MILLION; the 8th
    place is CRORE and TEN MILLION; the 9th place is TEN CRORE and HUNDRED
    MILLION; the 10th place is ARAB (100 crore) and BILLION. Count the zeros to
    check: 1 crore = 1,00,00,000 has 7 zeros, and so does 10,000,000. A useful
    rule: millions × 10 = lakhs, and crores × 10 = millions. Now you try: how
    many lakh make 5 million? (50 lakh)

D6. A film earns ₹250 crore. How much is that in millions of rupees and in
    billions of rupees?
    Answer: ₹2,500 million = ₹2.5 billion. 250 crore = 250 × 10 million = 2,500
    million, and 2,500 million = 2.5 billion.
    Teacher explains: Change crores to millions by multiplying by 10, because 1
    crore = 10 million: 250 × 10 = 2,500 million. Change millions to billions by
    dividing by 1,000, because 1 billion = 1,000 million: 2,500 ÷ 1,000 = 2.5.
    Check with digits: 250 crore = 2,50,00,00,000 = 2,500,000,000, which is read
    as two billion five hundred million. Going the other way: millions ÷ 10 =
    crores and billions × 100 = crores. News reports use both systems, so it is
    useful to change quickly. Now you try: ₹45 million in crores and in lakhs.
    (4.5 crore = 450 lakh)

D7. The distance from the Earth to the Sun is about 149,600,000 km. Write it in
    words in the international system, then with Indian commas and in the Indian
    words.
    Answer: "one hundred forty-nine million six hundred thousand" km;
    14,96,00,000 km = "fourteen crore ninety-six lakh" km.
    Teacher explains: International: the periods are 149 | 600 | 000, so it is
    149 million and 600 thousand. Indian: regroup as 3 digits then 2s from the
    right: 14,96,00,000, that is 14 crore, 96 lakh. Check with the conversion
    rule: millions ÷ 10 gives crores, and 149.6 ÷ 10 is 14.96, so 149.6 million
    is 14.96 crore, which is 14 crore 96 lakh. Scientists round it to "about 150
    million km" (about 15 crore km). Now you try: the Moon is about 384,400 km
    from the Earth; write it in Indian commas and words. (3,84,400, three lakh
    eighty-four thousand four hundred)

D8. Which is greater: 4,56,78,912 (written in the Indian way) or 45,678,921
    (written in the international way)?
    Answer: 45,678,921 is greater. Both are 45678912 and 45678921 without
    commas, and 45,678,912 < 45,678,921.
    Teacher explains: Different commas can trick the eye. First write both
    numbers in the same system: 4,56,78,912 = 45,678,912. Now both have 8
    digits, so compare from the left: 4 = 4, 5 = 5, 6 = 6, 7 = 7, 8 = 8, 9 = 9,
    then 1 < 2 in the tens place. So 45,678,912 < 45,678,921. The digits in the
    ones place do not matter once a difference is found. If one number has more
    digits (and no leading zero) it is greater at once. Now you try: compare
    9,99,99,999 and 100,000,000. (9,99,99,999 < 100,000,000; the second has 9
    digits)

D9. Commonly quoted 2011 census populations of five cities (city limits) are:
    Bengaluru 84,43,675; Mumbai 1,24,42,373; Ahmedabad 55,77,940; Delhi
    1,10,34,555; Hyderabad 69,93,262. Order them from smallest to largest.
    Answer: 55,77,940 (Ahmedabad) < 69,93,262 (Hyderabad) < 84,43,675
    (Bengaluru) < 1,10,34,555 (Delhi) < 1,24,42,373 (Mumbai).
    Teacher explains: First count the digits (with Indian commas count the
    groups): 1,24,42,373 and 1,10,34,555 have 8 digits, crores; the other three
    have 7 digits, lakhs. So Mumbai and Delhi are the two largest; compare them:
    both have 1 crore, then 2 > 1 in the ten lakhs place, so Mumbai is largest.
    Among the 7-digit numbers compare the ten lakhs digit: 5 < 6 < 8. Line up
    the numbers under each other, right-aligned, so that equal places sit in the
    same column. Now you try: order 1,00,00,000; 99,99,999; 1,00,00,001.
    (99,99,999 < 1,00,00,000 < 1,00,00,001)

D10. A state's budget for schools is ₹3,47,68,250. Round it to the nearest lakh,
     to the nearest crore and (in international form) to the nearest million.
     Answer: Nearest lakh: ₹3,48,00,000. Nearest crore: ₹3,00,00,000. Nearest
     million: ₹35,000,000.
     Teacher explains: To round to the nearest lakh, look at the lakhs digit (7
     in 3,47,68,250) and at the digit just to its RIGHT, the ten thousands
     digit: 6. It is 5 or more, so round UP: 47 lakh becomes 48 lakh and all the
     places after it become 0: 3,48,00,000. Nearest crore: the crore digit is 3,
     the next digit is 4 (less than 5), so round DOWN: 3,00,00,000. Nearest
     million: in international form it is 34,768,250; the millions digit is 4
     and the next digit is 7, so round up to 35,000,000. Always underline the
     rounding place and circle the digit to its right. Now you try: round
     5,05,50,000 to the nearest crore and nearest lakh. (5,00,00,000;
     5,06,00,000)

D11. The world population passed about 8,000,000,000 people in 2022. How many
     crore is that? How many arab? India's population is about 143 crore; write
     this in billions.
     Answer: 8,000,000,000 = 800 crore = 8 arab = 8 billion. 143 crore = 1,430
     million = 1.43 billion.
     Teacher explains: 8,000,000,000 has 9 zeros. 1 crore has 7 zeros, so
     8,000,000,000 ÷ 1,00,00,000 = 800: it is 800 crore. 1 arab = 100 crore = 1
     billion, so 800 crore = 8 arab = 8 billion. For India: crores × 10 =
     millions, so 143 crore = 1,430 million; then ÷ 1,000 gives 1.43 billion.
     The word "arab" is used less today; newspapers usually say "800 crore" or
     "8 billion". Now you try: 35 crore in millions and billions. (350 million =
     0.35 billion)

D12. Use each of the digits 0, 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8 exactly once to make the
     greatest and the smallest 9-digit numbers. Write both in both systems and
     find their difference.
     Answer: Greatest 876,543,210 = 87,65,43,210; smallest 102,345,678 =
     10,23,45,678; difference 876,543,210 − 102,345,678 = 774,197,532.
     Teacher explains: For the GREATEST number put the biggest digits in the
     biggest places: 8 7 6 5 4 3 2 1 0. For the SMALLEST put the smallest digits
     first, but a number cannot start with 0, so the first digit is 1, then 0,
     then 2 3 4 5 6 7 8. In words the greatest is "eighty-seven crore sixty-five
     lakh forty-three thousand two hundred ten" (Indian) or "eight hundred
     seventy-six million five hundred forty-three thousand two hundred ten".
     Subtract in columns as usual. Now you try: the greatest and smallest
     8-digit numbers; what comes after the greatest? (99,999,999 and 10,000,000;
     then 100,000,000 = 10 crore)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write each number with Indian commas (3 digits, then groups of 2).
    a) 490457        b) 996651        c) 2003541       d) 8312087
    e) 7609680       f) 87199058      g) 19075080      h) 90989509
    i) 73013295      j) 885077767     k) 962004506     l) 111089050
    m) 810321383     n) 9708210429    o) 4880840900    p) 90810500500
    q) 100000        r) 1000000       s) 10000000      t) 1000000000

             e) 76,09,680   f) 8,71,99,058   g) 1,90,75,080   h) 9,09,89,509

             o) 4,88,08,40,900   p) 90,81,05,00,500   q) 1,00,000   r) 10,00,000
             s) 1,00,00,000   t) 1,00,00,00,000

    Answers: a) 160,950   b) 9,611,011   c) 7,462,754   d) 19,327,092

         sixty-seven thousand eight hundred ninety
      f) 99,99,99,999, ninety-nine crore ninety-nine lakh ninety-nine thousand
         nine hundred ninety-nine
      g) 10,01,00,100, ten crore one lakh one hundred
      h) 6,00,00,600, six crore six hundred
      i) 4,01,02,03,040, four hundred one crore two lakh three thousand forty
      j) 1,11,11,111, one crore eleven lakh eleven thousand one hundred eleven

M6. Name the place of the FIRST (leftmost) copy of the given digit in the Indian
    and international systems, and give its place value.
    a) 7 in 372,150,460      b) 4 in 48,000,000
    c) 5 in 5,000,000,000    d) 9 in 19,080,000
    e) 3 in 263,100,000      f) 6 in 3,642,100
    g) 8 in 81,000,000       h) 2 in 20,000,000,000
    i) 1 in 91,000,000       j) 0 in 10,200,300
    k) 4 in 740,000          l) 6 in 506,000,000
    m) 5 in 1,150,000,000    n) 8 in 8,000,800
    o) 3 in 30,000,000       p) 9 in 912,345,678
    Answers:
      a) crores / ten millions, 70,000,000
      b) crores / ten millions, 40,000,000
      c) arabs / billions, 5,000,000,000
      d) ten lakhs / millions, 9,000,000
      e) ten lakhs / millions, 3,000,000
      f) lakhs / hundred thousands, 600,000
      g) crores / ten millions, 80,000,000
      h) ten arabs / ten billions, 20,000,000,000
      i) ten lakhs / millions, 1,000,000
      j) ten lakhs / millions, 0
      k) ten thousands / ten thousands, 40,000
      l) ten lakhs / millions, 6,000,000
      m) crores / ten millions, 50,000,000
      n) ten lakhs / millions, 8,000,000
      o) crores / ten millions, 30,000,000
      p) ten crores / hundred millions, 900,000,000

M7. Write in expanded form using Indian commas (5,03,07,060 = 5,00,00,000 +
    3,00,000 + 7,000 + 60).
    a) 5,03,07,060      b) 40,80,00,900     c) 1,23,45,678
    d) 6,00,06,00,006   e) 9,09,09,090      f) 70,00,00,007
    g) 2,05,00,03,000   h) 3,10,00,013
    Answers:

      b) 40,00,00,000 + 80,00,000 + 900
      c) 1,00,00,000 + 20,00,000 + 3,00,000 + 40,000 + 5,000 + 600 + 70 + 8
      d) 6,00,00,00,000 + 6,00,000 + 6
      e) 9,00,00,000 + 9,00,000 + 9,000 + 90
      f) 70,00,00,000 + 7
      g) 2,00,00,00,000 + 5,00,00,000 + 3,000
      h) 3,00,00,000 + 10,00,000 + 10 + 3

M8. Fill in the blank.
    a) 1 million = __ lakh       b) 1 crore = __ million
    c) 1 billion = __ crore      d) 1 arab = __ crore
    e) 1 crore = __ lakh         f) 1 lakh = __ thousand
    g) 7 million = __ lakh       h) 3 crore = __ million
    i) 45 lakh = __ million      j) 2.5 billion = __ crore
    k) 70 million = __ crore     l) 600 crore = __ billion
    m) 25 lakh = __ thousand     n) 12 crore = __ million
    o) 8 billion = __ million    p) 350 million = __ crore
    q) 1.5 crore = __ lakh       r) 500 thousand = __ lakh
    s) 4 arab = __ billion       t) 90 lakh = __ crore
    u) 3.2 million = __ lakh     v) 15 billion = __ crore
    w) 1 million = __ thousand   x) 10 crore = __ billion
    Answers: a) 10   b) 10   c) 100   d) 100   e) 100   f) 100   g) 70   h) 30
             i) 4.5   j) 250   k) 7   l) 6   m) 2,500   n) 120   o) 8,000
             p) 35   q) 150   r) 5   s) 4   t) 0.9   u) 32   v) 1,500   w) 1,000
             x) 0.1

M9. Write <, > or = . Some numbers use Indian commas; change them to the same
    system first.
    a) 4,567,891 __ 4,568,791            b) 9,00,00,000 __ 9,000,000
    c) 12,34,56,789 __ 123,456,789       d) 99,999,999 __ 100,000,000
    e) 5,00,00,000 __ 50,000,000         f) 1,000,000 __ 10,00,000
    g) 7,08,090 __ 780,090               h) 3,004,005 __ 3,040,005
    i) 2,50,00,00,000 __ 2,500,000,000   j) 987,654,321 __ 987,654,312
    k) 1,00,00,000 __ 9,999,999          l) 500,500,500 __ 505,000,500
    m) 11,00,000 __ 1,010,000            n) 12,000,000 __ 12,00,00,000
    o) 3,12,00,000 __ 31,200,000         p) 4,000,000,000 __ 39,99,99,999
    q) 6,66,00,000 __ 66,060,000         r) 1,001,000 __ 10,00,100
    s) 8,00,00,000 __ 80,000,000         t) 7,654,321 __ 76,54,320
    Answers: a) <   b) >   c) =   d) <   e) =   f) =   g) <   h) <   i) =   j) >
             k) >   l) <   m) >   n) <   o) =   p) >   q) >   r) >   s) =   t) >

M10. Write in ascending order (smallest first). Indian commas are used.
     a) 45,67,890; 45,76,890; 45,67,809; 46,57,890
     b) 1,00,00,000; 99,99,999; 1,00,00,001; 10,00,000
     c) 30,50,00,000; 35,00,00,000; 30,05,00,000; 30,55,00,000
     d) 12,34,56,789; 12,34,65,789; 12,34,56,798; 13,24,56,789
     e) 90,90,909; 99,09,090; 90,09,909; 99,00,990
     f) 2,50,00,00,000; 25,00,00,000; 2,05,00,00,000; 2,00,50,00,000
     g) 7,07,07,070; 7,70,70,700; 7,07,70,070; 7,00,77,007
     h) 10,00,100; 10,01,000; 10,10,000; 10,00,010
     Answers:
       a) 45,67,809 < 45,67,890 < 45,76,890 < 46,57,890
       b) 10,00,000 < 99,99,999 < 1,00,00,000 < 1,00,00,001
       c) 30,05,00,000 < 30,50,00,000 < 30,55,00,000 < 35,00,00,000
       d) 12,34,56,789 < 12,34,56,798 < 12,34,65,789 < 13,24,56,789
       e) 90,09,909 < 90,90,909 < 99,00,990 < 99,09,090
       f) 25,00,00,000 < 2,00,50,00,000 < 2,05,00,00,000 < 2,50,00,00,000
       g) 7,00,77,007 < 7,07,07,070 < 7,07,70,070 < 7,70,70,700
       h) 10,00,010 < 10,00,100 < 10,01,000 < 10,10,000

M11. Round each number to the nearest lakh; to the nearest crore (Indian
     commas); and to the nearest million (international commas).
     a) 3,47,68,250      b) 55,50,000        c) 7,21,49,999
     d) 9,99,50,000      e) 40,50,50,000     f) 67,89,012

       f) 68,00,000; 1,00,00,000; 7,000,000
       g) 9,50,00,000; 10,00,00,000; 95,000,000
       h) 5,00,00,000; 5,00,00,000; 50,000,000
       i) 1,23,46,00,000; 1,23,00,00,000; 1,235,000,000
       j) 85,00,000; 1,00,00,000; 8,000,000
       k) 4,50,00,000; 5,00,00,000; 45,000,000
       l) 5,40,00,000; 5,00,00,000; 54,000,000
       m) 1,95,00,000; 2,00,00,000; 20,000,000
       n) 25,00,00,000; 25,00,00,000; 250,000,000

M12. Find each number. Give the answer with international commas.
     a) 1 more than 9,999,999               b) 1 less than 10,000,000
     c) 1 more than 99,999,999              d) 1 less than 1,000,000,000
     e) 10 lakh more than 45,600,000        f) 10 lakh less than 100,000,000
     g) 1 crore more than 95,000,000        h) 1 crore less than 1,005,000,000
     i) 1 million more than 9,500,000       j) 1 million less than 1,000,000,000
     k) 1 lakh more than 9,950,000          l) 1 lakh less than 10,050,000
     m) 1 billion more than 999,000,000     n) 100 crore less than 2,000,000,000
     Answers: a) 10,000,000   b) 9,999,999   c) 100,000,000   d) 999,999,999
              e) 46,600,000   f) 99,000,000   g) 105,000,000   h) 995,000,000
              i) 10,500,000   j) 999,000,000   k) 10,050,000   l) 9,950,000
              m) 1,999,000,000   n) 1,000,000,000

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make a long place value chart on a strip of paper with both sets
  of names (lakh/hundred thousand, ten lakh/million, crore/ten million) and
  slide digit cards along it; practise putting in commas with a pencil before
  reading; say numbers group by group. Harder: real data hunts in newspapers
  (budgets, populations, distances in space) and converting them between the
  systems; ten arab and trillion (1,000,000,000,000 = 1 lakh crore); estimating
  how long it would take to count to a crore at one number per second (about 116
  days without stopping).


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.2 — POWERS OF 10 AND EXPONENT NOTATION
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - An EXPONENT (power, index) tells how many times the BASE is used as a
    factor: 10⁴ = 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 10,000. Read 10⁴ as "10 to the power 4" or
    "10 to the fourth". 10² is "10 squared", 10³ is "10 cubed".
  - 10ⁿ is 1 followed by n zeros: 10¹ = 10, 10² = 100, 10³ = 1,000, ..., 10⁹ =
    1,000,000,000. And 10⁰ = 1 (no zeros).
  - Named powers: 10³ = thousand, 10⁵ = lakh (hundred thousand), 10⁶ = million
    (ten lakh), 10⁷ = crore (ten million), 10⁹ = billion (arab).
  - Other bases work the same way: 2⁵ = 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 32, 3⁴ = 81, 5³ =
    125. The exponent is NOT a multiplier: 2⁵ is not 2 × 5.
  - Expanded form with powers of 10: 4,305 = 4 × 10³ + 3 × 10² + 5 × 10⁰.
  - Multiplying powers of 10: ADD the exponents (count all the zeros): 10³ × 10⁴
    = 10⁷. Dividing: SUBTRACT the exponents: 10⁸ ÷ 10³ = 10⁵.
  - Multiply a number by 10ⁿ by moving its digits n places to the left: 36 × 10⁵
    = 3,600,000.
  - Standard form (for very big numbers): a number from 1 up to (but not
    including) 10, times a power of 10: 4,500,000 = 4.5 × 10⁶.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. What does 10⁴ mean? Write it as a multiplication and find its value.
        10¹ = 10                  =     10
        10² = 10 × 10             =    100
        10³ = 10 × 10 × 10        =  1,000
        10⁴ = 10 × 10 × 10 × 10   = 10,000
    Answer: 10⁴ = 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 10,000 (ten thousand).
    Teacher explains: In 10⁴ the big number 10 is the BASE and the small raised
    number 4 is the EXPONENT (also called the power or index). The exponent says
    how many 10s are multiplied together. Multiply step by step: 10 × 10 = 100,
    × 10 = 1,000, × 10 = 10,000. Each × 10 adds one zero, so 10⁴ is 1 followed
    by 4 zeros. Say "ten to the power four". A common mistake is 10⁴ = 40 (10 ×
    4). Now you try: write 10⁶ as a multiplication and find its value. (10 × 10
    × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 = 1,000,000)

D2. Explain why 10⁰ = 1.
        10³ = 1,000
        10² =   100   (÷ 10)
        10¹ =    10   (÷ 10)
        10⁰ =     1   (÷ 10)
    Answer: Going down the list of powers of 10, each one is the one above ÷ 10:
    10³ = 1,000, 10² = 100, 10¹ = 10, so 10⁰ = 10 ÷ 10 = 1.
    Teacher explains: Write the powers in a column. Each step DOWN the exponent
    goes down by 1 and the value is divided by 10: 1,000 ÷ 10 = 100, 100 ÷ 10 =
    10, 10 ÷ 10 = 1. So the pattern forces 10⁰ = 1, which also fits the rule "1
    followed by 0 zeros". It is NOT 0, and it is not 10. The same pattern works
    for any base (except 0): 2⁰ = 1, 5⁰ = 1. Now you try: what is 7⁰? (1)

D3. Write one lakh, one million, one crore and one billion as powers of 10.
        10⁰ =             1   (one)
        10¹ =            10   (ten)
        10² =           100   (hundred)
        10³ =         1,000   (thousand)
        10⁴ =        10,000   (ten thousand)
        10⁵ =       100,000   (hundred thousand = 1 lakh)
        10⁶ =     1,000,000   (million = 10 lakh)
        10⁷ =    10,000,000   (ten million = 1 crore)
        10⁸ =   100,000,000   (hundred million = 10 crore)
        10⁹ = 1,000,000,000   (billion = 1 arab (100 crore))
    Answer: 1 lakh = 10⁵, 1 million = 10⁶, 1 crore = 10⁷, 1 billion = 10⁹.
    Teacher explains: Count the zeros: 1 lakh = 1,00,000 has 5 zeros, so it is
    10⁵. One million = 1,000,000 has 6 zeros: 10⁶. One crore = 1,00,00,000 has 7
    zeros: 10⁷. One billion = 1,000,000,000 has 9 zeros: 10⁹. Powers of 10 are
    the same in both systems; only the NAMES differ, as the table shows. Writing
    big numbers as powers saves space and avoids miscounting zeros. Now you try:
    write ten lakh and ten crore as powers of 10. (10⁶ and 10⁸)

D4. A single bacterium splits into 2 every hour. How many bacteria are there
    after 5 hours? Write the answer using an exponent.
    Answer: 2⁵ = 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 32 bacteria.
    Teacher explains: After 1 hour there are 2, after 2 hours 2 × 2 = 4, after 3
    hours 8, after 4 hours 16, after 5 hours 32. Five doublings means five
    factors of 2: 2⁵. Base 2, exponent 5. Make a table: hours 1 2 3 4 5,
    bacteria 2 4 8 16 32. A common mistake is 2 × 5 = 10; the exponent is how
    many TIMES we multiply, not what we multiply BY. Now you try: after 10
    hours? (2¹⁰ = 1,024)

D5. Rahul says 3⁴ = 12. Explain his mistake and find the correct value.
    Answer: 3⁴ = 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 = 81, not 3 × 4 = 12.
    Teacher explains: Rahul multiplied the base by the exponent. The exponent
    means repeated MULTIPLICATION, just as multiplication means repeated
    addition: 3 × 4 = 3 + 3 + 3 + 3 = 12, but 3⁴ = 3 × 3 × 3 × 3. Work in pairs:
    3 × 3 = 9, 9 × 3 = 27, 27 × 3 = 81. Check the size: powers grow very fast.
    Now you try: 4³ and 4 × 3. (64 and 12)

D6. A big cube is built from small cubes, 5 along each edge. How many small
    cubes are used? What about a square of tiles with 5 along each side?
    Answer: Cube: 5³ = 5 × 5 × 5 = 125 small cubes. Square: 5² = 5 × 5 = 25
    tiles.
    Teacher explains: A square with side 5 has 5 rows of 5, so 5 × 5 = 5² tiles:
    that is why we say "5 SQUARED". A cube has 5 layers, each a 5 by 5 square,
    so 5 × 5 × 5 = 5³: that is why we say "5 CUBED". The first squares are 1, 4,
    9, 16, 25, 36, 49, 64, 81, 100 and the first cubes are 1, 8, 27, 64, 125,
    216, 343, 512, 729, 1,000. Notice 64 is both 8² and 4³. Now you try: 6³.
    (216)

D7. Write 60,408 in expanded form using powers of 10.
    Answer: 60,408 = 6 × 10⁴ + 4 × 10² + 8 × 10⁰.
    Teacher explains: First write the place values: 6 ten thousands, 0
    thousands, 4 hundreds, 0 tens, 8 ones. Then write each place as a power of
    10: ten thousands = 10⁴, hundreds = 10², ones = 10⁰. Leave out the zero
    digits (or write 0 × 10³ if you want to show every place). Check: 60,000 +
    400 + 8 = 60,408. The exponent is always one less than the number of digits
    counted from that digit to the right end. Now you try: 7,003,050. (7 × 10⁶ +
    3 × 10³ + 5 × 10¹)

D8. Work out 10³ × 10⁴ and explain the short cut.
    Answer: 10³ × 10⁴ = 1,000 × 10,000 = 10,000,000 = 10⁷.
    Teacher explains: Write out the factors: 10³ × 10⁴ = (10 × 10 × 10) × (10 ×
    10 × 10 × 10). That is 3 + 4 = 7 tens multiplied together, so 10⁷. With
    zeros: 1,000 has 3 zeros and 10,000 has 4, and the product has 3 + 4 = 7
    zeros. Short cut: when we multiply powers of the SAME base, ADD the
    exponents. Common mistake: multiplying the exponents (10¹²). This works only
    with the same base: 10³ × 2⁴ cannot be done this way. Now you try: 10² × 10⁵
    × 10. (10⁸)

D9. ₹10 crore (10⁸ rupees) is shared equally among 1,000 (10³) villages. How
    much does each village get? Use powers of 10.
    Answer: 10⁸ ÷ 10³ = 10⁵ = ₹1,00,000 = ₹1 lakh each.
    Teacher explains: Dividing by 10 removes one zero, so dividing by 10³
    removes three zeros: 100,000,000 ÷ 1,000 = 100,000. In exponents: 8 tens on
    top, 3 tens cancel with the 3 tens underneath, leaving 8 − 3 = 5 tens. Short
    cut: when we divide powers of the same base, SUBTRACT the exponents. Check:
    10⁵ × 10³ = 10⁸. Also note 10⁵ ÷ 10⁵ = 10⁰ = 1, which agrees with 10⁰ = 1.
    Now you try: 10⁹ ÷ 10⁶. (10³ = 1,000)

D10. A factory makes 36 lakh bottles in a year. Write this as 36 × a power of 10
     and as an ordinary number.
     Answer: 36 lakh = 36 × 10⁵ = 3,600,000 (36,00,000).
     Teacher explains: 1 lakh = 10⁵, so 36 lakh = 36 × 10⁵. To multiply by 10⁵
     move every digit 5 places to the left and fill the empty places with 5
     zeros: 36 becomes 3,600,000. Do not just add 5 zeros to a DECIMAL: 3.6 ×
     10⁶ means move the digits 6 places: 3,600,000 too. Check the size: 36 lakh
     is between 10 lakh (1 million) and 1 crore (10 million), and so is
     3,600,000. Now you try: 425 × 10⁴. (4,250,000)

D11. The Sun is about 150,000,000 km from the Earth, and about 1,430,000,000
     people live in India. Write both in standard form.
     Answer: 150,000,000 = 1.5 × 10⁸; 1,430,000,000 = 1.43 × 10⁹.
     Teacher explains: Standard form is (a number from 1 up to but not including
     10) × (a power of 10). Put the decimal point after the first digit: 1.5.
     Count how many places the point must move to the RIGHT to get back to
     150,000,000: 8 places, so 1.5 × 10⁸. For 1,430,000,000: 1.43, and the point
     moves 9 places: 1.43 × 10⁹. Common mistakes: 15 × 10⁷ (true, but 15 is not
     less than 10, so it is not standard form) and counting the zeros only (7
     zeros in 150,000,000, but 8 places). Now you try: 4,500,000 in standard
     form. (4.5 × 10⁶)

D12. Which is bigger: 2¹⁰ or 10³? 3⁵ or 5³? 2⁴ or 4²?
     Answer: 2¹⁰ = 1,024 > 10³ = 1,000; 3⁵ = 243 > 5³ = 125; 2⁴ = 16 and 4² =
     16, so 2⁴ = 4².
     Teacher explains: Work out each power: 2¹⁰ = 2 × 2 × ... (10 twos) = 1,024;
     10³ = 1,000. Computer memory uses 2¹⁰ = 1,024 as a "kilo", because it is so
     close to 1,000. 3⁵ = 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 = 243 and 5³ = 125. Swapping the
     base and the exponent usually changes the value; 2⁴ and 4² are a special
     pair that are equal. Now you try: 2⁵ or 5²? (2⁵ = 32 > 5² = 25)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write as a power of 10.
    a) 100                      b) 1,000
    c) 10                       d) 1
    e) 100,000                  f) 10,000
    g) 1,000,000                h) 10,000,000
    i) 1,000,000,000            j) 100,000,000
    k) 10 × 10 × 10             l) 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10
    m) one lakh                 n) one crore
    o) one million              p) one billion
    q) ten lakh                 r) ten crore
    s) ten thousand             t) one arab
    u) 10,000,000,000           v) 1,000,000,000,000
    Answers: a) 10²   b) 10³   c) 10¹   d) 10⁰   e) 10⁵   f) 10⁴   g) 10⁶
             h) 10⁷   i) 10⁹   j) 10⁸   k) 10³   l) 10⁵   m) 10⁵   n) 10⁷
             o) 10⁶   p) 10⁹   q) 10⁶   r) 10⁸   s) 10⁴   t) 10⁹   u) 10¹⁰
             v) 10¹²

M2. Write the value with commas, and how many zeros it has.
    a) 10³    b) 10⁰    c) 10⁷    d) 10¹    e) 10⁵
    f) 10⁹    g) 10²    h) 10⁶    i) 10⁴    j) 10⁸
    k) 10¹⁰   l) 10¹²
    Answers: a) 1,000 (3)   b) 1 (0)   c) 10,000,000 (7)   d) 10 (1)
             e) 100,000 (5)   f) 1,000,000,000 (9)   g) 100 (2)
             h) 1,000,000 (6)   i) 10,000 (4)   j) 100,000,000 (8)
             k) 10,000,000,000 (10)   l) 1,000,000,000,000 (12)

M3. Find the value (powers of 2 and 3).
    a) 2¹    b) 2²    c) 2³    d) 2⁴    e) 2⁵
    f) 2⁶    g) 2⁷    h) 2⁸    i) 2⁹    j) 2¹⁰
    k) 3¹    l) 3²    m) 3³    n) 3⁴    o) 3⁵
    p) 3⁶    q) 2⁰    r) 3⁰
    Answers: a) 2   b) 4   c) 8   d) 16   e) 32   f) 64   g) 128   h) 256
             i) 512   j) 1,024   k) 3   l) 9   m) 27   n) 81   o) 243   p) 729
             q) 1   r) 1

M4. Find the value.
    a) 4²     b) 4³     c) 4⁴     d) 5²     e) 5³
    f) 5⁴     g) 6²     h) 6³     i) 7²     j) 7³
    k) 8²     l) 8³     m) 9²     n) 9³     o) 11²
    p) 12²    q) 1⁷     r) 0⁴     s) 20²    t) 20³
    u) 15²    v) 25²    w) 100³   x) 1¹⁰⁰
    Answers: a) 16   b) 64   c) 256   d) 25   e) 125   f) 625   g) 36   h) 216
             i) 49   j) 343   k) 64   l) 512   m) 81   n) 729   o) 121   p) 144
             q) 1   r) 0   s) 400   t) 8,000   u) 225   v) 625   w) 1,000,000
             x) 1

M5. Write in exponent form. For k) onwards, write the number as a power of the
    given base.
    a) 2 × 2 × 2 × 2                      b) 7 × 7 × 7
    c) 5 × 5                              d) 3 × 3 × 3 × 3 × 3
    e) 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10   f) 9
    g) 11 × 11 × 11                       h) 6 × 6 × 6 × 6
    i) 4 × 4 × 4 × 4 × 4                  j) 8 × 8
    k) 81 = 3ⁿ                            l) 64 = 2ⁿ
    m) 125 = 5ⁿ                           n) 1,000 = 10ⁿ
    o) 128 = 2ⁿ                           p) 243 = 3ⁿ
    q) 49 = 7ⁿ                            r) 216 = 6ⁿ
    s) 1,024 = 2ⁿ                         t) 100,000 = 10ⁿ
    u) 625 = 5ⁿ                           v) 343 = 7ⁿ
    w) 256 = 4ⁿ                           x) 729 = 9ⁿ
    Answers: a) 2⁴   b) 7³   c) 5²   d) 3⁵   e) 10⁷   f) 9¹   g) 11³   h) 6⁴
             i) 4⁵   j) 8²   k) 3⁴   l) 2⁶   m) 5³   n) 10³   o) 2⁷   p) 3⁵
             q) 7²   r) 6³   s) 2¹⁰   t) 10⁵   u) 5⁴   v) 7³   w) 4⁴   x) 9³

M6. Write <, > or = between the two powers.
    a) 2³ __ 3²      b) 2⁴ __ 4²      c) 2⁵ __ 5²      d) 3⁴ __ 4³
    e) 10² __ 2¹⁰    f) 1¹⁰ __ 10¹    g) 2⁶ __ 6²      h) 5³ __ 3⁵
    i) 10³ __ 2¹⁰    j) 4³ __ 8²      k) 2⁸ __ 4⁴      l) 9² __ 3⁴
    m) 7² __ 2⁷      n) 10⁰ __ 0¹⁰    o) 6³ __ 3⁶      p) 5⁴ __ 4⁵
    q) 2⁹ __ 8³      r) 12² __ 2⁷     s) 10⁴ __ 100²   t) 3³ __ 5²
    Answers: a) 2³ < 3² (8, 9)                b) 2⁴ = 4² (16, 16)
             c) 2⁵ > 5² (32, 25)              d) 3⁴ > 4³ (81, 64)
             e) 10² < 2¹⁰ (100, 1,024)        f) 1¹⁰ < 10¹ (1, 10)
             g) 2⁶ > 6² (64, 36)              h) 5³ < 3⁵ (125, 243)
             i) 10³ < 2¹⁰ (1,000, 1,024)      j) 4³ = 8² (64, 64)
             k) 2⁸ = 4⁴ (256, 256)            l) 9² = 3⁴ (81, 81)
             m) 7² < 2⁷ (49, 128)             n) 10⁰ > 0¹⁰ (1, 0)
             o) 6³ < 3⁶ (216, 729)            p) 5⁴ < 4⁵ (625, 1,024)
             q) 2⁹ = 8³ (512, 512)            r) 12² > 2⁷ (144, 128)
             s) 10⁴ = 100² (10,000, 10,000)   t) 3³ > 5² (27, 25)

M7. Write a) to l) in expanded form using powers of 10. Write m) to v) as
    ordinary numbers with commas.
    a) 4,305
    b) 60,408
    c) 7,003,050
    d) 5,555
    e) 90,009
    f) 123,456
    g) 2,000,000
    h) 30,303,030
    i) 804
    j) 100,001
    k) 7,654,321
    l) 450,000,000
    m) 5 × 10⁴ + 3 × 10² + 7 × 10⁰
    n) 4 × 10⁶ + 2 × 10⁴ + 1 × 10²
    o) 9 × 10⁸ + 9 × 10⁰
    p) 1 × 10³ + 2 × 10² + 3 × 10¹ + 4 × 10⁰
    q) 8 × 10⁴ + 8 × 10¹
    r) 6 × 10⁹
    s) 7 × 10⁵ + 7 × 10¹
    t) 3 × 10⁶ + 4 × 10⁴ + 5 × 10² + 6 × 10⁰
    u) 1 × 10⁷ + 1 × 10⁰
    v) 9 × 10² + 9 × 10¹ + 9 × 10⁰
    Answers:
      a) 4 × 10³ + 3 × 10² + 5 × 10⁰
      b) 6 × 10⁴ + 4 × 10² + 8 × 10⁰

      d) 5 × 10³ + 5 × 10² + 5 × 10¹ + 5 × 10⁰
      e) 9 × 10⁴ + 9 × 10⁰
      f) 1 × 10⁵ + 2 × 10⁴ + 3 × 10³ + 4 × 10² + 5 × 10¹ + 6 × 10⁰
      g) 2 × 10⁶
      h) 3 × 10⁷ + 3 × 10⁵ + 3 × 10³ + 3 × 10¹
      i) 8 × 10² + 4 × 10⁰
      j) 1 × 10⁵ + 1 × 10⁰
      k) 7 × 10⁶ + 6 × 10⁵ + 5 × 10⁴ + 4 × 10³ + 3 × 10² + 2 × 10¹ + 1 × 10⁰
      l) 4 × 10⁸ + 5 × 10⁷
      m) 50,307
      n) 4,020,100
      o) 900,000,009
      p) 1,234
      q) 80,080
      r) 6,000,000,000
      s) 700,070
      t) 3,040,506
      u) 10,000,001
      v) 999

M8. Multiply. Give the answer as a power of 10.
    a) 10³ × 10⁴           b) 10² × 10⁵           c) 10 × 10
    d) 10⁴ × 10⁴           e) 10⁰ × 10⁵           f) 10⁶ × 10³
    g) 10 × 10⁸            h) 10² × 10² × 10²     i) 10⁵ × 10⁰
    j) 10³ × 10³ × 10³     k) 10 × 10² × 10³      l) 10⁷ × 10²
    m) 10⁹ × 10            n) 10⁴ × 10⁵           o) 10² × 10⁷
    p) 10 × 10 × 10 × 10   q) 10⁵ × 10⁵           r) 10⁰ × 10⁰
    s) 10³ × 10⁶           t) 10² × 10² × 10⁵
    Answers: a) 10⁷   b) 10⁷   c) 10²   d) 10⁸   e) 10⁵   f) 10⁹   g) 10⁹
             h) 10⁶   i) 10⁵   j) 10⁹   k) 10⁶   l) 10⁹   m) 10¹⁰   n) 10⁹
             o) 10⁹   p) 10⁴   q) 10¹⁰   r) 10⁰   s) 10⁹   t) 10⁹

M9. Divide. Give the answer as a power of 10 and as a number.
    a) 10⁸ ÷ 10³    b) 10⁶ ÷ 10²    c) 10⁹ ÷ 10⁶    d) 10⁵ ÷ 10⁵
    e) 10⁷ ÷ 10     f) 10⁴ ÷ 10⁴    g) 10¹⁰ ÷ 10³   h) 10⁶ ÷ 10⁰
    i) 10⁹ ÷ 10     j) 10⁷ ÷ 10⁵    k) 10³ ÷ 10²    l) 10⁸ ÷ 10⁷
    m) 10¹² ÷ 10⁶   n) 10⁵ ÷ 10     o) 10⁹ ÷ 10⁹    p) 10⁶ ÷ 10³
    q) 10² ÷ 10     r) 10¹⁰ ÷ 10⁸   s) 10⁴ ÷ 10⁰    t) 10⁸ ÷ 10⁴
    Answers: a) 10⁵ = 100,000       b) 10⁴ = 10,000        c) 10³ = 1,000
             d) 10⁰ = 1             e) 10⁶ = 1,000,000     f) 10⁰ = 1
             g) 10⁷ = 10,000,000    h) 10⁶ = 1,000,000     i) 10⁸ = 100,000,000
             j) 10² = 100           k) 10¹ = 10            l) 10¹ = 10
             m) 10⁶ = 1,000,000     n) 10⁴ = 10,000        o) 10⁰ = 1
             p) 10³ = 1,000         q) 10¹ = 10            r) 10² = 100
             s) 10⁴ = 10,000        t) 10⁴ = 10,000

M10. Work out. Give the answer with commas.
     a) 36 × 10⁵              b) 425 × 10⁴             c) 7 × 10⁶
     d) 12 × 10⁷              e) 3 × 10⁹               f) 81 × 10³
     g) 405 × 10²             h) 1 × 10⁸               i) 250 × 10⁶
     j) 9 × 10⁰               k) 60 × 10⁵              l) 1,001 × 10³
     m) 4,500,000 ÷ 10³       n) 360,000 ÷ 10⁴         o) 7,000,000 ÷ 10⁶

M11. Write a) to p) in standard form. Write q) to x) as ordinary numbers.
     a) 4,500,000        b) 150,000,000      c) 1,430,000,000

M12. Find n.
     a) 10ⁿ = 1,000,000        b) 10ⁿ = 10               c) 10ⁿ = 1
     d) 10ⁿ = 1,00,00,000      e) 2ⁿ = 128               f) 2ⁿ = 1,024
     g) 3ⁿ = 729               h) 5ⁿ = 625               i) 10³ × 10ⁿ = 10⁹
     j) 10ⁿ × 10² = 10¹⁰       k) 10⁸ ÷ 10ⁿ = 10⁵        l) 10ⁿ ÷ 10⁴ = 10⁴
     m) 10⁶ ÷ 10ⁿ = 1          n) 4ⁿ = 64                o) 7ⁿ = 2,401

              s) 9   t) 1

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: build powers with a calculator or by hand one step at a time (10,
  100, 1,000 ... and 2, 4, 8, 16 ...), keep a wall chart of 10⁰ to 10⁹ with the
  lakh/crore and million/billion names, and always write the repeated
  multiplication before the value. Harder: the rules 10ᵃ × 10ᵇ = 10ᵃ⁺ᵇ with
  letters, powers of 2 in computers (bytes, kilobytes = 2¹⁰), the chessboard
  rice story (1 + 2 + 4 + ... doubling for 64 squares), and very small numbers
  such as 10⁻¹ = 0.1 as a preview.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.3 — MULTIPLYING AND DIVIDING DECIMALS BY 10, 100, 1000
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Multiplying by 10 makes every digit 10 times bigger, so every digit moves
    ONE place to the LEFT (tenths become ones, ones become tens): 4.75 × 10 =
    47.5. The decimal point stays still; the digits move. (It LOOKS as if the
    point moves one place right.)
  - × 100 moves the digits TWO places left and × 1000 moves them THREE places
    left. Fill empty places before the point with zeros: 0.25 × 1000 = 250;
    0.004 × 1000 = 4; 0.06 × 10 = 0.6.
  - Dividing by 10, 100, 1000 moves the digits 1, 2, 3 places to the RIGHT. Fill
    empty places after the point with zeros: 3.5 ÷ 100 = 0.035; 45 ÷ 1000 =
    0.045.
  - Never just "add a zero" to a decimal: 2.5 × 10 is 25, not 2.50 (2.50 is the
    same as 2.5). Zeros at the end of a decimal part can be dropped: 4.50 = 4.5.
  - Find a missing multiplier or divisor by counting how many places the digits
    moved: 0.36 × 100 = 36 (2 places left), 7 ÷ 1000 = 0.007 (3 places right).
  - × 10 and ÷ 10 undo each other; × 100 then ÷ 1000 is the same as ÷ 10.
  - Convert units by × or ÷ a power of 10: 10 mm = 1 cm, 100 cm = 1 m, 1000 m =
    1 km, 1000 g = 1 kg, 1000 mL = 1 L, 100 paise = ₹1. Big unit to small unit:
    MULTIPLY (more of them). Small unit to big unit: DIVIDE.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. One pen costs ₹4.75. How much do 10 pens cost?
                       T   O   . t   h
        4.75               4   . 7   5
        4.75 × 10      4   7   . 5
    Answer: ₹47.50. 4.75 × 10 = 47.5.
    Teacher explains: Put 4.75 in a place value chart: 4 ones, 7 tenths, 5
    hundredths. Multiplying by 10 makes each digit worth 10 times as much: 4
    ones become 4 tens, 7 tenths become 7 ones, 5 hundredths become 5 tenths. So
    every digit moves one place to the LEFT and the answer is 47.5, which we
    write as ₹47.50 for money. The point itself never moves in the chart, but it
    looks as if it jumped one place right. Estimate to check: ₹5 × 10 = ₹50. Now
    you try: 10 bananas at ₹3.60 each. (₹36)

D2. A school buys 100 notebooks at ₹12.35 each. What is the total cost? Also
    work out 0.06 × 100.
                       Th  H   T   O   . t   h
        12.35                  1   2   . 3   5
        12.35 × 100    1   2   3   5   .
    Answer: ₹1,235. 12.35 × 100 = 1,235. And 0.06 × 100 = 6.
    Teacher explains: × 100 = × 10 × 10, so every digit moves TWO places left.
    12.35: the 1 ten becomes 1 thousand, the 2 ones become 2 hundreds, the 3
    tenths become 3 tens, the 5 hundredths become 5 ones: 1,235. For 0.06, the 6
    hundredths move two places to become 6 ones: 6. Zeros in front of a whole
    number are dropped (06 = 6). Estimate: ₹12 × 100 = ₹1,200, close. Now you
    try: 3.07 × 100 and 0.5 × 100. (307 and 50)

D3. One seed weighs 0.004 kg. How much do 1,000 seeds weigh? And 1,000 packets
    of tea of 0.25 kg each?
    Answer: 0.004 × 1000 = 4 kg. 0.25 × 1000 = 250 kg.
    Teacher explains: × 1000 moves every digit THREE places left. In 0.004 the 4
    is in the thousandths place; three places left is the ones place: 4. In
    0.25, the 2 tenths become 2 hundreds and the 5 hundredths become 5 tens, and
    the empty ones place is filled with 0: 250. A useful picture: write the
    number, then hop the decimal point three places to the right, filling any
    gaps with zeros: 0.250 → 250. Now you try: 0.035 × 1000 and 1.2 × 1000. (35
    and 1,200)

D4. Meera says 2.5 × 10 = 2.50 because "to multiply by 10 you put a zero on the
    end". Is she right?
    Answer: No. 2.5 × 10 = 25. 2.50 is the same as 2.5, so adding the zero did
    not change the number at all.
    Teacher explains: The "add a zero" trick works only for WHOLE numbers (36 ×
    10 = 360). For a decimal, a zero at the end of the decimal part does not
    change the value: 2.50 means 2 ones, 5 tenths and 0 hundredths, the same as
    2.5. The correct rule is: the digits move one place left. 2 ones become 2
    tens, 5 tenths become 5 ones: 25. Check with money: 10 coins of ₹2.50 make
    ₹25, not ₹2.50. Now you try: 0.8 × 10 and 6.04 × 10. (8 and 60.4)

D5. ₹35.60 is shared equally among 10 children. How much does each child get?
                       T   O   . t   h
        35.6           3   5   . 6
        35.6 ÷ 10          3   . 5   6
    Answer: ₹3.56. 35.6 ÷ 10 = 3.56.
    Teacher explains: Dividing by 10 makes every digit 10 times SMALLER, so each
    digit moves one place to the RIGHT: 3 tens become 3 ones, 5 ones become 5
    tenths, 6 tenths become 6 hundredths. 35.6 becomes 3.56. Check by
    multiplying back: 3.56 × 10 = 35.6. Estimate: ₹36 ÷ 10 is about ₹3.60. Now
    you try: 7 ÷ 10 and 480.5 ÷ 10. (0.7 and 48.05)

D6. A 3.5 m ribbon is cut into 100 equal pieces. How long is each piece, in
    metres and in centimetres?
                       O   . t   h   th
        3.5            3   . 5
        3.5 ÷ 100      0   . 0   3   5
    Answer: 3.5 ÷ 100 = 0.035 m = 3.5 cm.
    Teacher explains: ÷ 100 moves every digit TWO places right. The 3 ones
    become 3 hundredths and the 5 tenths become 5 thousandths. The ones and
    tenths places are now empty, so we must write zeros there: 0.035. The zeros
    hold the digits in their places; without them 0.35 would be ten times too
    big. Check: 0.035 × 100 = 3.5. In centimetres: 3.5 m = 350 cm, and 350 ÷ 100
    = 3.5 cm, the same length. Now you try: 6 ÷ 100 and 0.4 ÷ 100. (0.06 and
    0.004)

D7. A pile of 1,000 sheets of paper is 45 mm high. How thick is one sheet?
    Answer: 45 ÷ 1000 = 0.045 mm.
    Teacher explains: ÷ 1000 moves the digits THREE places right. 45 is 4 tens
    and 5 ones; three places right the 4 is in the hundredths place and the 5 in
    the thousandths place: 0.045. Another way to see it: imagine the point after
    45 (45.) and hop it three places LEFT, putting a 0 in front when you run out
    of digits: 45. → 4.5 → 0.45 → 0.045. Check: 0.045 × 1000 = 45. Now you try:
    7 ÷ 1000 and 1,250 ÷ 1000. (0.007 and 1.25)

D8. Fill in the blanks: 0.36 × __ = 36 and 4.2 ÷ __ = 0.042.
    Answer: 0.36 × 100 = 36; 4.2 ÷ 100 = 0.042.
    Teacher explains: Compare the digits. In 0.36 the 6 is in the hundredths
    place; in 36 it is in the ones place: it moved TWO places left, so we
    multiplied by 100. In 4.2 the 4 is in the ones place; in 0.042 it is in the
    hundredths place: two places right, so we divided by 100. Count the places
    moved: 1 place = 10, 2 places = 100, 3 places = 1000. Check each answer by
    doing the calculation. Now you try: 5.07 × __ = 5,070 and 81 ÷ __ = 8.1.
    (1000 and 10)

D9. A road is 2.45 km long. How many metres is that? How many centimetres? And
    how many metres is a 375 mm pencil box?
        km  --×1000-->  m  --×100-->  cm  --×10-->  mm
        km  <--÷1000--  m  <--÷100--  cm  <--÷10--  mm
    Answer: 2.45 km = 2,450 m = 245,000 cm. 375 mm = 37.5 cm = 0.375 m.
    Teacher explains: Big unit to small unit: there will be MORE of the small
    units, so multiply. 1 km = 1000 m, so 2.45 × 1000 = 2,450 m. 1 m = 100 cm,
    so 2,450 × 100 = 245,000 cm. Small unit to big unit: FEWER big units, so
    divide. 10 mm = 1 cm, so 375 ÷ 10 = 37.5 cm; 100 cm = 1 m, so 37.5 ÷ 100 =
    0.375 m (or 375 ÷ 1000 = 0.375 m, since 1000 mm = 1 m). Ask "should the
    number get bigger or smaller?" before choosing × or ÷. Now you try: 0.8 km
    in m and 65 cm in m. (800 m and 0.65 m)

D10. A recipe needs 0.75 kg of flour, 60 g of butter and 1,250 mL of milk. Write
     the flour in grams, the butter in kilograms and the milk in litres.
     Answer: 0.75 kg = 750 g; 60 g = 0.06 kg; 1,250 mL = 1.25 L.
     Teacher explains: 1 kg = 1000 g, so kilograms to grams is × 1000: 0.75 ×
     1000 = 750. Grams to kilograms is ÷ 1000: 60 ÷ 1000 = 0.06 (the 6 tens
     become 6 hundredths; keep the 0 in the tenths place). Millilitres to
     litres: ÷ 1000, since 1 L = 1000 mL: 1,250 ÷ 1000 = 1.25. Common mistake:
     60 g = 0.6 kg, which is ten times too big; 0.6 kg would be 600 g. Now you
     try: 2.5 L in mL and 5 g in kg. (2,500 mL and 0.005 kg)

D11. A shopkeeper has 845 paise in coins and a customer pays ₹12.05. Write 845
     paise in rupees and ₹12.05 in paise. How much is 1,000 toffees at ₹0.50
     each?
     Answer: 845 paise = ₹8.45; ₹12.05 = 1,205 paise; 1,000 × ₹0.50 = ₹500.
     Teacher explains: 100 paise = ₹1, so paise to rupees is ÷ 100: 845 ÷ 100 =
     8.45. Rupees to paise is × 100: 12.05 × 100 = 1,205. Look at the 0 in
     ₹12.05: it means 0 tenths of a rupee (no 10-paise coins); dropping it would
     give ₹12.5 = 1,250 paise, which is wrong. For the toffees, 0.50 × 1000
     moves the digits three places left: 500. Now you try: 7 paise in rupees and
     ₹0.3 in paise. (₹0.07 and 30 paise)

D12. Work out 0.06 × 10 × 100 and 7.2 ÷ 10 × 1000. Is × 100 then ÷ 1000 the same
     as ÷ 10?
     Answer: 0.06 × 10 × 100 = 0.6 × 100 = 60. 7.2 ÷ 10 × 1000 = 0.72 × 1000 =
     720. Yes: × 100 then ÷ 1000 moves the digits 2 places left and then 3
     places right, 1 place right in all, which is ÷ 10.
     Teacher explains: Work from left to right, one step at a time, and write
     each result. 0.06 × 10 = 0.6, then 0.6 × 100 = 60. Or count moves: 1 place
     left + 2 places left = 3 places left, the same as × 1000: 0.06 × 1000 = 60.
     For 7.2 ÷ 10 × 1000: 1 place right then 3 places left is 2 places left in
     total, the same as × 100: 7.2 × 100 = 720. Test the last claim with 45: 45
     × 100 = 4,500 and 4,500 ÷ 1000 = 4.5 = 45 ÷ 10. Now you try: 3.8 × 1000 ÷
     100. (38)

      a) 45; 450; 4,500; 0.45; 0.045; 0.0045

      c) 120.8; 1,208; 12,080; 1.208; 0.1208; 0.01208
      d) 70; 700; 7,000; 0.7; 0.07; 0.007
      e) 0.05; 0.5; 5; 0.0005; 0.00005; 0.000005
      f) 3,052; 30,520; 305,200; 30.52; 3.052; 0.3052
      g) 9; 90; 900; 0.09; 0.009; 0.0009
      h) 22.5; 225; 2,250; 0.225; 0.0225; 0.00225
      i) 600; 6,000; 60,000; 6; 0.6; 0.06
      j) 10.01; 100.1; 1,001; 0.1001; 0.01001; 0.001001
      k) 0.72; 7.2; 72; 0.0072; 0.00072; 0.000072
      l) 486; 4,860; 48,600; 4.86; 0.486; 0.0486

M8. Find the missing number.
    a) 0.36 × __ = 36       b) 4.2 ÷ __ = 0.042     c) 5.07 × __ = 5,070
    d) 81 ÷ __ = 8.1        e) 0.004 × __ = 4       f) 3.5 ÷ __ = 0.0035

M12. Work from left to right.

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use a place value chart with the decimal point drawn firmly on it
  and digit cards that slide left for × and right for ÷; say "each digit becomes
  ten times bigger"; check every answer with an estimate and with money (₹ and
  paise). Harder: × and ÷ by 10,000 and by 0.1 or 0.01 (a preview: × 0.1 is the
  same as ÷ 10), chains of conversions (km → cm), and scale problems such as a
  1:100 model.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.4 — HCF/GCD AND LCM (LISTING, PRIME FACTORISATION, EUCLID'S METHOD)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - The HCF (highest common factor, also called the GCD, greatest common
    divisor) of two numbers is the BIGGEST number that divides both exactly.
    HCF(18, 24) = 6.
  - The LCM (lowest common multiple) is the SMALLEST number (not 0) that both
    numbers divide into exactly. LCM(12, 18) = 36.
  - Method 1, LISTING: list the factors (for HCF) or the multiples (for LCM) of
    each number and pick the biggest common factor or the smallest common
    multiple.
  - Method 2, PRIME FACTORISATION: break each number into primes with a factor
    tree or the ladder (repeated division), e.g. 360 = 2³ × 3² × 5. HCF =
    product of the COMMON primes, each to its SMALLER power; LCM = product of
    ALL the primes, each to its BIGGER power.
  - Method 3, EUCLID'S DIVISION ALGORITHM: divide the bigger number by the
    smaller, then divide the smaller number by the remainder, and so on; when
    the remainder is 0, the last divisor is the HCF. 1,071 = 2 × 462 + 147, 462
    = 3 × 147 + 21, 147 = 7 × 21 + 0, so HCF = 21.
  - For TWO numbers: HCF × LCM = the product of the numbers. 6 × 36 = 12 × 18 =
    216. The HCF always divides the LCM.
  - CO-PRIME numbers have HCF 1 (8 and 15); their LCM is their product. Co-prime
    numbers need not be prime themselves.
  - In problems: HCF answers "the biggest equal pieces / groups / tiles"; LCM
    answers "when will they happen together again" or "the smallest amount that
    fits both".

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Asha has 18 laddoos and 24 barfis. She wants to pack them into identical
    boxes, each box with the same mixture, and none left over. What is the
    greatest number of boxes she can make? What goes in each?
    Answer: 6 boxes, each with 3 laddoos and 4 barfis. HCF(18, 24) = 6.
    Teacher explains: The number of boxes must divide 18 AND 24, so it is a
    COMMON FACTOR. List the factors in pairs: 18: 1, 2, 3, 6, 9, 18. 24: 1, 2,
    3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24. The common factors are 1, 2, 3, 6; the highest is 6.
    Then 18 ÷ 6 = 3 laddoos and 24 ÷ 6 = 4 barfis in each box. Listing is good
    for small numbers; list in factor pairs so none is missed. Now you try: 20
    pens and 30 pencils. (HCF 10: 10 packs of 2 pens and 3 pencils)

D2. From the bus stand, a red bus leaves every 12 minutes and a blue bus every
    18 minutes. Both leave together at 8:00 a.m. When do they next leave
    together?
    Answer: At 8:36 a.m. LCM(12, 18) = 36 minutes.
    Teacher explains: Red buses leave after 12, 24, 36, 48, 60, 72 ... minutes
    (multiples of 12). Blue buses leave after 18, 36, 54, 72 ... minutes
    (multiples of 18). The common multiples are 36, 72, ...; the lowest is 36.
    So they meet again 36 minutes later, at 8:36 a.m., and then every 36
    minutes. Tip: list the multiples of the BIGGER number and stop at the first
    one the smaller number divides (18 no, 36 yes). Now you try: every 8 and
    every 10 minutes. (40 minutes)

D3. Write 360 as a product of prime factors using a factor tree.
              360
             /   \
           (2)   180
                /   \
              (2)    90
                    /  \
                  (2)   45
                       /  \
                     (3)   15
                          /  \
                        (3)  (5)
    Answer: 360 = 2 × 2 × 2 × 3 × 3 × 5 = 2³ × 3² × 5.
    Teacher explains: Split 360 into any two factors, then keep splitting until
    every branch ends in a prime (circle the primes). Here we took 2 each time
    while the number was even, then 3s, then 5. A different first split (like
    360 = 10 × 36) gives a different-looking tree but ALWAYS the same primes:
    that is why prime factorisation is so useful. Check by multiplying: 8 × 9 ×
    5 = 360. Write repeated primes with exponents (Topic 5.2). Common mistake:
    stopping at 9 or 15, which are not prime. Now you try: 84. (2² × 3 × 7)

D4. Find the HCF and LCM of 84 and 120 with the ladder (common division) method.
        2 | 84   120
          |-----------
        2 | 42    60
          |-----------
        3 | 21    30
          |-----------
          |  7    10
    Answer: HCF = 2 × 2 × 3 = 12. LCM = 2 × 2 × 3 × 7 × 10 = 840.
    Teacher explains: Write the two numbers side by side. Divide BOTH by a prime
    that goes into both, write the quotients underneath, and repeat: 2 goes into
    84 and 120 (42, 60), 2 again (21, 30), 3 (7, 10). Now 7 and 10 have no
    common factor except 1, so stop. The HCF is the product of the primes down
    the side: 2 × 2 × 3 = 12. The LCM is the side primes times the numbers left
    at the bottom: 12 × 7 × 10 = 840. Check: 840 ÷ 84 = 10 and 840 ÷ 120 = 7.
    Now you try: 48 and 180. (HCF 12, LCM 720)

D5. Find the HCF and LCM of 72 and 108 using prime factorisation.
         72 = 2 × 2 × 2 × 3 × 3       = 2³ × 3²
        108 = 2 × 2     × 3 × 3 × 3   = 2² × 3³
    Answer: 72 = 2³ × 3², 108 = 2² × 3³. HCF = 2² × 3² = 36; LCM = 2³ × 3³ =
    216.
    Teacher explains: Write both factorisations one under the other, lining up
    the same primes. For the HCF take only the primes in BOTH numbers, each with
    the SMALLER exponent: 2² (not 2³) and 3² (not 3³): 4 × 9 = 36. For the LCM
    take every prime that appears, with the BIGGER exponent: 2³ and 3³: 8 × 27 =
    216. Why? The HCF must fit inside both numbers, so it cannot use more 2s
    than the number with fewer 2s; the LCM must contain both numbers, so it
    needs as many 2s as the number with more. Now you try: 60 = 2² × 3 × 5 and
    90 = 2 × 3² × 5. (HCF 30, LCM 180)

D6. Find the HCF of 1,071 and 462 by Euclid's division algorithm.
        1,071 = 2 × 462 + 147
        462 = 3 × 147 + 21
        147 = 7 × 21 + 0
    Answer: HCF(1,071, 462) = 21.
    Teacher explains: Divide the bigger number by the smaller and write it as
    dividend = quotient × divisor + remainder: 1,071 = 2 × 462 + 147. Now forget
    1,071: divide the old divisor 462 by the remainder 147: 462 = 3 × 147 + 21.
    Then 147 by 21: 147 = 7 × 21 + 0. The remainder is 0, so the last divisor,
    21, is the HCF. Check: 1,071 ÷ 21 = 51 and 462 ÷ 21 = 22, and 51 and 22 have
    no common factor. Euclid's method is fast for big numbers where listing
    factors would take ages. Now you try: HCF(252, 105). (252 = 2 × 105 + 42,
    105 = 2 × 42 + 21, 42 = 2 × 21 + 0; HCF 21)

D7. Use Euclid's algorithm to find HCF(667, 391), and explain why the method
    works.
        667 = 1 × 391 + 276
        391 = 1 × 276 + 115
        276 = 2 × 115 + 46
        115 = 2 × 46 + 23
        46 = 2 × 23 + 0
    Answer: HCF = 23.
    Teacher explains: Steps: 667 = 1 × 391 + 276; 391 = 1 × 276 + 115; 276 = 2 ×
    115 + 46; 115 = 2 × 46 + 23; 46 = 2 × 23 + 0. The last non-zero remainder is
    23. Why it works: any number that divides 667 and 391 also divides 667 − 391
    = 276 (take equal groups away from both). So 667 and 391 have exactly the
    same common factors as 391 and 276, and the same HCF. Each step swaps the
    pair for a smaller pair with the same HCF, until one number divides the
    other exactly. Check: 667 = 29 × 23 and 391 = 17 × 23. Now you try: HCF(437,
    323). (437 = 1 × 323 + 114, 323 = 2 × 114 + 95, 114 = 1 × 95 + 19, 95 = 5 ×
    19 + 0; HCF 19)

D8. Check that HCF × LCM = product for 12 and 18. Then use the rule to find the
    LCM of 667 and 391 (their HCF is 23).
    Answer: HCF(12, 18) = 6, LCM = 36, 6 × 36 = 216 = 12 × 18. LCM(667, 391) =
    667 × 391 ÷ 23 = 11,339.
    Teacher explains: 12 = 2² × 3 and 18 = 2 × 3². The HCF takes the smaller
    power of each prime and the LCM the bigger power, so between them they use
    every prime factor of both numbers exactly once: HCF × LCM = 2³ × 3³ = 216 =
    12 × 18. This gives a quick LCM: LCM = product ÷ HCF. Divide first to keep
    numbers small: 391 ÷ 23 = 17, so LCM = 667 × 17 = 11,339. The rule is for
    TWO numbers only; for three numbers it does not work. Now you try: HCF(24,
    36) = 12; find the LCM. (24 × 36 ÷ 12 = 72)

D9. Are 8 and 15 co-prime? Find their HCF and LCM. Are 9 and 21 co-prime?
    Answer: 8 and 15 are co-prime: HCF = 1 and LCM = 8 × 15 = 120. 9 and 21 are
    not: HCF = 3.
    Teacher explains: Factors of 8: 1, 2, 4, 8. Factors of 15: 1, 3, 5, 15. The
    only common factor is 1, so they are CO-PRIME (relatively prime). Their
    prime factorisations 2³ and 3 × 5 share no prime, so the LCM uses all of
    them: 120. Neither 8 nor 15 is prime; co-prime is about the PAIR. Two
    different primes are always co-prime, and two consecutive numbers (like 20
    and 21) are always co-prime. 9 = 3² and 21 = 3 × 7 share the prime 3. Now
    you try: 14 and 25; 16 and 28. (co-prime; not co-prime, HCF 4)

D10. A hall is 4.5 m long and 3.6 m wide. It is to be covered with square tiles,
     all the same size, without cutting any. What is the largest tile that can
     be used, and how many are needed?
     Answer: Tiles of side 90 cm; 5 × 4 = 20 tiles. HCF(450, 360) = 90.
     Teacher explains: Change to centimetres so the numbers are whole: 450 cm
     and 360 cm. The side of the tile must divide both lengths exactly, and we
     want the LARGEST, so we need the HCF. 450 = 2 × 3² × 5², 360 = 2³ × 3² × 5;
     HCF = 2 × 3² × 5 = 90 cm. (Euclid: 450 = 1 × 360 + 90, 360 = 4 × 90 + 0.)
     Along the length 450 ÷ 90 = 5 tiles, along the width 360 ÷ 90 = 4 tiles, so
     20 tiles. Draw the 5 by 4 grid to see it. Now you try: a floor 6 m by 4.8
     m. (tiles of 120 cm; 5 × 4 = 20 tiles)

D11. Three temple bells ring every 6, 8 and 12 minutes. They ring together at
     9:00 a.m. When will they next ring together? How many times will they ring
     together from 9:00 a.m. to 11:00 a.m., counting both?
     Answer: At 9:24 a.m. LCM(6, 8, 12) = 24. They ring together 6 times: 9:00,
     9:24, 9:48, 10:12, 10:36, 11:00.
     Teacher explains: We need the smallest time that is a multiple of 6, 8 AND
     12. Prime factors: 6 = 2 × 3, 8 = 2³, 12 = 2² × 3. Take each prime with its
     biggest power: 2³ × 3 = 24 minutes. (Or list multiples of 12: 12 (not a
     multiple of 8), 24 (yes, and of 6).) Two hours is 120 minutes and 120 ÷ 24
     = 5 gaps, so 6 rings together counting the one at 9:00. Now you try: lights
     that flash every 4, 6 and 10 seconds. (every 60 seconds)

D12. Three ribbons are 72 cm, 96 cm and 120 cm long. They are cut into pieces
     all of the same length, as long as possible, with nothing wasted. How long
     is each piece and how many pieces are there?
     Answer: 24 cm pieces; 3 + 4 + 5 = 12 pieces. HCF(72, 96, 120) = 24.
     Teacher explains: The piece length divides all three lengths and is as big
     as possible: the HCF of three numbers. 72 = 2³ × 3², 96 = 2⁵ × 3, 120 = 2³
     × 3 × 5. Common primes with smallest powers: 2³ × 3 = 24. (Or: HCF(72, 96)
     = 24, then HCF(24, 120) = 24.) Pieces: 72 ÷ 24 = 3, 96 ÷ 24 = 4, 120 ÷ 24 =
     5, total 12. Deciding HCF or LCM: here we are cutting into smaller equal
     pieces, so HCF; when things repeat and we wait for them to meet, LCM. Now
     you try: what is the smallest number that leaves remainder 5 when divided
     by 12, by 15 and by 20? (LCM 60, so 65)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. List the factors of each number and find the HCF.
    a) 12, 18   b) 18, 24   c) 16, 20   d) 15, 25
    e) 14, 21   f) 24, 36   g) 9, 12    h) 20, 30
    i) 27, 36   j) 8, 12    k) 10, 15   l) 32, 48

      d) 15: 1, 3, 5, 15; 25: 1, 5, 25; HCF 5
      e) 14: 1, 2, 7, 14; 21: 1, 3, 7, 21; HCF 7
      f) 24: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 24; 36: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36; HCF 12
      g) 9: 1, 3, 9; 12: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12; HCF 3
      h) 20: 1, 2, 4, 5, 10, 20; 30: 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 10, 15, 30; HCF 10
      i) 27: 1, 3, 9, 27; 36: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36; HCF 9
      j) 8: 1, 2, 4, 8; 12: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 12; HCF 4
      k) 10: 1, 2, 5, 10; 15: 1, 3, 5, 15; HCF 5
      l) 32: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32; 48: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24, 48; HCF 16
      m) 7: 1, 7; 13: 1, 13; HCF 1
      n) 30: 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 10, 15, 30; 45: 1, 3, 5, 9, 15, 45; HCF 15
      o) 28: 1, 2, 4, 7, 14, 28; 42: 1, 2, 3, 6, 7, 14, 21, 42; HCF 14
      p) 36: 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 9, 12, 18, 36; 60: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 10, 12, 15, 20,
         30, 60; HCF 12

M2. List multiples of each number until you find the LCM.
    a) 4, 6     b) 6, 8     c) 8, 12    d) 9, 12
    e) 10, 15   f) 12, 18   g) 5, 7     h) 6, 9
    i) 4, 10    j) 15, 20   k) 8, 20    l) 12, 16
    m) 3, 11    n) 14, 21   o) 18, 27   p) 25, 30
    Answers:
      a) 4: 4, 8, 12; 6: 6, 12; LCM 12
      b) 6: 6, 12, 18, 24; 8: 8, 16, 24; LCM 24

      e) 10: 10, 20, 30; 15: 15, 30; LCM 30

      g) 5: 5, 10, 15, 20, 25, 30, 35; 7: 7, 14, 21, 28, 35; LCM 35
      h) 6: 6, 12, 18; 9: 9, 18; LCM 18
      i) 4: 4, 8, 12, 16, 20; 10: 10, 20; LCM 20
      j) 15: 15, 30, 45, 60; 20: 20, 40, 60; LCM 60
      k) 8: 8, 16, 24, 32, 40; 20: 20, 40; LCM 40
      l) 12: 12, 24, 36, 48; 16: 16, 32, 48; LCM 48
      m) 3: 3, 6, 9, 12, 15, 18, 21, 24, 27, 30, 33; 11: 11, 22, 33; LCM 33
      n) 14: 14, 28, 42; 21: 21, 42; LCM 42

      p) 25: 25, 50, 75, 100, 125, 150; 30: 30, 60, 90, 120, 150; LCM 150

M3. Write as a product of primes (use exponents). Use a factor tree or the
    ladder.
    a) 12     b) 18     c) 24     d) 30     e) 36     f) 45
    g) 48     h) 60     i) 64     j) 72     k) 81     l) 84
    m) 90     n) 96     o) 100    p) 108    q) 120    r) 126
    s) 144    t) 150    u) 180    v) 210    w) 225    x) 360
    y) 1000   z) 97
    Answers: a) 2² × 3          b) 2 × 3²          c) 2³ × 3
             d) 2 × 3 × 5       e) 2² × 3²         f) 3² × 5
             g) 2⁴ × 3          h) 2² × 3 × 5      i) 2⁶
             j) 2³ × 3²         k) 3⁴              l) 2² × 3 × 7
             m) 2 × 3² × 5      n) 2⁵ × 3          o) 2² × 5²
             p) 2² × 3³         q) 2³ × 3 × 5      r) 2 × 3² × 7
             s) 2⁴ × 3²         t) 2 × 3 × 5²      u) 2² × 3² × 5
             v) 2 × 3 × 5 × 7   w) 3² × 5²         x) 2³ × 3² × 5
             y) 2³ × 5³         z) 97

M4. Find the HCF by prime factorisation. Give the HCF as a product of primes and
    as a number.
    a) 72, 108     b) 60, 90      c) 84, 120     d) 48, 180
    e) 96, 144     f) 126, 210    g) 150, 225    h) 64, 80
    i) 98, 140     j) 135, 225    k) 1000, 250   l) 54, 81
    m) 112, 168    n) 99, 165     o) 360, 480    p) 26, 39
    Answers: a) 2² × 3² = 36       b) 2 × 3 × 5 = 30
             c) 2² × 3 = 12        d) 2² × 3 = 12
             e) 2⁴ × 3 = 48        f) 2 × 3 × 7 = 42
             g) 3 × 5² = 75        h) 2⁴ = 16
             i) 2 × 7 = 14         j) 3² × 5 = 45
             k) 2 × 5³ = 250       l) 3³ = 27
             m) 2³ × 7 = 56        n) 3 × 11 = 33
             o) 2³ × 3 × 5 = 120   p) 13

M5. Find the LCM by prime factorisation. Give the LCM as a product of primes and
    as a number.
    a) 72, 108    b) 60, 90     c) 12, 20     d) 18, 30
    e) 24, 40     f) 36, 48     g) 45, 75     h) 16, 24
    i) 28, 35     j) 50, 80     k) 21, 49     l) 32, 40
    m) 27, 45     n) 44, 66     o) 100, 150   p) 64, 96
    Answers: a) 2³ × 3³ = 216       b) 2² × 3² × 5 = 180
             c) 2² × 3 × 5 = 60     d) 2 × 3² × 5 = 90
             e) 2³ × 3 × 5 = 120    f) 2⁴ × 3² = 144
             g) 3² × 5² = 225       h) 2⁴ × 3 = 48
             i) 2² × 5 × 7 = 140    j) 2⁴ × 5² = 400
             k) 3 × 7² = 147        l) 2⁵ × 5 = 160
             m) 3³ × 5 = 135        n) 2² × 3 × 11 = 132
             o) 2² × 3 × 5² = 300   p) 2⁶ × 3 = 192

M6. Find the HCF by Euclid's division algorithm. Show every step.
    a) 1,071 and 462      b) 252 and 105        c) 437 and 323
    d) 391 and 667        e) 144 and 90         f) 525 and 231
    g) 1,001 and 385      h) 2,024 and 1,518    i) 455 and 42
    j) 729 and 243        k) 4,052 and 12,576   l) 851 and 629
    Answers:
      a) 1,071 = 2 × 462 + 147; 462 = 3 × 147 + 21; 147 = 7 × 21 + 0; HCF 21
      b) 252 = 2 × 105 + 42; 105 = 2 × 42 + 21; 42 = 2 × 21 + 0; HCF 21
      c) 437 = 1 × 323 + 114; 323 = 2 × 114 + 95; 114 = 1 × 95 + 19; 95 = 5 × 19
         + 0; HCF 19
      d) 667 = 1 × 391 + 276; 391 = 1 × 276 + 115; 276 = 2 × 115 + 46; 115 = 2 ×
         46 + 23; 46 = 2 × 23 + 0; HCF 23
      e) 144 = 1 × 90 + 54; 90 = 1 × 54 + 36; 54 = 1 × 36 + 18; 36 = 2 × 18 + 0;
         HCF 18
      f) 525 = 2 × 231 + 63; 231 = 3 × 63 + 42; 63 = 1 × 42 + 21; 42 = 2 × 21 +
         0; HCF 21
      g) 1,001 = 2 × 385 + 231; 385 = 1 × 231 + 154; 231 = 1 × 154 + 77; 154 = 2
         × 77 + 0; HCF 77
      h) 2,024 = 1 × 1,518 + 506; 1,518 = 3 × 506 + 0; HCF 506
      i) 455 = 10 × 42 + 35; 42 = 1 × 35 + 7; 35 = 5 × 7 + 0; HCF 7
      j) 729 = 3 × 243 + 0; HCF 243
      k) 12,576 = 3 × 4,052 + 420; 4,052 = 9 × 420 + 272; 420 = 1 × 272 + 148;
         272 = 1 × 148 + 124; 148 = 1 × 124 + 24; 124 = 5 × 24 + 4; 24 = 6 × 4 +
         0; HCF 4
      l) 851 = 1 × 629 + 222; 629 = 2 × 222 + 185; 222 = 1 × 185 + 37; 185 = 5 ×
         37 + 0; HCF 37

M7. Find the HCF and the LCM of the three numbers.
    a) 6, 8, 12      b) 4, 6, 10      c) 12, 18, 24    d) 72, 96, 120
    e) 8, 12, 20     f) 15, 20, 30    g) 9, 12, 15     h) 10, 14, 35
    i) 16, 24, 40    j) 18, 27, 45    k) 5, 7, 11      l) 21, 28, 42
    m) 24, 30, 36    n) 2, 3, 4
    Answers: a) HCF 2, LCM 24       b) HCF 2, LCM 60       c) HCF 6, LCM 72
             d) HCF 24, LCM 1,440   e) HCF 4, LCM 120      f) HCF 5, LCM 60
             g) HCF 3, LCM 180      h) HCF 1, LCM 70       i) HCF 8, LCM 240
             j) HCF 9, LCM 270      k) HCF 1, LCM 385      l) HCF 7, LCM 84
             m) HCF 6, LCM 360      n) HCF 1, LCM 12

M8. Find the HCF and the LCM, then check that HCF × LCM = the product of the
    numbers.
    a) 12, 18   b) 8, 20    c) 15, 35   d) 24, 36
    e) 9, 14    f) 16, 40   g) 21, 30   h) 45, 60
    i) 7, 49    j) 26, 65   k) 32, 48   l) 50, 75
    Answers: a) 6 × 36 = 216 = 12 × 18       b) 4 × 40 = 160 = 8 × 20
             c) 5 × 105 = 525 = 15 × 35      d) 12 × 72 = 864 = 24 × 36
             e) 1 × 126 = 126 = 9 × 14       f) 8 × 80 = 640 = 16 × 40
             g) 3 × 210 = 630 = 21 × 30      h) 15 × 180 = 2,700 = 45 × 60
             i) 7 × 49 = 343 = 7 × 49        j) 13 × 130 = 1,690 = 26 × 65
             k) 16 × 96 = 1,536 = 32 × 48    l) 25 × 150 = 3,750 = 50 × 75

M9. Use HCF × LCM = product of the two numbers.
    a) HCF 6, LCM 72, one number 18; other?
    b) HCF 4, LCM 60, one number 12; other?
    c) HCF 12, LCM 144, one number 36; other?
    d) HCF 5, LCM 150, one number 25; other?
    e) HCF 9, LCM 270, one number 27; other?
    f) HCF 8, LCM 96, one number 24; other?
    g) HCF 15, LCM 90, one number 45; other?
    h) HCF 1, LCM 35, one number 5; other?
    i) HCF(391, 667) = 23; LCM?
    j) HCF(84, 120) = 12; LCM?
    k) HCF(144, 90) = 18; LCM?
    l) HCF(437, 323) = 19; LCM?
    m) Can HCF = 4 and LCM = 30?
    n) Can HCF = 6 and LCM = 90?
    o) Can HCF = 5 and LCM = 5?
    p) Can HCF = 8 and LCM = 36?
    Answers:
      a) 24
      b) 20
      c) 48
      d) 30
      e) 90
      f) 32
      g) 30
      h) 7
      i) 11,339
      j) 840
      k) 720
      l) 7,429
      m) no (4 does not divide 30)
      n) yes (6 divides 90, e.g. 18 and 30)
      o) yes (5 and 5)
      p) no (8 does not divide 36)

M10. Co-prime or not? If not, give the HCF.
     a) 8, 15     b) 9, 21     c) 14, 25    d) 16, 28    e) 20, 21
     f) 35, 49    g) 12, 35    h) 27, 64    i) 33, 55    j) 17, 51
     k) 11, 13    l) 1, 100    m) 45, 56    n) 26, 39    o) 99, 100
     p) 34, 85    q) 49, 50    r) 18, 81    s) 25, 36    t) 91, 65
     Answers: a) co-prime     b) no, HCF 3    c) co-prime     d) no, HCF 4
              e) co-prime     f) no, HCF 7    g) co-prime     h) co-prime
              i) no, HCF 11   j) no, HCF 17   k) co-prime     l) co-prime
              m) co-prime     n) no, HCF 13   o) co-prime     p) no, HCF 17
              q) co-prime     r) no, HCF 9    s) co-prime     t) no, HCF 13

M11. Simplify each fraction in ONE step by dividing top and bottom by their HCF.
     a) 18/24      b) 36/60      c) 42/56      d) 84/120
     e) 72/108     f) 45/75      g) 96/144     h) 125/1000
     i) 64/80      j) 91/119     k) 111/148    l) 221/247
     m) 210/330    n) 48/180     o) 143/187    p) 105/252
     Answers: a) ÷6: 3/4   b) ÷12: 3/5   c) ÷14: 3/4   d) ÷12: 7/10
              e) ÷36: 2/3   f) ÷15: 3/5   g) ÷48: 2/3   h) ÷125: 1/8
              i) ÷16: 4/5   j) ÷7: 13/17   k) ÷37: 3/4   l) ÷13: 17/19
              m) ÷30: 7/11   n) ÷12: 4/15   o) ÷11: 13/17   p) ÷21: 5/12

M12. Find the HCF and the LCM (any method).
     a) 6, 18      b) 25, 100    c) 13, 13     d) 1, 17      e) 7, 11
     f) 24, 25     g) 40, 60     h) 54, 72     i) 65, 91     j) 120, 150
     k) 17, 34     l) 36, 54     m) 38, 57     n) 100, 1     o) 80, 120
     p) 121, 143   q) 48, 64     r) 75, 90     s) 144, 216   t) 250, 400
     Answers: a) HCF 6, LCM 18       b) HCF 25, LCM 100     c) HCF 13, LCM 13
              d) HCF 1, LCM 17       e) HCF 1, LCM 77       f) HCF 1, LCM 600
              g) HCF 20, LCM 120     h) HCF 18, LCM 216     i) HCF 13, LCM 455
              j) HCF 30, LCM 600     k) HCF 17, LCM 34      l) HCF 18, LCM 108
              m) HCF 19, LCM 114     n) HCF 1, LCM 100      o) HCF 40, LCM 240
