              c) co-interior (sum 180°)   d) vertically opposite (equal)
              e) corresponding (equal)   f) alternate (equal)
              g) co-interior (sum 180°)   h) vertically opposite (equal)
              i) corresponding (equal)   j) vertically opposite (equal)
              k) corresponding (equal)   l) vertically opposite (equal)
              m) on a straight line (sum 180°)
              n) on a straight line (sum 180°)   o) corresponding (equal)
              p) vertically opposite (equal)

M12. Name the vertex and the arms of each angle (a to h), then count (i to p).
     a) ∠ABC
     b) ∠PQR
     c) ∠XYZ
     d) ∠MON
     e) ∠DEF
     f) ∠CBA
     g) ∠TSR
     h) ∠KLM
     i) 3 points on a line: how many segments?
     j) 4 points on a line: how many segments?
     k) 5 points on a line: how many segments?
     l) 6 points on a line: how many segments?
     m) 8 points on a line: how many segments?
     n) 10 points on a line: how many segments?
     o) A, B, C on a line in that order: rays starting at B?
     p) 4 points, no three on a line: how many lines?
     Answers: a) vertex B; arms BA, BC   b) vertex Q; arms QP, QR
              c) vertex Y; arms YX, YZ   d) vertex O; arms OM, ON
              e) vertex E; arms ED, EF   f) vertex B; arms BC, BA
              g) vertex S; arms ST, SR   h) vertex L; arms LK, LM   i) 3   j) 6
              k) 10   l) 15   m) 28   n) 45   o) 2 (BA and BC)   p) 6

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: make angles with two geostrips or pencils joined at the vertex;
  fold paper to make right angles and straight lines and tear off the corners to
  see that angles on a line make 180°; use scissors to see vertically opposite
  angles; say the fact before writing the sum. Harder: find missing angles in
  diagrams with several lines through one point; prove that alternate angles are
  equal using corresponding and vertically opposite angles; find the angle
  between the hands of a clock at 3:00 (90°), 4:00 (120°) and 2:30 (105°);
  design a puzzle with parallel lines for a friend.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.22 — ANGLE SUM OF A TRIANGLE (180°) AND OF A QUADRILATERAL (360°), BY
             TEARING AND MEASURING
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - The three angles of EVERY triangle add up to 180°, whatever its shape or
    size. We can SEE this by tearing: colour the three corners of a paper
    triangle, tear them off and place them side by side at one point — they fit
    exactly along a straight line, and a straight angle is 180°.
  - Measuring with a protractor gives a sum close to 180° (179° or 181° is
    normal): small errors come from thick pencil lines and reading the scale.
    The rule itself is exact.
  - Missing angle of a triangle = 180° − (sum of the other two). Example:
    180° − (50° + 70°) = 60°.
  - Special triangles: an EQUILATERAL triangle has three angles of 60°. An
    ISOSCELES triangle has two equal BASE angles (opposite the equal sides):
    base angle = (180° − apex) ÷ 2 and apex = 180° − 2 × base angle. In a
    RIGHT-ANGLED triangle the two acute angles add up to 90°.
  - A triangle can have at most ONE right angle or ONE obtuse angle; the other
    two angles are always acute. Three angles make a triangle only if each is
    more than 0° and they add up to exactly 180°.
  - EXTERIOR ANGLE: extend one side of a triangle. The outside angle equals the
    SUM of the two interior angles opposite to it, and it makes 180° with the
    interior angle next to it.
  - The four angles of every QUADRILATERAL add up to 360°: tear off the four
    corners and they fit round a point, or cut along a diagonal into two
    triangles, 2 × 180° = 360°. Missing angle = 360° − (sum of the other three).
  - A polygon with n sides can be cut into (n − 2) triangles from one corner, so
    its angles add up to (n − 2) × 180°. In a REGULAR polygon all angles are
    equal: each angle = (n − 2) × 180° ÷ n.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Sana cuts a triangle out of paper, colours its three corners red, blue and
    green, tears them off and places them side by side with their points
    together. What does she see, and what does it show?
            /\                     blue
           /R \          red  \   |   /  green
          /    \                \  |  /
         /B    G\                 \ | /
        ----------         ---------+---------
    Answer: The three corners fit together exactly along a straight line. A
    straight angle is 180°, so the three angles of the triangle add up to 180°.
    Teacher explains: Show it on the board step by step. (1) Draw any triangle —
    make it odd-shaped on purpose. (2) Mark the three angles with arcs of
    different colours. (3) Tear (do not cut straight) so the corners are easy to
    recognise. (4) Put the three points together on a ruled line, with no gaps
    and no overlaps. The outer edges make one straight line, and angles on a
    straight line add up to 180°.
    Now let every child in the class try with a DIFFERENT triangle: long and
    thin, fat, right-angled. It works every time. That is the power of the fact:
    180° for EVERY triangle. Common mistake: leaving small gaps between the
    corners and then thinking the sum is more than 180°. Now you try: tear the
    corners of a right-angled triangle. Which two corners together make a right
    angle? (the two acute corners, 90°)

D2. Ravi measures the angles of his triangle with a protractor and gets 62°, 57°
    and 63°. He says, "The angles of my triangle add up to 182°, so the rule is
    wrong." What would you tell him?
    Answer: 62° + 57° + 63° = 182°. The true sum is exactly 180°; the extra 2°
    comes from small measuring errors, not from the rule.
    Teacher explains: A protractor can only be read to about 1°, and a thick
    pencil line or a vertex not placed exactly on the centre mark adds another
    degree. Three readings each 1° too big give 3° too much. The rule has been
    PROVED (by tearing and later by parallel lines), so we trust it and look
    again at our measurements. A good habit: measure all three angles, add them,
    and if the total is not between 178° and 182°, measure again more carefully.
    Tell Ravi to sharpen his pencil, draw a bigger triangle and read from the 0
    on the arm each time. Now you try: Meera measures 48°, 71° and 60°. Is her
    measuring good? (sum 179°, yes — within 1°)

D3. A triangular kite has two angles of 48° and 75°. Find the third angle.
    Answer: 57°. 48° + 75° = 123° and 180° − 123° = 57°.
    Teacher explains: Write the rule first: A + B + C = 180°. Put in what you
    know: 48° + 75° + C = 180°. Add the known angles: 48 + 75 = 123. The third
    angle is what is left from 180: 180 − 123 = 57. So C = 57°. Check by adding
    all three: 48° + 75° + 57° = 180°.
         1 8 0
       − 1 2 3
       -------
           5 7
    Common mistake: subtracting only one angle (180 − 75 = 105) and forgetting
    the other. Now you try: two angles are 36° and 97°. (47°)

D4. A "Give Way" road sign is an equilateral triangle. What is the size of each
    of its angles? Why?
    Answer: 60°. The three angles are equal and add up to 180°, so each is
    180° ÷ 3 = 60°.
    Teacher explains: EQUILATERAL means "equal sides". A triangle with equal
    sides also has equal angles (fold a paper equilateral triangle along each
    line of symmetry and the corners match). Three equal parts of 180°:
    180 ÷ 3 = 60. So every equilateral triangle — tiny or huge — has angles 60°,
    60°, 60°. This is why we can construct 60° with a compass (topic 5.23).
    The reverse is also true: if a triangle has all three angles 60°, it is
    equilateral. Now you try: a triangle has angles 60° and 60°. What is the
    third angle, and what kind of triangle is it? (60°, equilateral)

D5. The roof of a hut is an isosceles triangle. The angle at the top (the apex)
    is 40°. What are the two base angles?
                /\  40°
               /  \
              /    \
             /      \
            /__    __\
            ?          ?
    Answer: 70° each. 180° − 40° = 140° and 140° ÷ 2 = 70°.
    Teacher explains: In an ISOSCELES triangle two sides are equal, and the
    angles opposite them (the BASE angles) are equal too. Step 1: take away the
    apex from 180°: 180 − 40 = 140. This 140° is shared EQUALLY by the two base
    angles. Step 2: 140 ÷ 2 = 70. So the base angles are 70° and 70°. Check:
    40° + 70° + 70° = 180°.
    The other direction: if each base angle is 65°, the apex is
    180° − 2 × 65° = 180° − 130° = 50°. Common mistake: halving 180° first, or
    forgetting to double the base angle. Now you try: apex 100°; find each base
    angle. (40°)

D6. A ladder leans against a vertical wall. It makes an angle of 62° with the
    ground. What angle does it make with the wall?
    Answer: 28°. The wall meets the ground at 90°, so the other two angles add
    up to 90°: 90° − 62° = 28°.
    Teacher explains: The wall, the ground and the ladder make a RIGHT-ANGLED
    triangle. The right angle is 90°, so the other two angles share the
    remaining 180° − 90° = 90°. They are always both acute and they add up to
    90°. So the angle at the top is 90° − 62° = 28°. Check:
    62° + 28° + 90° = 180°.
            |\
            | \  ladder
       wall |  \
            |   \
            |_   \ 62°
            |_|___\____ ground
    Now you try: in a right-angled triangle one acute angle is 35°. Find the
    other. (55°)

D7. Can a triangle have two right angles? Two obtuse angles? Can the angles 50°,
    60° and 80° be the angles of a triangle?
    Answer: No, no, and no. Two right angles already make 180°, leaving 0° for
    the third angle. Two obtuse angles make more than 180°. And
    50° + 60° + 80° = 190°, not 180°.
    Teacher explains: Use the rule as a test. Two right angles:
    90° + 90° = 180°, and the third corner would be 0° — the two "sides" would
    be parallel and never meet. Two obtuse angles, even 91° + 91° = 182°, are
    already over 180°. So a triangle has AT MOST one right or obtuse angle, and
    at least two acute angles.
    To test three angles, add them: they make a triangle only if the sum is
    exactly 180° (and every angle is more than 0°). 50° + 60° + 80° = 190°, so
    no. But 50° + 60° + 70° = 180°, so yes. Now you try: can 30°, 45° and 105°
    be the angles of a triangle? (yes, sum 180°)

D8. In triangle ABC, angle A = 45° and angle B = 65°. The side BC is extended
    beyond C to a point D. Find the exterior angle ACD.
              A
              /\ 45°
             /  \
            /    \
       65° /      \  ?
        B ---------C--------- D
    Answer: 110°. The exterior angle equals the sum of the two opposite interior
    angles: 45° + 65° = 110°.
    Teacher explains: First way (step by step): angle C inside the triangle is
    180° − (45° + 65°) = 180° − 110° = 70°. Angle ACD is on a straight line with
    it, so ACD = 180° − 70° = 110°.
    Second way (the shortcut): A + B and the exterior angle ACD are BOTH what is
    left when angle C is taken from 180°. In the triangle: A + B = 180° − C. On
    the line: ACD = 180° − C. So ACD = A + B. The EXTERIOR ANGLE is the sum of
    the two INTERIOR OPPOSITE angles. Tear off corners A and B and they fit
    exactly into angle ACD. Common mistake: adding the angle next to the
    exterior angle (C) instead of the two far ones. Now you try: interior
    opposite angles 38° and 77°. (115°)

D9. Explain in two ways why the four angles of any quadrilateral add up to 360°.
    Answer: Tearing: the four corners fit exactly round a point, and a full turn
    is 360°. Cutting: a diagonal splits the quadrilateral into two triangles,
    and 2 × 180° = 360°.
    Teacher explains: Draw quadrilateral ABCD and its diagonal AC on the board.
        A +-----------+ B
          |  \        |
          |     \     |
          |        \  |
        D +-----------+ C
    Way 1: draw a quadrilateral, tear off its four corners and put the points
    together: they fill the space round the point with no gap — a full turn,
    360°. Way 2: draw diagonal AC. It cuts ABCD into triangles ABC and ACD. The
    angles of the two triangles together make up exactly the four angles of the
    quadrilateral, so the total is 180° + 180° = 360°. Check with a rectangle:
    4 × 90° = 360°.
    Common mistake: thinking a bigger quadrilateral has a bigger angle sum. Size
    does not matter — only the number of sides. Now you try: how many triangles
    does a diagonal make in a square, and what is the angle sum? (2 triangles,
    360°)

D10. A farmer's field is a quadrilateral. Three of its corners measure 85°, 110°
     and 75°. What is the fourth angle?
     Answer: 90°. 85° + 110° + 75° = 270° and 360° − 270° = 90°.
     Teacher explains: Rule: the four angles add up to 360°. Add the three we
     know: 85 + 110 = 195, 195 + 75 = 270. Subtract from 360: 360 − 270 = 90.
     The fourth corner is a right angle. Check: 85° + 110° + 75° + 90° = 360°.
     Common mistake: using 180° (the triangle rule) for a quadrilateral. Before
     you start, say aloud: "triangle 180, quadrilateral 360". Now you try: three
     angles are 120°, 95° and 60°. (85°)

D11. One angle of a parallelogram is 70°. Find the other three angles.
     Answer: 110°, 70° and 110°. Opposite angles are equal, and two angles next
     to each other add up to 180°.
     Teacher explains: Draw the parallelogram and mark the angles.
              +------------------+
             / 110°         70° /
            /                  /
           / 70°         110° /
          +------------------+
     In a parallelogram the opposite angles are equal, so there are two angles
     of one size and two of another. Call them x and y: x + y + x + y = 360°, so
     x + y = 180°. Neighbouring angles add up to 180°. So the angle next to 70°
     is 180° − 70° = 110°. The four angles are 70°, 110°, 70°, 110°. Check:
     70 + 110 + 70 + 110 = 360.
     The same is true for a rhombus (a special parallelogram). In a KITE only
     one pair of opposite angles is equal. Now you try: a rhombus has an angle
     of 124°. Find the others. (56°, 124°, 56°)

D12. What do the angles of a pentagon add up to? What is each angle of a regular
     hexagon (like a cell of a honeycomb)?
     Answer: Pentagon: 540°. Regular hexagon: each angle is 120°.
     Teacher explains: From one corner of a pentagon draw all the diagonals:
     they cut it into 3 triangles, so the angles add up to 3 × 180° = 540°. A
     polygon with n sides gives (n − 2) triangles, so the angle sum is
     (n − 2) × 180°.
        sides n   triangles   angle sum
           3          1          180°
           4          2          360°
           5          3          540°
           6          4          720°
     A hexagon has 6 sides: (6 − 2) × 180° = 720°. In a REGULAR hexagon the 6
     angles are equal: 720° ÷ 6 = 120°. Bees use this shape because three angles
     of 120° fit exactly round a point: 3 × 120° = 360°. Now you try: each angle
     of a regular octagon. (1080° ÷ 8 = 135°)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

M4. Can these three angles be the angles of a triangle? Write yes or no; if no,
    give the reason (the sum, or an angle of 0°).
    a) 50°, 60°, 70°    b) 90°, 45°, 45°    c) 100°, 50°, 40°

M5. Two angles of a triangle are given. Find the third angle and say whether the

M11. Find the angle x.
     a) triangle: x, x, x                 b) triangle: 50°, x, x
     c) triangle: x, 2x, 90°              d) triangle: x, 2x, 3x
     e) triangle: x, x, 90°               f) triangle: x, 3x, 100°
     g) triangle: x, x + 20°, 70°         h) triangle: 2x, 2x, x
     i) quadrilateral: x, x, x, x         j) quadrilateral: 100°, 100°, x, x
     k) quadrilateral: x, 2x, x, 2x       l) quadrilateral: 90°, 90°, x, 3x
     m) quadrilateral: x, 2x, 3x, 4x      n) quadrilateral: 120°, x, x, x
     o) pentagon: x, x, x, x, x           p) hexagon: x, x, x, x, x, x
     Answers: a) x = 60°   b) x = 65°   c) x = 30°   d) x = 30°   e) x = 45°
              f) x = 20°   g) x = 45°   h) x = 36°   i) x = 90°   j) x = 80°

              p) x = 120°

M12. True or false?
     a) The angles of a triangle add up to 180°.
     b) A big triangle has a bigger angle sum than a small one.
     c) A triangle can have two obtuse angles.
     d) A triangle can have one right angle and one obtuse angle.
     e) Every triangle has at least two acute angles.
     f) Each angle of an equilateral triangle is 60°.
     g) The base angles of an isosceles triangle are equal.
     h) In a right-angled triangle the acute angles add up to 90°.
     i) An exterior angle equals the sum of the two interior opposite angles.
     j) The angles of a quadrilateral add up to 180°.
     k) A quadrilateral can have four right angles.
     l) A quadrilateral can have four obtuse angles.
     m) A quadrilateral can have three obtuse angles.
     n) The angles of a pentagon add up to 540°.
     o) Each angle of a regular hexagon is 120°.
     p) Measured angles of 61°, 59°, 61° mean the rule has failed.
     Answers: a) true   b) false (always 180°)   c) false   d) false   e) true
              f) true   g) true   h) true   i) true   j) false (360°)   k) true
              l) false (more than 360°)   m) true (e.g. 100°, 100°, 100°, 60°)
              n) true   o) true   p) false (measuring error)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: let the child tear corners of many paper triangles and
  quadrilaterals and stick them in the notebook along a line or round a dot;
  write "triangle 180, quadrilateral 360" at the top of the page; do the
  missing-angle sum as two separate steps (add the known angles, then subtract
  from the total) and always check by adding all the angles back. Harder: prove
  the triangle rule with a line through one vertex parallel to the opposite side
  (alternate angles); find the exterior angles of regular polygons (they add up
  to 360°, so each is 360° ÷ n); find which regular polygons tile a floor (the
  angle must divide 360°: triangles, squares, hexagons); solve harder "find x"
  problems such as angles x + 10°, 2x − 20° and 3x + 10° of a triangle
  (x = 30°).


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.23 — CONSTRUCTIONS WITH RULER AND COMPASS (BISECTORS, 60°, 90°)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A CONSTRUCTION uses only a ruler (to draw straight lines and measure
    lengths), a compass (to draw circles and ARCS) and a sharp pencil. The
    protractor is used only at the end, to CHECK.
  - Keep the compass width fixed while you draw the arcs of one step — every
    point on an arc is the same distance from the compass point. That is the
    reason every construction works.
  - PERPENDICULAR BISECTOR of a segment AB: with a width MORE than half of AB,
    draw arcs from A and from B above and below the segment; join the two
    crossing points. The line cuts AB into two equal halves at 90°.
  - ANGLE BISECTOR: from the vertex draw an arc cutting both arms; from those
    two points draw two arcs with the same width that cross; join the vertex to
    the crossing. Each half is half the angle.
  - 60°: from the vertex O draw an arc cutting the arm at P; with the SAME
    width, from P cut the arc at Q; join OQ. Triangle OPQ is equilateral, so
    angle POQ = 60°. Two such steps give 120°.
  - 90°: bisect a straight angle (180°) — that is, draw the perpendicular at a
    point on a line; or bisect the angle between 60° and 120°. Other angles come
    from bisecting and adding: 30° = 60° ÷ 2, 45° = 90° ÷ 2, 15° = 30° ÷ 2,
    75° = 60° + 15°, 135° = 90° + 45°, 150° = 180° − 30°.
  - Also learn to COPY an angle, to drop a PERPENDICULAR from a point to a line,
    and to construct a triangle from its three sides (SSS) with the compass.
    Three lengths make a triangle only if the two shorter ones add up to MORE
    than the longest.
  - Always check a construction: measure the halves with a ruler or protractor.
    Leave all arcs showing — they are the working.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Construct the perpendicular bisector of a segment AB = 8 cm. Describe the
    steps and say how you would check it.
    Answer: Arcs of the same width (more than 4 cm) from A and B cross at P and
    Q; the line PQ is the perpendicular bisector. It meets AB at M with
    AM = MB = 4 cm and makes a right angle with AB.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: draw AB = 8 cm with the ruler. Step 2: open the
    compass to MORE than half of AB, say 5 cm. Step 3: with the point on A, draw
    an arc above and an arc below AB. Step 4: WITHOUT changing the width, put
    the point on B and draw arcs that cross the first two at P (above) and Q
    (below). Step 5: join P and Q with the ruler; it meets AB at M.
                  P
                 \|/
                  |
        A --------M-------- B
                  |
                 /|\
                  Q
    Check: AM and MB should both measure 4 cm (8 ÷ 2 = 4), and the angle at M
    should be 90°. Why it works: P is 5 cm from A and 5 cm from B, and so is Q;
    every point on PQ is the same distance from A and B. Common mistake: opening
    the compass to less than half of AB — then the arcs do not meet. Now you
    try: bisect a segment of 11 cm. What should each half measure? (5.5 cm)

D2. Bisect an angle of 70°. Describe the steps. What is the size of each half?
    Answer: Each half is 35°. 70° ÷ 2 = 35°.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: draw the 70° angle XOY with a protractor (or you
    are given it). Step 2: with the compass point on O draw an arc that cuts OX
    at A and OY at B. Step 3: from A draw an arc inside the angle; with the SAME
    width, from B draw an arc crossing it at C. Step 4: join OC. OC is the angle
    bisector.
                 Y
                /
               B     C
              /   .
             /  .
          O +.--------A------ X
    Check: measure angles XOC and COY: both should be 35°. Why it works: OA = OB
    (same arc) and AC = BC (same width), so the two triangles OAC and OBC are
    copies of each other and the two angles at O are equal. Common mistake:
    changing the width between the two arcs in step 3. Now you try: bisect 126°.
    (63° each)

D3. Construct an angle of 60° at the point O on a ray OA without a protractor.
    Why is it exactly 60°?
    Answer: Draw an arc from O cutting OA at P; with the same width, from P cut
    the arc at Q; join OQ. Angle AOQ = 60° because triangle OPQ is equilateral.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: put the compass point on O and draw a large arc
    cutting OA at P. Step 2: keep the SAME width, move the point to P and draw a
    small arc cutting the first arc at Q. Step 3: join O to Q and extend it.
                Q
               /\
              /  \
             /    \
          O +------P--- A
    Why 60°: OP = OQ (both are the first arc's radius) and PQ = OP (same width).
    So triangle OPQ has three equal sides — it is equilateral — and every angle
    of an equilateral triangle is 60° (180° ÷ 3 = 60°, topic 5.22). Common
    mistake: changing the width before step 2. Now you try: which angle do you
    get if you cut the arc again from Q, and join O to the new point? (120°)

D4. How do you construct an angle of 30°?
    Answer: Construct 60° and then bisect it: 60° ÷ 2 = 30°.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: construct 60° at O as in D3 (arc cuts the arm at
    P, the same width from P gives Q). Step 2: bisect angle POQ: from P and from
    Q draw arcs of the same width inside the angle, crossing at R. Step 3: join
    OR. Angle POR = 30°.
    The points P and Q from the 60° step can be used again, so there is no new
    arc from O. Check with the protractor: 30°. Bisecting once more gives 15°,
    and once more 7.5°. Common mistake: bisecting the wrong angle (the 120° on
    the other side), which gives 60°, not 30°. Now you try: which angle do you
    get by bisecting 30°? (15°)

D5. Construct an angle of 90° at a point P on a straight line.
    Answer: Draw an arc from P cutting the line at A and B; bisect the straight
    angle APB (arcs from A and B cross at C); join PC. Angle APC = 90°.
    Teacher explains: A straight line at P is a straight angle, 180°. Half of it
    is 180° ÷ 2 = 90°. Step 1: with the point on P draw an arc cutting the line
    at A and B (so PA = PB). Step 2: with a larger width, draw arcs from A and
    from B above the line, crossing at C. Step 3: join PC.
                C
               \|/
                |
        --A-----P-----B--
    This is the same as the perpendicular bisector of AB. A second way: mark 60°
    and 120° with two arc steps, then bisect the angle between them:
    (60° + 120°) ÷ 2 = 90°. Check with a set square or protractor. Now you try:
    what angle is on the OTHER side of PC, between PC and PB? (90°)

D6. Construct an angle of 45°.
    Answer: Construct 90° and bisect it: 90° ÷ 2 = 45°.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: construct a right angle at P as in D5. Step 2:
    from P draw an arc cutting both arms of the right angle at X and Y. Step 3:
    from X and Y draw arcs of the same width crossing at Z. Step 4: join PZ.
    Each half is 45°.
    Angles that can be made by halving: 90° → 45° → 22.5°; 180° → 90° → 45°. And
    by adding: 90° + 45° = 135°. Check the 45° with the set square that has 45°
    corners. Common mistake: bisecting the 90° angle but measuring the angle to
    the other part of the line (135°). Now you try: bisect the 45° angle. What
    do you get? (22.5°)

D7. Construct an angle of 120° at O on the ray OA.
    Answer: Draw an arc from O cutting OA at P; with the same width step from P
    to Q and from Q to R; join OR. Angle AOR = 60° + 60° = 120°.
    Teacher explains: The 60° step can be repeated: the arc is cut at P, then Q
    (60°), then R (another 60°). So angle AOR = 120°. Another way:
    180° − 60° = 120°. If you construct 60° on one side of a straight line, the
    angle on the other side is 120°.
            R        Q
             \      /
              \    /
               \  /
        ---------O--------P--- A
    Stepping round the whole circle with the same width gives 6 points
    (6 × 60° = 360°): that is how we draw a regular hexagon. Common mistake:
    joining O to Q instead of R. Now you try: how many 60° steps make a straight
    angle? (3)

D8. Explain how to construct angles of 75°, 105°, 135° and 150°.
    Answer: 75° = 60° + 15° (bisect the angle between 60° and 90°).
    105° = 90° + 15° (bisect between 90° and 120°). 135° = 90° + 45° (bisect
    between 90° and 180°). 150° = 120° + 30° (bisect between 120° and 180°).
    Teacher explains: Idea: the bisector of the angle BETWEEN two constructed
    arms lies exactly halfway. Halfway between 60° and 90° is
    (60° + 90°) ÷ 2 = 75°. Halfway between 90° and 120° is
    (90° + 120°) ÷ 2 = 105°. Halfway between 90° and 180° is
    (90° + 180°) ÷ 2 = 135°. Halfway between 120° and 180° is
    (120° + 180°) ÷ 2 = 150°.
    So on one diagram: draw the line, mark 60° and 120° with arc steps,
    construct 90°, and then bisect the gap you need. Write the plan before
    drawing, e.g. "75° = 60° + 15°". Now you try: plan 165°. (bisect between
    150° and 180°: 150° + 15°)

D9. Copy an angle ABC onto a new ray QR, without a protractor.
    Answer: With the same width draw arcs from B (cutting the arms at D and E)
    and from Q (cutting QR at F). Take the width DE and, from F, cut the second
    arc at G. Join QG. Angle GQF = angle ABC.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: from B draw an arc cutting BA at D and BC at E.
    Step 2: with the SAME width, from Q draw an arc cutting QR at F. Step 3: set
    the compass to the distance from E to D. Step 4: from F cut the new arc at
    G. Step 5: join Q to G.
    Why it works: the triangles DBE and GQF have all three sides equal (the same
    arc width twice, and DE = GF), so they are copies and the angles at B and Q
    are equal. This is useful for copying a triangle or drawing parallel lines.
    Check by laying tracing paper over both. Now you try: which width do you
    copy in step 3 — the arc radius, or the distance between the two cutting
    points? (the distance between the two cutting points)

D10. Draw the perpendicular from a point P to a line l (P is not on l). Why is
     it the SHORTEST path from P to the line?
     Answer: From P draw an arc cutting l at A and B; from A and B draw arcs of
     the same width on the other side of l, crossing at Q; join PQ, meeting l at
     M. PM is the perpendicular, and it is the shortest distance.
     Teacher explains: The finished drawing looks like this.
                  P
                  |
         l --A----M----B--
                  |
                  Q
     Step 1: with the point on P, open the compass wide enough to cut the line
     twice, at A and B. Step 2: from A and from B, with the SAME width, draw
     arcs below the line crossing at Q. Step 3: join P to Q. PQ meets l at M,
     and angle PMA = 90°. PM is the PERPENDICULAR DISTANCE from P to l.
     Any other path from P to the line, say PA, is the slanting side of the
     right-angled triangle PMA, and the side opposite the right angle is always
     the longest. This is the height used in area formulas (topic 5.25). Now you
     try: P is 4 cm from l along the perpendicular; can a slanting path from P
     to l be 3 cm? (no, it is more than 4 cm)

D11. Construct a triangle with sides 5 cm, 6 cm and 7 cm. Could you construct
     one with sides 3 cm, 4 cm and 8 cm?
     Answer: Draw AB = 7 cm; arcs of 5 cm from A and 6 cm from B cross at C;
     join AC and BC. The second is impossible: 3 + 4 = 7, which is less than 8.
     Teacher explains: Step 1: draw the longest side as the base: AB = 7 cm.
     Step 2: open the compass to 5 cm and draw an arc from A. Step 3: open it to
     6 cm and draw an arc from B crossing the first one at C. Step 4: join AC
     and BC. Check: measure AC = 5 cm and BC = 6 cm; the angles should add up to
     about 180°.
     For 3 cm, 4 cm and 8 cm: draw 8 cm, arcs of 3 cm and 4 cm from the ends —
     they cannot reach each other, because 3 + 4 = 7 is short of 8. The TRIANGLE
     RULE: the two shorter sides must add up to MORE than the longest side. If
     they are equal (3, 4, 7), the triangle is flat. Now you try: can 5 cm, 5 cm
     and 9 cm make a triangle? (yes, 5 + 5 = 10, more than 9)

D12. Kabir bisects an 80° angle and measures the halves as 38° and 42°. What
     went wrong? What should the halves be?
     Answer: Each half should be 40° (80° ÷ 2 = 40°). He most likely changed the
     compass width between the two crossing arcs, or the compass slipped.
     Teacher explains: A correct bisection gives two EQUAL halves:
     80° ÷ 2 = 40°. His two parts add up to 38° + 42° = 80°, so he did cut the
     angle, just not in the middle. The crossing point C is only on the bisector
     if the arcs from A and B have the SAME width. A loose compass that opens a
     little while drawing gives exactly this error.
     Checklist for every construction: tighten the compass; keep the pencil
     sharp; do not change the width in the middle of a step; draw arcs long
     enough to cross clearly; measure to check. A difference of up to 1° is
     normal; 4° means a mistake. Now you try: halves of 61° and 59° for a 120°
     angle — acceptable? (yes, 1° from 60° each)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Each angle is bisected. Write the size of each half.
    a) 10°    b) 20°    c) 30°    d) 40°    e) 50°    f) 60°    g) 70°
    h) 80°    i) 90°    j) 100°   k) 110°   l) 120°   m) 130°   n) 140°
    o) 150°   p) 160°   q) 170°   r) 180°   s) 35°    t) 45°    u) 75°
    v) 105°   w) 135°   x) 15°
    Answers: a) 5°   b) 10°   c) 15°   d) 20°   e) 25°   f) 30°   g) 35°
             h) 40°   i) 45°   j) 50°   k) 55°   l) 60°   m) 65°   n) 70°
             o) 75°   p) 80°   q) 85°   r) 90°   s) 17.5°   t) 22.5°   u) 37.5°
             v) 52.5°   w) 67.5°   x) 7.5°

M2. Which angle do you get?
    a) construct 60° with one arc step   b) bisect 60°
    c) bisect 180° (a straight angle)    d) bisect 90°
    e) two 60° arc steps                 f) bisect 30°
    g) bisect 45°                        h) bisect between 60° and 90°
    i) bisect between 90° and 120°       j) bisect between 120° and 180°
    k) bisect between 90° and 180°       l) three 60° arc steps
    m) bisect 120°                       n) bisect between 30° and 60°
    o) bisect 15°                        p) bisect between 150° and 180°
    Answers: a) 60°   b) 30°   c) 90°   d) 45°   e) 120°   f) 15°   g) 22.5°
             h) 75°   i) 105°   j) 150°   k) 135°   l) 180°   m) 60°   n) 45°
             o) 7.5°   p) 165°

M3. Plan each construction: write it as "bisect __°" or as a sum of angles you
    can already construct. (Other correct plans are possible; check that yours
    gives the same angle.)
    a) 30°     b) 90°     c) 120°    d) 45°     e) 15°     f) 75°     g) 135°
    h) 150°    i) 105°    j) 165°    k) 22.5°   l) 67.5°   m) 7.5°    n) 37.5°
    o) 180°    p) 52.5°
    Answers: a) bisect 60°   b) bisect 180°   c) 60° + 60°   d) bisect 90°
             e) bisect 30°   f) 60° + 15°   g) 90° + 45°   h) 120° + 30°
             i) 90° + 15°   j) 150° + 15°   k) bisect 45°   l) 45° + 22.5°
             m) bisect 15°   n) 30° + 7.5°   o) 60° + 60° + 60°   p) 45° + 7.5°

M4. Angle arithmetic for constructions. Work out each angle.
    a) 60° + 30°           b) 90° + 45°           c) 60° + 15°
    d) 120° + 30°          e) 90° + 15°           f) 180° − 30°
    g) 180° − 45°          h) 90° − 30°           i) 60° ÷ 2
    j) 90° ÷ 2             k) 120° ÷ 2            l) 180° ÷ 2
    m) 45° ÷ 2             n) 30° ÷ 2             o) 150° ÷ 2
    p) 135° ÷ 2            q) (60° + 90°) ÷ 2     r) (90° + 120°) ÷ 2
    s) (120° + 180°) ÷ 2   t) (30° + 60°) ÷ 2     u) (45° + 90°) ÷ 2
    v) 360° − 60°          w) 360° − 135°         x) 60° × 3
    Answers: a) 90°   b) 135°   c) 75°   d) 150°   e) 105°   f) 150°   g) 135°
             h) 60°   i) 30°   j) 45°   k) 60°   l) 90°   m) 22.5°   n) 15°
             o) 75°   p) 67.5°   q) 75°   r) 105°   s) 150°   t) 45°   u) 67.5°
             v) 300°   w) 225°   x) 180°

M5. The perpendicular bisector of AB cuts it at M. Write the length of AM. (The
    compass must be opened to MORE than this.)
    a) AB = 8 cm      b) AB = 10 cm     c) AB = 6 cm      d) AB = 12 cm
    e) AB = 7 cm      f) AB = 9 cm      g) AB = 5 cm      h) AB = 11 cm
    i) AB = 13 cm     j) AB = 7.4 cm    k) AB = 8.6 cm    l) AB = 15 cm
    m) AB = 4.2 cm    n) AB = 9.8 cm    o) AB = 6.5 cm    p) AB = 10.4 cm
    q) AB = 3 cm      r) AB = 14 cm     s) AB = 12.6 cm   t) AB = 5.5 cm
    Answers: a) 4 cm   b) 5 cm   c) 3 cm   d) 6 cm   e) 3.5 cm   f) 4.5 cm
             g) 2.5 cm   h) 5.5 cm   i) 6.5 cm   j) 3.7 cm   k) 4.3 cm
             l) 7.5 cm   m) 2.1 cm   n) 4.9 cm   o) 3.25 cm   p) 5.2 cm
             q) 1.5 cm   r) 7 cm   s) 6.3 cm   t) 2.75 cm

M6. Can a triangle be constructed with these three sides (in cm)? Write yes, or
    no with the reason (compare the two shorter sides with the longest).
    a) 5, 6, 7       b) 3, 4, 8       c) 3, 4, 5       d) 3, 4, 7
    e) 6, 6, 6       f) 2, 2, 5       g) 4, 5, 8       h) 1, 2, 3
    i) 7, 7, 13      j) 5, 5, 10      k) 10, 6, 3      l) 8, 9, 10
    m) 2, 3, 4       n) 12, 5, 6      o) 6, 8, 10      p) 9, 4, 4
    q) 4.5, 5.5, 9   r) 5.5, 3.5, 2   s) 7, 10, 16     t) 11, 11, 1
    Answers: a) yes   b) no (3 + 4 < 8)   c) yes   d) no (3 + 4 = 7)   e) yes
             f) no (2 + 2 < 5)   g) yes   h) no (1 + 2 = 3)   i) yes
             j) no (5 + 5 = 10)   k) no (3 + 6 < 10)   l) yes   m) yes
             n) no (5 + 6 < 12)   o) yes   p) no (4 + 4 < 9)   q) yes
             r) no (2 + 3.5 = 5.5)   s) yes   t) yes

M7. Start with the angle and bisect again and again (always keeping the half
    next to the first arm). What angle do you end with?
    a) 90°, bisect once       b) 90°, bisect twice      c) 90°, bisect 3 times
    d) 60°, bisect once       e) 60°, bisect twice      f) 60°, bisect 3 times
    g) 120°, bisect once      h) 120°, bisect twice     i) 120°, bisect 3 times
    j) 180°, bisect once      k) 180°, bisect twice     l) 180°, bisect 3 times
    m) 180°, bisect 4 times   n) 150°, bisect once      o) 135°, bisect once
    p) 30°, bisect twice
    Answers: a) 45°   b) 22.5°   c) 11.25°   d) 30°   e) 15°   f) 7.5°   g) 60°
             h) 30°   i) 15°   j) 90°   k) 45°   l) 22.5°   m) 11.25°   n) 75°
             o) 67.5°   p) 7.5°

M8. An angle is constructed at a point on a straight line. Find the angle on the
    other side of the new arm.
    a) 60°      b) 30°      c) 90°      d) 45°      e) 120°     f) 75°
    g) 135°     h) 150°     i) 105°     j) 15°      k) 22.5°    l) 67.5°
    m) 165°     n) 7.5°     o) 37.5°    p) 112.5°
    Answers: a) 120°   b) 150°   c) 90°   d) 135°   e) 60°   f) 105°   g) 45°
             h) 30°   i) 75°   j) 165°   k) 157.5°   l) 112.5°   m) 15°
             n) 172.5°   o) 142.5°   p) 67.5°

M9. Put the steps in the right order (write the letters).
    a) Perpendicular bisector of AB:
       K: join the two crossing points
       L: draw AB
       M: from B, with the SAME width, draw arcs crossing the first ones
       N: from A draw arcs above and below AB, width more than half AB
    b) Bisector of angle XOY:
       K: join O to the crossing point
       L: from O draw an arc cutting OX at A and OY at B
       M: from B, with the same width, draw an arc crossing it
       N: from A draw an arc inside the angle
    c) 60° at O on ray OA:
       K: join O to Q
       L: with the same width, from P cut the arc at Q
       M: from O draw an arc cutting OA at P
       N: draw the ray OA
    d) 90° at P on a line:
       K: join P to C
       L: from A and B, with a larger width, draw arcs crossing at C
       M: from P draw an arc cutting the line at A and B
       N: draw the line and mark P
    e) Triangle with sides 5 cm, 6 cm, 7 cm:
       K: from B draw an arc of 6 cm crossing it at C
       L: draw AB = 7 cm
       M: join AC and BC
       N: from A draw an arc of 5 cm
    f) Perpendicular from P to line l:
       K: from A and B draw arcs below l crossing at Q
       L: join P to Q
       M: from P draw an arc cutting l at A and B
    Answers: a) L, N, M, K   b) L, N, M, K   c) N, M, L, K   d) N, M, L, K
             e) L, N, K, M   f) M, K, L

M10. In a) to l) an equilateral triangle is constructed with the side given:
     write its perimeter. In m) to t) the 60° arc step is used all round a
     circle of the radius given to make a regular hexagon: write the side and
     the perimeter of the hexagon.
     a) side 3 cm       b) side 4 cm       c) side 5 cm       d) side 6 cm
     e) side 7 cm       f) side 8 cm       g) side 2.5 cm     h) side 4.5 cm
     i) side 5.5 cm     j) side 6.5 cm     k) side 10 cm      l) side 12 cm
     m) radius 2 cm     n) radius 3 cm     o) radius 4 cm     p) radius 5 cm
     q) radius 6 cm     r) radius 2.5 cm   s) radius 3.5 cm   t) radius 7 cm
     Answers: a) 9 cm   b) 12 cm   c) 15 cm   d) 18 cm   e) 21 cm   f) 24 cm
              g) 7.5 cm   h) 13.5 cm   i) 16.5 cm   j) 19.5 cm   k) 30 cm
              l) 36 cm   m) 2 cm, 12 cm   n) 3 cm, 18 cm   o) 4 cm, 24 cm
              p) 5 cm, 30 cm   q) 6 cm, 36 cm   r) 2.5 cm, 15 cm
              s) 3.5 cm, 21 cm   t) 7 cm, 42 cm

M11. Can the angle be made using ONLY the methods of this topic (60° arc steps,
     bisecting, adding and subtracting)? Write yes or no.
     a) 15°     b) 20°     c) 25°     d) 30°     e) 35°     f) 40°     g) 45°
     h) 50°     i) 52.5°   j) 60°     k) 70°     l) 75°     m) 80°     n) 105°
     o) 110°    p) 135°
     Answers: a) yes   b) no   c) no   d) yes   e) no   f) no   g) yes   h) no
              i) yes   j) yes   k) no   l) yes   m) no   n) yes   o) no   p) yes

M12. True or false?
     a) A construction uses only a ruler, a compass and a pencil.
     b) For a perpendicular bisector the compass width must be less than half of
        AB.
     c) The perpendicular bisector cuts AB into two equal parts at 90°.
     d) Every point on the perpendicular bisector of AB is the same distance
        from A and B.
     e) The 60° construction makes an equilateral triangle.
     f) Bisecting 60° gives 20°.
     g) Bisecting a straight angle gives 90°.
     h) 45° is made by bisecting 90°.
     i) 75° can be made as 60° + 15°.
     j) The width of the compass may change in the middle of a step.
     k) Sides 2 cm, 3 cm and 6 cm make a triangle.
     l) The perpendicular from a point to a line is the shortest path to the
        line.
     m) Six 60° arc steps go once round the circle.
     n) Arcs should be rubbed out after a construction.
     o) Copying an angle uses the distance between the two cutting points.
     p) Halves of 44° and 46° for a 90° bisection are acceptable.
     Answers: a) true   b) false (more than half)   c) true   d) true   e) true
              f) false (30°)   g) true   h) true   i) true   j) false
              k) false (2 + 3 < 6)   l) true   m) true
              n) false (they show the working)   o) true
              p) false (should be 45° each; 1° off is the most allowed)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: practise just drawing circles and arcs first (a compass with a
  screw that holds its width helps); do each construction slowly from a printed
  step card and tick the steps off; use large drawings (arms of 8 to 10 cm);
  after each construction check with the protractor so the child sees that it
  really works. Harder: construct a triangle and bisect all three angles — the
  bisectors meet at one point, the centre of a circle that touches all three
  sides; draw the perpendicular bisectors of all three sides — they meet at the
  centre of a circle through all three corners; construct a square of side 5 cm
  and a regular hexagon using only ruler and compass; find out why 20° cannot be
  made with ruler and compass alone.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.24 — CIRCLE: CENTRE, RADIUS, DIAMETER, CHORD
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - A CIRCLE is the set of all points at the same distance from a fixed point,
    the CENTRE. We draw it with a compass: the point stays on the centre and the
    pencil goes round at a fixed width.
  - RADIUS (plural radii): a segment from the centre to the circle, and its
    length r. All radii of a circle are equal. DIAMETER: a segment through the
    centre from one side of the circle to the other; d = 2 × r and r = d ÷ 2.
  - CHORD: any segment joining two points of the circle. The diameter is the
    LONGEST chord, because it passes through the centre.
  - ARC: a part of the circle between two points. A diameter cuts the circle
    into two SEMICIRCLES. SECTOR: the "pizza slice" between two radii and an
    arc. SEGMENT: the region between a chord and its arc.
  - CIRCUMFERENCE: the distance all the way round the circle (its perimeter).
    For EVERY circle, circumference ÷ diameter is the same number, called π
    ("pi"), a little more than 3: π ≈ 3.14 or 22/7. So C = π × d = 2 × π × r.
  - A full turn at the centre is 360°: half a circle is 180°, a quarter 90°, and
    n equal sectors each have an angle of 360° ÷ n.
  - Units: r, d and C are lengths (cm, m). Use 22/7 when r or d is a multiple of
    7, and 3.14 otherwise.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Look at the circle. Name the point O, and the segments OA, BC and DE. (BC
    passes through O; D and E are two other points on the circle.)
                     * * *
                 *           *
              D                 *
             *   \                *
            *      \               *
            B--------\---O---------C
            *          \    \      *
             *          \     \   *
              *          E       A
                 *           *
                     * * *
    Answer: O is the centre. OA is a radius. BC is a diameter (a chord through
    the centre). DE is a chord.
    Teacher explains: Go round the words slowly and let the child point. CENTRE:
    the middle point, where the compass point stands. RADIUS: from the centre to
    the circle — OA, and also OB and OC (each half of the diameter). DIAMETER:
    goes right across THROUGH the centre — BC. CHORD: any straight line from the
    circle to the circle — DE; a diameter is a special chord.
    Common mistake: calling any line across the circle a diameter. Ask "does it
    pass through the centre?" Now you try: in this circle, how many radii are
    drawn? (3: OA, OB and OC — OB and OC are the halves of BC)

D2. A bangle has a radius of 3 cm. What is its diameter? Why is the diameter
    always twice the radius?
    Answer: 6 cm. d = 2 × r = 2 × 3 cm = 6 cm.
    Teacher explains: A diameter goes from the circle to the centre (one radius)
    and then on from the centre to the other side (another radius). So it is
    made of two radii: d = r + r = 2 × r. Put a ruler across the bangle through
    its middle: it measures 6 cm.
    Going back: r = d ÷ 2. A 1 rupee coin with diameter 2.4 cm has radius
    2.4 ÷ 2 = 1.2 cm. Common mistake: doubling when you should halve. Say:
    "diameter is the BIG one". Now you try: radius 4.5 cm; find the diameter.
    (9 cm)

D3. A large pizza has a diameter of 30 cm. How far is it from the centre of the
    pizza to its edge?
    Answer: 15 cm. The distance from the centre to the edge is the radius, half
    the diameter: 30 cm ÷ 2 = 15 cm.
    Teacher explains: "From the centre to the edge" is the definition of the
    RADIUS. The diameter (30 cm) is two radii, so each radius is 30 ÷ 2 = 15 cm.
    Every slice cut from the centre has straight edges 15 cm long.
    For an odd diameter the radius is a decimal: d = 25 cm gives r = 12.5 cm.
    Now you try: a round table has a diameter of 1.5 m; find its radius.
    (0.75 m)

D4. Draw a circle of radius 4 cm with a compass. Describe the steps.
    Answer: Open the compass to 4 cm on a ruler, put the point on the centre O
    and turn the pencil once round without changing the width.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: mark the centre O with a dot. Step 2: put the
    compass point on the 0 of the ruler and open it until the pencil tip is on
    4 cm. Step 3: place the point on O, hold the compass by the top handle, and
    turn it steadily all the way round. Step 4: label O and draw one radius to
    show r = 4 cm.
    Common mistakes: opening the compass to 8 cm (the diameter) when the RADIUS
    is asked; pressing on the pencil leg so the width changes; starting at 1 on
    the ruler. If a DIAMETER of 10 cm is asked, open the compass to
    10 ÷ 2 = 5 cm. Now you try: to draw a circle of diameter 7 cm, open the
    compass to __. (3.5 cm)

D5. In a circle of radius 5 cm, Riya draws chords of 4 cm, 7 cm and 10 cm. Can
    she draw a chord of 12 cm? Explain.
    Answer: No. The longest chord is the diameter, 2 × 5 cm = 10 cm, so no chord
    can be longer than 10 cm.
    Teacher explains: Draw chords that move closer and closer to the centre:
    they get longer and longer. The longest one passes through the centre — the
    diameter, 10 cm. A chord of 12 cm cannot fit. Chords close to the edge are
    short; a chord can be as short as we like.
    Why the diameter is longest: for a chord AB, go from A to the centre and on
    to B: that path is r + r = d. A straight line is the shortest path, so AB is
    at most d, and equal to d only when the centre is ON AB. Now you try: in a
    circle of diameter 9 cm, can there be a chord of 9.5 cm? (no)

D6. Amma cuts a round roti with two straight cuts from the centre, and Papa cuts
    a piece off the edge with one straight cut. What shapes are the pieces
    called?
    Answer: Amma's piece (two radii and an arc) is a SECTOR. Papa's piece
    (between a chord and the arc) is a SEGMENT.
    Teacher explains: Draw both pieces on the board.
         sector: two radii + arc        segment: chord + arc
                * * *                        * * *
              *   |   *                   *         *
             *    |    *                 *           *
             *    O----*                 *           *
             *         *                 *-----------*
              *       *                   *.........*
                * * *                        * * *
    A SECTOR always has its corner at the centre: slices of a pizza or of a pie
    chart are sectors. Half a circle (cut along a diameter) is a SEMICIRCLE; it
    is both a sector (angle 180°) and a segment. A quarter circle is a sector
    with angle 90°. A SEGMENT has one straight edge, a chord, which does not
    have to pass through the centre. The curved edge of both is an ARC.
    Common mistake: calling the segment of a circle a "sector". Remember "SECtor
    has a CEntre". Now you try: a slice that is 1/4 of the roti, cut from the
    centre, is a __ with angle __. (sector, 90°)

D7. Class 6 wrapped a thread round circular objects and measured the diameters.
    A bangle: C = 22 cm, d = 7 cm. A plate: C = 66 cm, d = 21 cm. A bucket:
    C = 88 cm, d = 28 cm. Divide C by d each time. What do you notice?
    Answer: Each time C ÷ d = 22/7, which is about 3.14. The circumference is
    always a little more than 3 times the diameter. This number is called π.
    Teacher explains: 22 ÷ 7 = 22/7, 66 ÷ 21 = 22/7 and 88 ÷ 28 = 22/7. As
    decimals, 22/7 is about 3.14. For EVERY circle, small or big, C ÷ d is the
    same number, π (the Greek letter "pi"). Real measurements with a thread give
    3.1 or 3.2 — small errors — but π is about 3.14159..., and its digits never
    end or repeat.
    So C = π × d. Think of it as: "go round a circle and you travel a bit more
    than three diameters". A quick check: a wheel of diameter 1 m goes about 3 m
    in one turn. Now you try: measured C = 31.4 cm and d = 10 cm. What is C ÷ d?
    (3.14)

D8. A bicycle wheel has a diameter of 70 cm. How far does the bicycle go when
    the wheel turns once? And when it turns 100 times? (Use π = 22/7.)
    Answer: One turn: C = 22/7 × 70 cm = 220 cm. 100 turns: 22,000 cm = 220 m.
    Teacher explains: In one turn every point of the tyre touches the road once,
    so the bicycle moves forward one circumference. C = π × d = 22/7 × 70.
    Divide first: 70 ÷ 7 = 10, then 22 × 10 = 220. So 220 cm in one turn. In 100
    turns: 220 × 100 = 22,000 cm. Since 100 cm = 1 m, 22,000 cm = 220 m.
    Tip: choose π = 22/7 when the diameter or radius is a multiple of 7 — the 7
    cancels and the working is easy. Common mistake: using the radius in
    C = π × d. Now you try: a wheel of diameter 56 cm; how far in one turn?
    (176 cm)

D9. A circular park has a radius of 21 m. How long is the fence round it?
    Fencing costs ₹50 per metre. What is the total cost? (Use π = 22/7.)
    Answer: Fence: 132 m. Cost: 132 × ₹50 = ₹6600.
    Teacher explains: The fence goes all the way round: the circumference. With
    the radius, C = 2 × π × r = 2 × 22/7 × 21. Cancel: 21 ÷ 7 = 3, so
    C = 2 × 22 × 3 = 132 m. Or find the diameter first: d = 2 × 21 = 42 m, and
    C = 22/7 × 42 = 132 m. Cost: 132 × 50 = 6600 rupees.
    Common mistake: forgetting to double the radius (answer 66 m, half the
    fence). Check: the fence should be a little more than 3 × 42 = 126 m. Now
    you try: a round pond of radius 14 m; find the length of the path round it.
    (88 m)

D10. A circle fits exactly inside a square of side 10 cm, touching all four
     sides. What is its diameter and radius? What if 3 such circles sit side by
     side in a row inside a rectangle?
     Answer: Diameter 10 cm, radius 5 cm. The rectangle for 3 circles is
     3 × 10 cm = 30 cm long and 10 cm wide.
     Teacher explains: Draw the square, then the row of three.
         +----------+----------+----------+
         |  * * *   |  * * *   |  * * *   |
         | *     *  | *     *  | *     *  |
         | *  O  *  | *  O  *  | *  O  *  |
         | *     *  | *     *  | *     *  |
         |  * * *   |  * * *   |  * * *   |
         +----------+----------+----------+
     The circle touches the left and right sides, so its width — the diameter —
     equals the side of the square, 10 cm. Radius: 10 ÷ 2 = 5 cm. In a row, each
     circle takes up one diameter of length, so 3 circles take 3 × 10 = 30 cm.
     The centres of two touching circles are one diameter (two radii) apart:
     5 + 5 = 10 cm.
     Think of tennis balls in a box or coins in a row. Now you try: 5 coins of
     radius 1.2 cm in a row touching each other. How long is the row?
     (5 × 2.4 = 12 cm)

D11. A wheel has a circumference of 3.14 m. What is its diameter? Explain why
     this is quick.
     Answer: 1 m. d = C ÷ π = 3.14 ÷ 3.14 = 1 m.
     Teacher explains: C = π × d, so d = C ÷ π — just like
     "area = length × breadth, so length = area ÷ breadth". With π = 3.14, a
     circumference of 3.14 m gives d = 3.14 ÷ 3.14 = 1 m. For 31.4 cm,
     d = 10 cm; for 6.28 m, d = 2 m.
     Common mistake: dividing by 2 instead of by π. Check: the circumference
     should be a little more than 3 times the answer. Now you try: C = 15.7 cm;
     find d with π = 3.14. (5 cm)

D12. The trunk of an old banyan tree has a circumference of 88 cm. Find its
     diameter and radius. (Use π = 22/7.)
     Answer: d = 28 cm and r = 14 cm.
     Teacher explains: Work backwards: C = π × d, so d = C ÷ π = 88 ÷ 22/7.
     Dividing by 22/7 is the same as multiplying by 7/22:
     88 × 7/22 = (88 ÷ 22) × 7 = 4 × 7 = 28 cm. Then r = 28 ÷ 2 = 14 cm. Check
     forwards: 22/7 × 28 = 22 × 4 = 88.
     In the field: wrap a measuring tape round the trunk (easy), then find the
     diameter without cutting the tree (impossible to measure directly). This is
     how foresters do it. Now you try: C = 44 cm, π = 22/7. Find r. (d = 14 cm,
     r = 7 cm)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. The radius is given (in cm). Write the diameter.
    a) r = 3      b) r = 5      c) r = 7      d) r = 10     e) r = 1
    f) r = 12     g) r = 25     h) r = 8      i) r = 15     j) r = 9
    k) r = 50     l) r = 11     m) r = 2.5    n) r = 4.5    o) r = 0.5
    p) r = 1.2    q) r = 3.5    r) r = 6.25   s) r = 0.8    t) r = 7.5
    u) r = 14     v) r = 21     w) r = 100    x) r = 0.25
    Answers: a) d = 6   b) d = 10   c) d = 14   d) d = 20   e) d = 2   f) d = 24
             g) d = 50   h) d = 16   i) d = 30   j) d = 18   k) d = 100
             l) d = 22   m) d = 5   n) d = 9   o) d = 1   p) d = 2.4   q) d = 7
             r) d = 12.5   s) d = 1.6   t) d = 15   u) d = 28   v) d = 42
             w) d = 200   x) d = 0.5

M2. The diameter is given (in cm). Write the radius.
    a) d = 10     b) d = 8      c) d = 14     d) d = 6      e) d = 20
    f) d = 2      g) d = 30     h) d = 100    i) d = 5      j) d = 9

    p) d = 11     q) d = 2.4    r) d = 4.8    s) d = 0.6    t) d = 12.6
    u) d = 8.4    v) d = 25     w) d = 1.5    x) d = 0.5
    Answers: a) r = 5   b) r = 4   c) r = 7   d) r = 3   e) r = 10   f) r = 1
             g) r = 15   h) r = 50   i) r = 2.5   j) r = 4.5   k) r = 6.5
             l) r = 0.5   m) r = 7.5   n) r = 3.5   o) r = 1.5   p) r = 5.5
             q) r = 1.2   r) r = 2.4   s) r = 0.3   t) r = 6.3   u) r = 4.2
             v) r = 12.5   w) r = 0.75   x) r = 0.25

M3. Name the part of the circle.
    a) the fixed middle point of a circle
    b) a segment from the centre to the circle
    c) a chord through the centre
    d) a segment joining two points of the circle
    e) a part of the circle between two points
    f) the distance all round the circle
    g) half of a circle, cut along a diameter
    h) region between two radii and an arc
    i) region between a chord and its arc
    j) the longest chord
    k) circumference ÷ diameter
    l) the plural of radius
    m) a slice of pizza cut from the centre
    n) the rim of a bangle
    o) the spoke of a bicycle wheel (hub to rim)
    p) the piece cut off a roti by one straight cut
    Answers: a) centre   b) radius   c) diameter   d) chord   e) arc
             f) circumference   g) semicircle   h) sector   i) segment
             j) diameter   k) π (pi)   l) radii   m) sector   n) circumference
             o) radius   p) segment

M4. In a) to l) the radius is given (in cm): write the length of the longest
    chord. In m) to x): can a circle of the given radius have a chord of the
    given length? Write yes or no.
    a) r = 4               b) r = 7               c) r = 2.5
    d) r = 10              e) r = 1.5             f) r = 6
    g) r = 12              h) r = 0.5             i) r = 3.2
    j) r = 9               k) r = 15              l) r = 20
    m) r = 5, chord 8      n) r = 5, chord 10     o) r = 5, chord 11
    p) r = 3, chord 5.5    q) r = 3, chord 6.5    r) r = 7, chord 14
    s) r = 7, chord 14.1   t) r = 2.5, chord 4    u) r = 2.5, chord 6
    v) r = 10, chord 19    w) r = 10, chord 21    x) r = 1, chord 2
    Answers: a) 8 cm   b) 14 cm   c) 5 cm   d) 20 cm   e) 3 cm   f) 12 cm
             g) 24 cm   h) 1 cm   i) 6.4 cm   j) 18 cm   k) 30 cm   l) 40 cm
             m) yes   n) yes   o) no   p) yes   q) no   r) yes   s) no   t) yes
             u) no   v) yes   w) no   x) yes

M5. Find the circumference C = π × d, using π ≈ 3.14. The diameter is given in
    cm.
    a) d = 1     b) d = 2     c) d = 3     d) d = 4     e) d = 5     f) d = 10
    g) d = 20    h) d = 100   i) d = 6     j) d = 8     k) d = 50    l) d = 15
    m) d = 0.5   n) d = 2.5   o) d = 12    p) d = 7
    Answers: a) 3.14 cm   b) 6.28 cm   c) 9.42 cm   d) 12.56 cm   e) 15.7 cm
             f) 31.4 cm   g) 62.8 cm   h) 314 cm   i) 18.84 cm   j) 25.12 cm
             k) 157 cm   l) 47.1 cm   m) 1.57 cm   n) 7.85 cm   o) 37.68 cm
             p) 21.98 cm

M6. Find the circumference C = 2 × π × r, using π ≈ 22/7. The radius is given in
    cm.
    a) r = 7      b) r = 14     c) r = 21     d) r = 28     e) r = 35
    f) r = 3.5    g) r = 42     h) r = 70     i) r = 49     j) r = 56
    k) r = 0.7    l) r = 10.5   m) r = 63     n) r = 1.4    o) r = 17.5
    p) r = 140
    Answers: a) 44 cm   b) 88 cm   c) 132 cm   d) 176 cm   e) 220 cm   f) 22 cm
             g) 264 cm   h) 440 cm   i) 308 cm   j) 352 cm   k) 4.4 cm
             l) 66 cm   m) 396 cm   n) 8.8 cm   o) 110 cm   p) 880 cm

M7. The circumference is given (in cm) with the value of π to use. Find the
    diameter and the radius.
    a) C = 22, π = 22/7     b) C = 44, π = 22/7     c) C = 66, π = 22/7
    d) C = 88, π = 22/7     e) C = 132, π = 22/7    f) C = 220, π = 22/7
    g) C = 31.4, π = 3.14   h) C = 62.8, π = 3.14   i) C = 314, π = 3.14
    j) C = 9.42, π = 3.14   k) C = 15.7, π = 3.14   l) C = 11, π = 22/7
    Answers: a) d = 7, r = 3.5   b) d = 14, r = 7   c) d = 21, r = 10.5
             d) d = 28, r = 14   e) d = 42, r = 21   f) d = 70, r = 35
             g) d = 10, r = 5   h) d = 20, r = 10   i) d = 100, r = 50
             j) d = 3, r = 1.5   k) d = 5, r = 2.5   l) d = 3.5, r = 1.75

M8. In a) to l) a circle of the given DIAMETER is to be drawn: to what width
    must the compass be opened? In m) to t) a circle fits exactly inside a
    square of the given side: write its radius. (All in cm.)
    a) d = 10     b) d = 8      c) d = 12     d) d = 7      e) d = 15
    f) d = 9      g) d = 4.6    h) d = 20     i) d = 13     j) d = 6.4
    k) d = 3      l) d = 25     m) side 6     n) side 10    o) side 8.4
    p) side 15    q) side 3     r) side 7     s) side 4.4   t) side 18
    Answers: a) 5 cm   b) 4 cm   c) 6 cm   d) 3.5 cm   e) 7.5 cm   f) 4.5 cm
             g) 2.3 cm   h) 10 cm   i) 6.5 cm   j) 3.2 cm   k) 1.5 cm
             l) 12.5 cm   m) 3 cm   n) 5 cm   o) 4.2 cm   p) 7.5 cm   q) 1.5 cm
             r) 3.5 cm   s) 2.2 cm   t) 9 cm

M9. Angles at the centre (a full turn is 360°). In a) to l) find the angle of a
    sector that is the given fraction of the circle. In m) to x) a circle is cut
    into that many equal sectors: find the angle of each.
    a) 1/2          b) 1/4          c) 3/4          d) 1/3
    e) 2/3          f) 1/6          g) 5/6          h) 1/8
    i) 3/8          j) 1/12         k) 5/12         l) 1/10
    m) 2 sectors    n) 3 sectors    o) 4 sectors    p) 5 sectors
    q) 6 sectors    r) 8 sectors    s) 9 sectors    t) 10 sectors
    u) 12 sectors   v) 18 sectors   w) 20 sectors   x) 24 sectors
    Answers: a) 180°   b) 90°   c) 270°   d) 120°   e) 240°   f) 60°   g) 300°
             h) 45°   i) 135°   j) 30°   k) 150°   l) 36°   m) 180°   n) 120°
             o) 90°   p) 72°   q) 60°   r) 45°   s) 40°   t) 36°   u) 30°
             v) 20°   w) 18°   x) 15°

M10. Equal circles of the given radius (cm) sit in a straight row, each touching
     the next. How long is the row?
     a) 3 circles, r = 2      b) 5 circles, r = 1      c) 4 circles, r = 3
     d) 6 circles, r = 2.5    e) 2 circles, r = 7      f) 10 circles, r = 1.2
     g) 8 circles, r = 0.5    h) 3 circles, r = 4.5    i) 7 circles, r = 3
     j) 12 circles, r = 1     k) 4 circles, r = 10     l) 5 circles, r = 1.5
     m) 9 circles, r = 2      n) 6 circles, r = 0.75   o) 20 circles, r = 1
     p) 2 circles, r = 12.5
     Answers: a) 12 cm   b) 10 cm   c) 24 cm   d) 30 cm   e) 28 cm   f) 24 cm
              g) 8 cm   h) 27 cm   i) 42 cm   j) 24 cm   k) 80 cm   l) 15 cm
              m) 36 cm   n) 9 cm   o) 40 cm   p) 50 cm

M11. In a) to j) two circles have the same centre (a ring). The two radii are
     given (cm): find the width of the ring. In k) to r) two circles with the
     given radii touch from outside: find the distance between their centres.
     a) R = 10, r = 7      b) R = 8, r = 5       c) R = 6, r = 2
     d) R = 12, r = 11.5   e) R = 5, r = 4.5     f) R = 20, r = 15
     g) R = 9, r = 6       h) R = 7.5, r = 2.5   i) R = 15, r = 14
     j) R = 3, r = 1       k) 3 and 2            l) 5 and 5
     m) 4 and 1.5          n) 7 and 3            o) 2.5 and 2.5
     p) 10 and 6           q) 1.2 and 0.8        r) 6 and 9
     Answers: a) 3 cm   b) 3 cm   c) 4 cm   d) 0.5 cm   e) 0.5 cm   f) 5 cm
              g) 3 cm   h) 5 cm   i) 1 cm   j) 2 cm   k) 5 cm   l) 10 cm
              m) 5.5 cm   n) 10 cm   o) 5 cm   p) 16 cm   q) 2 cm   r) 15 cm

M12. True or false?
     a) All radii of a circle are equal.
     b) The diameter is half the radius.
     c) Every diameter is a chord.
     d) Every chord is a diameter.
     e) The diameter is the longest chord.
     f) A circle has exactly two diameters.
     g) A sector is bounded by two radii and an arc.
     h) A segment is bounded by two radii and an arc.
     i) A semicircle is half a circle, cut along a diameter.
     j) C ÷ d is the same number for every circle.
     k) π is exactly 3.14.
     l) The circumference is a little more than 3 diameters.
     m) C = π × r.
     n) To draw a circle of diameter 8 cm, open the compass to 8 cm.
     o) A quarter circle is a sector with angle 90°.
     p) A circle of radius 5 cm can have a chord of 11 cm.
     Answers: a) true   b) false (twice the radius)   c) true   d) false
              e) true   f) false (as many as we like)   g) true
              h) false (a chord and an arc)   i) true   j) true (π)
              k) false (about 3.14)   l) true   m) false (C = π × d = 2 × π × r)
              n) false (4 cm)   o) true   p) false (longest is 10 cm)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: draw many circles with a compass and label the centre, one radius
  and one diameter each time; use real objects (bangles, coins, plates) and a
  ruler across the middle; fold a paper circle in half twice to find the centre
  and see that the fold lines are diameters; practise "diameter is double,
  radius is half" with quick oral drills before any π work. Harder: find the
  perimeter of a semicircle (half the circumference plus the diameter: for
  d = 14 cm, 22 + 14 = 36 cm); find how many turns a wheel of diameter 70 cm
  makes in 1.1 km (1,100 m ÷ 2.2 m = 500 turns); find the centre of a circle by
  drawing the perpendicular bisectors of two chords (topic 5.23); read about how
  Aryabhata gave π ≈ 3.1416 more than 1,500 years ago.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.25 — AREA OF TRIANGLES, PARALLELOGRAMS, TRAPEZOIDS
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - AREA is the amount of flat surface, counted in unit squares: cm² (square
    centimetres), m² (square metres). A rectangle has area length × breadth
    (topic 4.29).
  - TRIANGLE: area = 1/2 × base × height. The HEIGHT is the PERPENDICULAR
    distance from the opposite corner to the line of the base — not a slanting
    side. Any side can be the base, as long as the height goes with it.
  - Why a half: a right-angled triangle is half of a rectangle (cut along the
    diagonal); any triangle fits in a rectangle of the same base and height and
    fills exactly half of it. For an OBTUSE triangle the height may fall OUTSIDE
    the triangle: extend the base with a dotted line.
  - PARALLELOGRAM: area = base × height. Cut a right-angled triangle off one end
    and move it to the other end: it becomes a rectangle. The slanting side is
    NOT used.
  - TRAPEZIUM (trapezoid): area = 1/2 × (a + b) × h, where a and b are the two
    PARALLEL sides and h is the distance between them. Two copies make a
    parallelogram with base a + b.
  - Missing length: base = 2 × area ÷ height for a triangle;
    base = area ÷ height for a parallelogram; a + b = 2 × area ÷ h for a
    trapezium.
  - Composite shapes: split into rectangles, triangles and trapeziums, find each
    area and add (or subtract a hole). Units: 1 cm² = 100 mm²,
    1 m² = 100 × 100 = 10,000 cm².

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. A rectangular garden bed is 8 m long and 6 m wide. A path is laid along one
    diagonal, cutting the bed into two triangles. What is the area of each
    triangle?
    Answer: 24 m². The rectangle is 8 m × 6 m = 48 m², and each triangle is
    half: 48 ÷ 2 = 24 m².
    Teacher explains: Draw it:
        +----------------+
        | \              |
        |    \           |  6 m
        |        \       |
        |            \   |
        +----------------+
               8 m
    The diagonal cuts the rectangle into two right-angled triangles that are
    exactly the same (turn one round and it fits on the other). So each has half
    the area: 1/2 × 8 × 6 = 24. The two shorter sides of a right-angled triangle
    are a base and its height, because they meet at 90°. Common mistake:
    forgetting to halve (48 m²). Now you try: a right-angled triangle with
    shorter sides 10 cm and 7 cm. (35 cm²)

D2. Explain why the area of ANY triangle is 1/2 × base × height. Use a triangle
    with base 10 cm and height 6 cm.
    Answer: Area = 1/2 × 10 × 6 = 30 cm². The triangle fills exactly half of the
    rectangle 10 cm by 6 cm around it.
    Teacher explains: Draw the rectangle round the triangle, and drop the height
    from the top corner. The height splits the rectangle into two smaller
    rectangles, and each is cut in half by a side of the triangle.
        +------+----------+
        |    / |  \       |
        |  /   |     \    |  6 cm
        |/     |        \ |
        +------+----------+
              10 cm
    In each small rectangle one half is inside the triangle and the other half
    is outside. So the triangle is half of the big rectangle:
    1/2 × 10 × 6 = 30 cm². Written as a formula: A = 1/2 × b × h. Now you try:
    base 12 cm, height 5 cm. (30 cm²)

D3. A triangle has an obtuse angle. Its base is 6 cm, and the height from the
    top corner falls OUTSIDE the triangle, 4 cm above the extended base. Find
    its area.
    Answer: 12 cm². Area = 1/2 × 6 × 4 = 12 cm². The formula still works.
    Teacher explains: Draw the triangle and the dotted extension of the base.
                            C
                         / /|
                      /   / |
                   /     /  | 4 cm
                /       /   |
            A ---------B . .+ D
            |<- 6 cm ->|
    For an obtuse triangle ABC, extend the base AB with a dotted line and drop
    the perpendicular from C to meet it at D. The height is CD = 4 cm, even
    though D is outside the triangle. Why it still works: the big right-angled
    triangle ACD minus the small right-angled triangle BCD leaves ABC; both have
    height 4, and their bases differ by exactly AB = 6. So the area is
    1/2 × 6 × 4 = 12 cm².
    Common mistake: using the slanting side BC as the height. The height is
    ALWAYS at 90° to the base line. Now you try: an obtuse triangle with base
    9 cm and outside height 4 cm. (18 cm²)

D4. A parallelogram-shaped tile has a base of 12 cm, a slanting side of 7 cm and
    a height of 5 cm. Find its area.
    Answer: 60 cm². Area = base × height = 12 × 5 = 60 cm². The slanting 7 cm is
    not used.
    Teacher explains: Draw the parallelogram with its height.
             +-------------------+
            /|                  /
           / | 5 cm            / 7 cm
          /  |                /
         +---+---------------+
                 12 cm
    Cut off the right-angled triangle at the left end (along the height) and
    slide it to the right end. It fits exactly, and the shape becomes a
    RECTANGLE 12 cm long and 5 cm high: 12 × 5 = 60 cm². Nothing was added or
    removed, so the parallelogram has the same area.
    Common mistake: 12 × 7 = 84 (using the slant). Leaning a parallelogram more
    makes its slant side longer, but the area only depends on base and height.
    Now you try: base 9 m, height 4 m, slant 6 m. (36 m²)

D5. A trapezium-shaped plot has parallel sides of 8 m and 12 m, 5 m apart. Find
    its area and explain the formula.
    Answer: 50 m². Area = 1/2 × (8 + 12) × 5 = 1/2 × 20 × 5 = 50 m².
    Teacher explains: Take two copies of the trapezium and turn one upside down
    next to the other. Together they make a PARALLELOGRAM with base
    8 + 12 = 20 m and height 5 m, area 20 × 5 = 100 m². One trapezium is half:
    100 ÷ 2 = 50 m².
          8       12
        +----+-----------+
       /      \         /
      /        \       /
     +-----------+----+
          12       8
    So area = 1/2 × (sum of the parallel sides) × height. Another way to think
    of it: the AVERAGE width is (8 + 12) ÷ 2 = 10 m, and 10 × 5 = 50 m². Common
    mistake: using a slanting side as the height. Now you try: parallel sides
    6 cm and 10 cm, height 4 cm. (32 cm²)

D6. A triangular flag has an area of 36 cm² and a base of 9 cm. What is its
    height?
    Answer: 8 cm. 1/2 × 9 × h = 36, so 9 × h = 72 and h = 72 ÷ 9 = 8 cm.
    Teacher explains: Write the formula and put in what you know:
    1/2 × 9 × h = 36. Undo the half first: the full rectangle would be
    2 × 36 = 72 cm², so 9 × h = 72. Now divide: h = 72 ÷ 9 = 8. Check:
    1/2 × 9 × 8 = 36.
    Shortcut: height = 2 × area ÷ base. Common mistake: forgetting to double
    (36 ÷ 9 = 4, which gives an area of only 18 cm²). Now you try: area 45 cm²,
    height 10 cm; find the base. (9 cm)

D7. A parallelogram has an area of 84 cm² and a height of 7 cm. Find its base.
    Answer: 12 cm. base = area ÷ height = 84 ÷ 7 = 12 cm.
    Teacher explains: Area = base × height, so the base times 7 makes 84. Which
    number times 7 is 84? 84 ÷ 7 = 12. Check: 12 × 7 = 84. There is NO half here
    — a parallelogram is a whole rectangle after the cut-and-move.
    Compare: a TRIANGLE with area 84 cm² and height 7 cm would have base
    2 × 84 ÷ 7 = 24 cm. Now you try: a parallelogram of area 150 m² and base
    25 m; find the height. (6 m)

D8. The front wall of a house is a rectangle 8 m wide and 5 m high, with a
    triangular roof on top: base 8 m and height 3 m. What is the total area to
    be painted?
    Answer: 52 m². Rectangle 8 × 5 = 40 m², triangle 1/2 × 8 × 3 = 12 m², total
    40 + 12 = 52 m².
    Teacher explains: Draw the wall and the roof.
              /\
            /    \       3 m
          /        \
         +----------+
         |          |
         |          |    5 m
         |          |
         +----------+
             8 m
    Split a COMPOSITE shape into shapes you know. Here: one rectangle and one
    triangle. Find each area separately, write it next to the piece, then add.
    If there is a door 1 m by 2 m that is not painted, SUBTRACT it:
    52 − 2 = 50 m².
    Common mistake: using the total height 8 m for the triangle. The triangle's
    height is only the roof part, 3 m. Now you try: a rectangle 10 m × 4 m with
    a triangle of base 10 m and height 2 m on top. (40 + 10 = 50 m²)

D9. A school field is a trapezium with parallel sides 30 m and 50 m, and the
    distance between them is 20 m. Grass costs ₹12 per m². What is the cost of
    grassing the field?
    Answer: Area = 1/2 × (30 + 50) × 20 = 800 m². Cost = 800 × ₹12 = ₹9600.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: the area. Add the parallel sides: 30 + 50 = 80.
    Half of 80 is 40. Times the height: 40 × 20 = 800 m². Step 2: the cost. Each
    square metre costs 12 rupees, so 800 × 12 = 9600 rupees.
    Check the size: the field is between a 30 × 20 = 600 m² rectangle and a
    50 × 20 = 1000 m² rectangle, and 800 is exactly in the middle. Common
    mistake: multiplying all three numbers without halving (1600 m²). Now you
    try: parallel sides 15 m and 25 m, height 10 m, cost ₹20 per m². (200 m²,
    ₹4000)

D10. Triangles ABP, ABQ and ABR all have the same base AB = 6 cm, and their top
     corners P, Q and R all lie on a line parallel to AB, 4 cm away. Compare
     their areas.
     Answer: They all have the same area: 1/2 × 6 × 4 = 12 cm², even though they
     look very different.
     Teacher explains: Draw the base and the parallel line.
           P         Q                R
         ---------------------------------   (parallel to AB)


               A --------- B
     The area depends only on the base and the HEIGHT. All three corners are
     4 cm above the base line, so every triangle has height 4 cm. A long,
     leaning triangle and a neat one have the same area. The same is true for
     parallelograms on the same base between the same parallel lines — and a
     triangle is half of the parallelogram with the same base and height.
     Try it with a rubber band on a geoboard: keep A and B fixed and move the
     top corner along a row of pins. Count the squares each time. Now you try: a
     parallelogram and a triangle both have base 10 cm and height 6 cm. Find
     both areas. (60 cm² and 30 cm²)

D11. A floor 2 m by 3 m is to be covered with square tiles of side 25 cm. How
     many tiles are needed?
     Answer: 96 tiles. The floor is 200 cm × 300 cm = 60,000 cm², each tile is
     25 × 25 = 625 cm², and 60,000 ÷ 625 = 96.
     Teacher explains: Always use the SAME units before dividing areas.
     2 m = 200 cm and 3 m = 300 cm, so the floor is 200 × 300 = 60,000 cm². A
     tile is 25 × 25 = 625 cm². Tiles: 60,000 ÷ 625 = 96.
     A quicker way with lengths: 200 ÷ 25 = 8 tiles along one side and
     300 ÷ 25 = 12 along the other: 8 × 12 = 96. Remember
     1 m² = 100 cm × 100 cm = 10,000 cm² — NOT 100 cm². Common mistake:
     6 m² ÷ 625 cm² without converting. Now you try: how many cm² are in 3 m²?
     (30,000 cm²)

D12. A right-angled triangle has sides 6 cm, 8 cm and 10 cm (the right angle is
     between the 6 cm and 8 cm sides). Find its area. Then find the height from
     the right angle to the 10 cm side.
     Answer: Area = 1/2 × 6 × 8 = 24 cm². Height to the 10 cm
     side = 2 × 24 ÷ 10 = 4.8 cm.
     Teacher explains: Choose the easiest base-height pair: the two sides at the
     right angle. Area = 1/2 × 6 × 8 = 24 cm². Now use the SAME area with a
     different base. With base 10 cm: 1/2 × 10 × h = 24, so 10 × h = 48 and
     h = 4.8 cm.
     Any side can be the base, but each base has its own height. The area does
     not change, so this is a clever way to find a height you cannot measure
     easily. Check: 1/2 × 10 × 4.8 = 24. Now you try: a right triangle with
     sides 5 cm, 12 cm and 13 cm (right angle between 5 and 12). Find its area.
     (30 cm²)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------

    s) 16, 3    t) 2.5, 4   u) 6, 1.5   v) 30, 40   w) 7, 3     x) 11, 11

             p) 100 cm²   q) 0.5 cm²   r) 40.5 cm²   s) 24 cm²   t) 5 cm²
             u) 4.5 cm²   v) 600 cm²   w) 10.5 cm²   x) 60.5 cm²

             p) 13.5 cm²   q) 8 cm²   r) 2250 cm²   s) 40.5 cm²   t) 300 cm²
             u) 63 cm²   v) 25.5 cm²   w) 42 cm²   x) 1 cm²

M3. Parallelograms. The base, height and slanting side are given (cm). Find the
    area (the slant is not needed).
    a) b = 12, h = 5, slant 7     b) b = 9, h = 4, slant 6
    c) b = 10, h = 8, slant 9     d) b = 15, h = 6, slant 10

    i) b = 25, h = 12, slant 13   j) b = 8, h = 1.5, slant 3
