     Answers: a) 36 cm   b) 60 cm   c) 120 cm   d) 18 cm   e) 144 cm   f) 84 cm
              g) 40 cm   h) 80 cm   i) 64 cm   j) 56 cm   k) 72 cm   l) 24 cm
              m) 180 cm   n) 72 cm   o) 80 cm   p) 220 cm

M11. Views of stacks of cubes. The numbers give the height of the stack on each
     square of the top view ("/" starts the next row; the first row is at the
     BACK). Write the heights seen from the FRONT (the tallest in each column,
     left to right) and from the LEFT side (the tallest in each row, back row
     first).
     a) 3 2 1 / 2 2 1           b) 1 1 1 / 1 1 1
     c) 2 1 / 1 1               d) 1 2 3
     e) 3 / 2 / 1               f) 1 0 2 / 2 1 0
     g) 4 1 1 / 1 1 1 / 1 1 3   h) 2 2 2 / 2 3 2 / 2 2 2
     Answers: a) front 3 2 1; left 3 2   b) front 1 1 1; left 1 1
              c) front 2 1; left 2 1   d) front 1 2 3; left 3
              e) front 3; left 3 2 1   f) front 2 1 2; left 2 2
              g) front 4 1 3; left 4 1 3   h) front 2 3 2; left 2 3 2

M12. True or false?
     a) A cube has 8 vertices.
     b) A triangular prism has 6 faces.
     c) A square pyramid has 5 vertices.
     d) F + V − E = 2 for every polyhedron without holes.
     e) A cube has 12 different nets.
     f) A row of 6 squares folds into a cube.
     g) A net of a cube never contains a 2 × 2 block of squares.
     h) On a real dice, 2 is opposite 5.
     i) A tetrahedron has 4 faces, 4 vertices and 6 edges.
     j) The net of a cylinder has a rectangle as long as the diameter.
     k) A sphere has no net.
     l) A cone has one vertex (the apex).
     m) A hexagonal prism has 18 edges.
     n) A pyramid on a 10-gon has 11 faces.
     o) In a net, two squares with one square between them in a line are
        opposite faces.
     p) The top view of a solid always shows how tall it is.
     Answers: a) true   b) false (5)   c) true   d) true   e) false (11)
              f) false   g) true   h) true   i) true
              j) false (as long as the circumference)   k) true   l) true
              m) true   n) true   o) true   p) false

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: handle real solids (boxes, dice, a Toblerone box, a tin, a party
  cap) and count with stickers; cut nets out of squared paper and fold them
  every time before deciding; colour opposite faces of a net the same colour;
  build stacks with real cubes before drawing views. Harder: find all 11 cube
  nets and prove there are no more; find the nets of an octahedron; check
  Euler's formula for a "house" shape (a cube with a square pyramid on top:
  F = 9, V = 9, E = 16); design a net for a gift box with a lid flap; draw the
  three views of a solid and ask a friend to build it from cubes.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.28 — MEAN, MEDIAN, MODE, RANGE
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - An AVERAGE is one number that represents a whole set of data. There are
    three kinds: mean, median and mode.
  - MEAN = total of all the values ÷ number of values. It is the "fair share":
    if everything were shared out equally, each would get the mean. Mean of 4,
    7, 10: (4 + 7 + 10) ÷ 3 = 21 ÷ 3 = 7.
  - MEDIAN = the middle value when the data are put IN ORDER. With an odd number
    of values there is one middle value; with an even number, the median is
    halfway between the two middle values (add them and divide by 2). The
    (n + 1) ÷ 2-th value is the middle one.
  - MODE = the value that occurs most often. A set can have one mode, several
    modes (if they tie), or no mode (if every value occurs the same number of
    times).
  - RANGE = largest value − smallest value. It measures how SPREAD OUT the data
    are; it is not an average.
  - Working backwards: total = mean × number of values, so a missing
    value = (mean × n) − (sum of the others). Adding a value above the mean
    raises the mean; below the mean lowers it.
  - Choosing an average: the MEDIAN is best when there is an OUTLIER (one value
    far away from the rest); the MODE is best for the most popular item (shoe
    sizes, favourite colours); the MEAN uses every value. From a FREQUENCY
    TABLE: mean = total of (value × frequency) ÷ total frequency.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Virat scores 45, 30, 62, 18 and 55 runs in five matches. What is his mean
    score?
    Answer: 42 runs. Total 45 + 30 + 62 + 18 + 55 = 210, and 210 ÷ 5 = 42.
    Teacher explains: Two steps: ADD all the values, then DIVIDE by HOW MANY
    there are. 45 + 30 = 75, 75 + 62 = 137, 137 + 18 = 155, 155 + 55 = 210.
    There are 5 matches: 210 ÷ 5 = 42.
    Check that the mean is sensible: it must lie between the smallest (18) and
    the largest (62). Common mistake: dividing by the wrong number (for example
    by 4 because one match is forgotten). Now you try: 12, 15, 9, 20, 14.
    (70 ÷ 5 = 14)

D2. The heights of 7 children in cm are 142, 135, 150, 138, 147, 135, 141. Find
    the median height.
    Answer: 141 cm. In order: 135, 135, 138, 141, 142, 147, 150. The middle
    (4th) value is 141.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: put the values IN ORDER (cross each one off as you
    write it, so none is lost): 135, 135, 138, 141, 142, 147, 150. Step 2:
    count: 7 values. The middle one is the (7 + 1) ÷ 2 = 4th. Step 3: the 4th
    value is 141. There are 3 values below it and 3 above.
    Common mistake: taking the middle of the list WITHOUT ordering it (the 4th
    value of the original list is 138 — wrong). Now you try: 9, 3, 7, 5, 11. (7)

D3. Find the median of 8, 3, 12, 5, 10, 6.
    Answer: 7. In order: 3, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12. The two middle values are 6 and 8,
    and (6 + 8) ÷ 2 = 7.
    Teacher explains: With an EVEN number of values there is no single middle
    value. Order them: 3, 5, 6, 8, 10, 12. Cross off one from each end until two
    are left in the middle: 6 and 8. The median is halfway between them:
    (6 + 8) ÷ 2 = 14 ÷ 2 = 7.
    The median need not be one of the values, and it can be a decimal: for 2, 5,
    6, 9 the median is (5 + 6) ÷ 2 = 5.5. Now you try: 4, 1, 9, 7. (5.5)

D4. Find the mode: a) shoe sizes of 10 children: 5, 6, 5, 7, 4, 5, 6, 8, 5, 6;
    b) 3, 7, 3, 9, 7, 1; c) 2, 4, 6, 8.
    Answer: a) 5 (it occurs 4 times). b) 3 and 7 (each occurs twice): two modes.
    c) no mode (every value occurs once).
    Teacher explains: Make a tally: in a) 4 → 1, 5 → 4, 6 → 3, 7 → 1, 8 → 1. The
    most frequent value is 5. The MODE is a value (size 5), not how many times
    it occurs (4).
    In b) two values tie for the most: both are modes (the data are "bimodal").
    In c) nothing repeats, so there is no mode — do NOT write 0, because 0 might
    be a real value! Now you try: 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 3. (3)

D5. The noon temperatures in Shimla for a week were 12°, 15°, 9°, 14°, 8°, 11°
    and 13° Celsius. What is the range?
    Answer: 7 degrees. Highest 15, lowest 8, and 15 − 8 = 7.
    Teacher explains: Find the LARGEST value (15) and the SMALLEST (8) — no need
    to order everything. Range = 15 − 8 = 7. The range tells us how much the
    temperature varied during the week.
    Compare: Chennai the same week had 30 to 33 degrees, range 3 — much
    steadier. Common mistake: writing "8 to 15" (that is the spread, but the
    range is ONE number, the difference). Now you try: 45, 60, 38, 72, 50.
    (72 − 38 = 34)

D6. Meena's mean mark in 4 tests is 72. Her first three marks are 68, 75 and 80.
    What did she score in the fourth test?
    Answer: 65. The total must be 4 × 72 = 288. The three known marks add up to
    223, and 288 − 223 = 65.
    Teacher explains: Work backwards from the mean: mean = total ÷ number, so
    total = mean × number = 72 × 4 = 288. Known: 68 + 75 + 80 = 223. Missing:
    288 − 223 = 65. Check: (68 + 75 + 80 + 65) ÷ 4 = 288 ÷ 4 = 72.
    This "total first" idea solves every missing-value question. Common mistake:
    taking 72 − (something) instead of working with totals. Now you try: the
    mean of 5 numbers is 10; four of them are 8, 12, 9 and 11. Find the fifth.
    (10)

D7. The mean of 5 numbers is 20. A sixth number, 50, is added. What is the new
    mean?
    Answer: 25. Old total 5 × 20 = 100. New total 100 + 50 = 150. New mean
    150 ÷ 6 = 25.
    Teacher explains: Again use totals. The five numbers add up to 100. Add 50:
    150. Now there are 6 numbers: 150 ÷ 6 = 25.
    The mean went UP because 50 is more than the old mean (20). Adding a value
    equal to the mean (20) would keep the mean at 20: 120 ÷ 6 = 20. Adding a
    smaller value lowers it. Common mistake: (20 + 50) ÷ 2 = 35 — that treats
    the old five numbers as if they were one. Now you try: mean of 4 numbers is
    10; add 0. New mean? (40 ÷ 5 = 8)

D8. Five people in a small office earn ₹12,000, ₹15,000, ₹14,000, ₹13,000 and
    ₹96,000 a month (the last is the owner). Find the mean and the median. Which
    average describes a typical worker better?
    Answer: Mean = ₹150,000 ÷ 5 = ₹30,000. Median = ₹14,000. The median is
    better: four of the five earn less than half of the mean.
    Teacher explains: Total:
    12,000 + 15,000 + 14,000 + 13,000 + 96,000 = 150,000. Mean:
    150,000 ÷ 5 = 30,000. In order: 12,000, 13,000, 14,000, 15,000, 96,000; the
    middle value is 14,000.
    One very large value (an OUTLIER) pulls the mean up a lot but does not move
    the median, because the median only looks at the middle. When a set has an
    outlier, use the median. (In Indian numbering 1,50,000 is one lakh fifty
    thousand.) Now you try: find the mean and median of 2, 3, 3, 4, 38. (mean
    10, median 3)

D9. A shoe shop sold these sizes in one day: 6, 7, 7, 8, 7, 9, 6, 7, 10, 8. The
    owner wants to know which size to order most. Which average should she use?
    Answer: The mode: size 7 (sold 4 times).
    Teacher explains: The mean of the sizes is 75 ÷ 10 = 7.5, but no shoe comes
    in size 7.5! The median is also 7. What the owner really wants is the MOST
    POPULAR size — that is the mode, 7.
    Guide: use the MODE for the most common choice (sizes, colours, favourite
    fruit — it even works for data that are not numbers); the MEDIAN when there
    are outliers; the MEAN when all values matter and there are no extreme
    values (like marks in tests). Now you try: favourite colours: red, blue,
    blue, green, red, blue. Which average can you find? (mode: blue)

D10. A team recorded the goals it scored in 20 matches. Find the mean number of
     goals per match.
          goals (x)          0    1    2    3    4
          matches (f)        3    6    5    4    2
     Answer: 1.8 goals per match. Total goals = 36, total matches = 20, and
     36 ÷ 20 = 1.8.
     Teacher explains: Each column says "this many goals happened in this many
     matches". Multiply to get the goals from each column, then add:
          x × f:   0 × 3 = 0,  1 × 6 = 6,  2 × 5 = 10,
                   3 × 4 = 12, 4 × 2 = 8     total = 36
     Number of matches: 3 + 6 + 5 + 4 + 2 = 20. Mean = 36 ÷ 20 = 1.8. Common
     mistake: adding 0 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 = 10 and dividing by 5 — that ignores how
     often each value happened. From the table, the mode is 1 goal (6 matches)
     and the median is the average of the 10th and 11th values: both 2, so 2.
     Now you try: x = 1, 2, 3 with f = 2, 5, 3. Find the mean. (21 ÷ 10 = 2.1)

D11. Three stacks of cubes have 4, 7 and 7 cubes. Show with the cubes what the
     mean means.
     Answer: The mean is (4 + 7 + 7) ÷ 3 = 6: if the cubes are moved to make all
     the stacks equal, each stack has 6 cubes.
     Teacher explains: Move one cube from each tall stack to the short one:
         before:  4, 7, 7      after:  6, 6, 6
         total 18 cubes         total 18 cubes
     Nothing is added or taken away, so the total stays 18; shared equally among
     3 stacks gives 18 ÷ 3 = 6. That is exactly what the mean is — the level
     everything would reach if shared out fairly. It also shows why the mean is
     always between the smallest and largest values. Now you try: stacks of 2,
     5, 3, 6. Level them. (4 each)

D12. Class 6A has 20 students with a mean mark of 60. Class 6B has 30 students
     with a mean mark of 70. What is the mean mark of all 50 students?
     Answer: 66, not 65. Total marks: 20 × 60 + 30 × 70 = 1200 + 2100 = 3300,
     and 3300 ÷ 50 = 66.
     Teacher explains: It is tempting to say (60 + 70) ÷ 2 = 65, but that would
     be right only if the two classes were the same size. 6B is bigger, so its
     mean counts more. Use totals: 6A has 20 × 60 = 1200 marks, 6B has
     30 × 70 = 2100 marks. Altogether 3300 marks for 50 students:
     3300 ÷ 50 = 66.
     The combined mean is always between the two means and closer to the bigger
     group. Now you try: 10 children with mean 8 and 30 children with mean 12.
     Combined mean? (440 ÷ 40 = 11)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the mean.
    a) 4, 7, 10                  b) 2, 4, 6, 8
    c) 5, 5, 5, 5, 5             d) 10, 20, 30, 40, 50
    e) 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7       f) 12, 15, 9, 20, 14
    g) 45, 30, 62, 18, 55        h) 3, 8, 7
    i) 0, 0, 6                   j) 100, 200, 300, 400
    k) 9, 11                     l) 6, 8, 10, 12
    m) 15, 25, 20, 30, 10        n) 2, 3, 5, 7, 11, 2
    o) 50, 60, 70, 80, 90, 100   p) 1, 1, 1, 9
    q) 7, 8, 9, 10, 11           r) 25, 35
    s) 13, 17, 19, 23            t) 4, 5, 6, 7
    u) 1, 2                      v) 8, 9, 10, 11, 12, 13
    w) 36, 42, 48, 54            x) 120, 80, 100, 90, 110
    Answers: a) 7   b) 5   c) 5   d) 30   e) 4   f) 14   g) 42   h) 6   i) 2
             j) 250   k) 10   l) 9   m) 20   n) 5   o) 75   p) 3   q) 9   r) 30
             s) 18   t) 5.5   u) 1.5   v) 10.5   w) 45   x) 100

M2. Find the median (odd number of values). Put the values in order first.
    a) 9, 3, 7, 5, 11                      b) 4, 1, 8
    c) 142, 135, 150, 138, 147, 135, 141   d) 6, 6, 2, 9, 1
    e) 10, 30, 20                          f) 5, 1, 4, 2, 3, 7, 6
    g) 15, 12, 18, 11, 20                  h) 0, 5, 0, 5, 0
    i) 99, 100, 98                         j) 23, 17, 31, 17, 25, 40, 19
    k) 7, 7, 7                             l) 2, 9, 4, 8, 1, 6, 3, 5, 7
    m) 60, 45, 80, 45, 90                  n) 1, 100, 3
    o) 12, 14, 13, 11, 15                  p) 8, 2, 6, 4, 10, 3, 9
    Answers: a) 7   b) 4   c) 141   d) 6   e) 20   f) 4   g) 15   h) 0   i) 99
             j) 23   k) 7   l) 5   m) 60   n) 3   o) 13   p) 6

M3. Find the median (even number of values): halfway between the two middle
    values.
    a) 8, 3, 12, 5, 10, 6          b) 4, 1, 9, 7
    c) 2, 5, 6, 9                  d) 10, 20
    e) 3, 3, 4, 4                  f) 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6
    g) 15, 11, 13, 17              h) 7, 9, 8, 6
    i) 20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70      j) 5, 1, 5, 1
    k) 12, 18, 14, 16, 11, 19      l) 100, 102, 101, 103
    m) 0, 4, 6, 2                  n) 9, 3, 7, 1, 5, 11, 13, 15
    o) 25, 24, 26, 30              p) 2, 2, 8, 8, 5, 5
    Answers: a) 7   b) 5.5   c) 5.5   d) 15   e) 3.5   f) 3.5   g) 14   h) 7.5
             i) 45   j) 3   k) 15   l) 101.5   m) 3   n) 8   o) 25.5   p) 5

M4. Find the mode(s). Write "no mode" if every value occurs equally often.
    a) 5, 6, 5, 7, 4, 5, 6, 8, 5, 6   b) 3, 7, 3, 9, 7, 1
    c) 2, 4, 6, 8                     d) 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 3
    e) 9, 9, 9, 9                     f) 4, 5, 4, 5
    g) 0, 1, 0, 2, 0                  h) 10, 20, 10, 30, 20, 10
    i) 6, 7, 8, 7, 6, 7               j) 11, 12, 13, 14, 15
    k) 5, 5, 6, 6, 7, 7               l) 1, 1, 2, 3, 3, 4
    m) 8, 3, 8, 3, 8                  n) 100, 90, 100, 80, 90
    o) 2, 3, 4, 3, 2, 3, 4            p) 3, 3, 3, 8
    q) 1, 2, 3, 1, 2, 3, 1            r) 15, 20, 25, 20, 15, 30
    s) 4, 4, 4, 5, 5, 5, 6            t) 12, 13, 12, 13, 14
    Answers: a) 5   b) 3 and 7   c) no mode   d) 3   e) 9   f) no mode   g) 0
             h) 10   i) 7   j) no mode   k) no mode   l) 1 and 3   m) 8
             n) 90 and 100   o) 3   p) 3   q) 1   r) 15 and 20   s) 4 and 5
             t) 12 and 13

M5. Find the range (largest − smallest).
    a) 12, 15, 9, 14, 8, 11, 13   b) 45, 60, 38, 72, 50
    c) 5, 5, 5                    d) 1, 100
    e) 23, 45, 12, 67, 34         f) 0, 9, 3
    g) 150, 120, 180, 135         h) 7, 2, 9, 4, 11, 6
    i) 99, 101, 100               j) 3.5, 2.5, 6
    k) 1.2, 0.8, 2                l) 30, 25, 40, 35, 20
    m) 1000, 250, 750             n) 17, 71
    o) 6, 6, 7, 6                 p) 48, 36, 60, 52, 44
    q) 11, 22, 33, 44, 55         r) 0, 0, 0, 1
    s) 365, 366, 364              t) 8, 15, 4, 23, 16, 42
    Answers: a) 7   b) 34   c) 0   d) 99   e) 55   f) 9   g) 60   h) 9   i) 2
             j) 3.5   k) 1.2   l) 20   m) 750   n) 54   o) 1   p) 24   q) 44
             r) 1   s) 2   t) 38

M6. Find the mean, median, mode and range of each set.
    a) 2, 4, 4, 5, 10       b) 3, 5, 7, 7, 8        c) 1, 1, 2, 4, 7
    d) 6, 8, 8, 9, 10, 13   e) 10, 20, 20, 30, 70   f) 4, 6, 6, 8
    g) 5, 9, 9, 12, 15      h) 2, 3, 3, 4, 5, 7     i) 12, 14, 14, 16, 19
    j) 0, 2, 2, 3, 8        k) 7, 7, 8, 9, 14       l) 20, 25, 30, 30, 45
    Answers: a) mean 5, median 4, mode 4, range 8
             b) mean 6, median 7, mode 7, range 5
             c) mean 3, median 2, mode 1, range 6
             d) mean 9, median 8.5, mode 8, range 7
             e) mean 30, median 20, mode 20, range 60
             f) mean 6, median 6, mode 6, range 4
             g) mean 10, median 9, mode 9, range 10
             h) mean 4, median 3.5, mode 3, range 5
             i) mean 15, median 14, mode 14, range 7
             j) mean 3, median 2, mode 2, range 8
             k) mean 9, median 8, mode 7, range 7
             l) mean 30, median 30, mode 30, range 25

M7. The mean of n numbers is given, with all but one of the numbers. Find the
    missing number.
    a) n = 4, mean 72: 68, 75, 80, __
    b) n = 5, mean 10: 8, 12, 9, 11, __
    c) n = 3, mean 20: 15, 25, __
    d) n = 4, mean 6: 5, 7, 8, __
    e) n = 5, mean 50: 40, 60, 45, 55, __
    f) n = 3, mean 9: 9, 9, __
    g) n = 6, mean 15: 10, 20, 12, 18, 14, __
    h) n = 4, mean 25: 30, 20, 10, __
    i) n = 5, mean 8: 0, 10, 12, 6, __
    j) n = 3, mean 100: 90, 120, __
    k) n = 4, mean 3.5: 2, 5, 4, __
    l) n = 5, mean 7: 7, 7, 7, 14, __
    m) n = 2, mean 45: 38, __
    n) n = 6, mean 11: 9, 13, 10, 12, 8, __
    o) n = 4, mean 60: 55, 70, 65, __
    p) n = 5, mean 1: 0, 2, 1, 1, __
    Answers: a) 65   b) 10   c) 20   d) 4   e) 50   f) 9   g) 16   h) 40   i) 12
             j) 90   k) 3   l) 0   m) 52   n) 14   o) 50   p) 1

M8. The mean of n numbers is given, then one more number is added. Find the new
    mean, and say whether it went up, down or stayed the same.
    a) n = 5, mean 20, add 50   b) n = 4, mean 10, add 0
    c) n = 4, mean 10, add 10   d) n = 3, mean 12, add 24
    e) n = 9, mean 5, add 15    f) n = 6, mean 7, add 14
    g) n = 4, mean 25, add 5    h) n = 2, mean 30, add 60
    i) n = 7, mean 8, add 16    j) n = 10, mean 6, add 17
    k) n = 5, mean 40, add 10   l) n = 3, mean 0, add 12
    Answers: a) 25, up   b) 8, down   c) 10, same   d) 15, up   e) 6, up
             f) 8, up   g) 21, down   h) 40, up   i) 9, up   j) 7, up
             k) 35, down   l) 3, up

M9. Frequency tables (x = value, f = how many times). Find the mean (total of
    x × f ÷ total of f), the median and the mode.
    a) x:  0   1   2   3   4
       f:  3   6   5   4   2
    b) x:  1   2   3
       f:  2   5   3
    c) x:  5   6   7   8
       f:  1   4   3   2
    d) x: 10  20  30
       f:  5   3   2
    e) x:  0   1   2
       f:  4   4   2
    f) x:  2   4   6   8
       f:  1   2   3   4
    g) x:  1   2   3   4   5
       f:  1   1   1   1   1
    h) x:  3   4   5   6
       f:  5   2   2   1
    Answers: a) mean 1.8, median 2, mode 1   b) mean 2.1, median 2, mode 2
             c) mean 6.6, median 6.5, mode 6   d) mean 17, median 15, mode 10
             e) mean 0.8, median 1, mode 0 and 1   f) mean 6, median 6, mode 8
             g) mean 3, median 3, no mode   h) mean 3.9, median 3.5, mode 3

M10. Find the total of the values from the number of values n and the mean.
     a) n = 5, mean 20     b) n = 4, mean 10     c) n = 3, mean 7
     d) n = 6, mean 15     e) n = 10, mean 8     f) n = 8, mean 12.5
     g) n = 4, mean 2.5    h) n = 12, mean 50    i) n = 7, mean 14
     j) n = 20, mean 3.5   k) n = 5, mean 0      l) n = 9, mean 11
     Answers: a) 100   b) 40   c) 21   d) 90   e) 80   f) 100   g) 10   h) 600
              i) 98   j) 70   k) 0   l) 99

M11. Change the values as shown and write the NEW mean, median and range.
     (Adding the same number to every value moves the averages but not the
     range.)
     a) 2, 4, 6, 8, 10; add 5 to each
     b) 3, 3, 6, 9, 9; add 10 to each
     c) 1, 2, 3, 4, 5; double each
     d) 10, 20, 30; subtract 5 from each
     e) 4, 6, 8, 12; add 1 to each
     f) 5, 7, 9; multiply each by 3
     g) 2, 2, 5, 7, 9; add 100 to each
     h) 6, 8, 10, 16; halve each
     i) 0, 4, 5, 7; add 3 to each
     j) 1, 3, 3, 5; multiply each by 10
     k) 12, 15, 18; subtract 12 from each
     l) 7, 9, 11, 13; double each
     Answers: a) mean 11, median 11, range 8   b) mean 16, median 16, range 6
              c) mean 6, median 6, range 8   d) mean 15, median 15, range 20
              e) mean 8.5, median 8, range 8   f) mean 21, median 21, range 12
              g) mean 105, median 105, range 7   h) mean 5, median 4.5, range 5
              i) mean 7, median 7.5, range 7   j) mean 30, median 30, range 40
              k) mean 3, median 3, range 6   l) mean 20, median 20, range 12

M12. True or false?
     a) The mean of 2, 4 and 9 is 5.
     b) The median of 3, 9, 1 is 9.
     c) The median of 1, 2, 3, 4 is 2.5.
     d) The mode of 4, 4, 5, 5, 6 is 4 and 5.
     e) 2, 3, 4 has mode 0.
     f) The range of 7, 3, 12 is 12.
     g) The mean is always one of the values.
     h) The mean always lies between the smallest and largest values.
     i) An outlier changes the median more than the mean.
     j) Adding 10 to every value adds 10 to the mean.
     k) Adding 10 to every value adds 10 to the range.
     l) If the mean of 4 numbers is 6, their total is 24.
     m) The mode can be used for data that are not numbers.
     n) For shoe sizes to stock, the mode is the most useful average.
     o) Combined mean of 10 values with mean 4 and 10 values with mean 8 is 6.
     p) Combined mean of 10 values with mean 4 and 30 values with mean 8 is 6.
     Answers: a) true   b) false (3)   c) true   d) true   e) false (no mode)
              f) false (9)   g) false   h) true   i) false (the mean more)
              j) true   k) false (no change)   l) true   m) true   n) true
              o) true   p) false (7)

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use cubes to build towers and level them for the mean; write the
  data on small cards and physically put them in order for the median, turning
  over one card from each end; tally for the mode; keep a card with "mean: add
  and share, median: order and middle, mode: most often, range:
  biggest − smallest". Harder: make up a set of 5 numbers with mean 10, median 8
  and mode 6 (for example 6, 6, 8, 12, 18); find how the mean changes when one
  value is removed; compare two cricket players using both mean and range;
  collect real data from the class (heights, travel times) and decide which
  average describes it best.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.29 — INTRODUCTION TO PROBABILITY (LIKELY, UNLIKELY, SIMPLE FRACTIONS)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - PROBABILITY measures how likely something is to happen. Each result of an
    experiment (tossing a coin, rolling a die, spinning a spinner, picking a
    counter) is an OUTCOME; an EVENT is one or more outcomes we are interested
    in.
  - The words: IMPOSSIBLE (can never happen), UNLIKELY, EVEN CHANCE (as likely
    as not), LIKELY, CERTAIN (must happen). Put them on the probability scale
    from 0 to 1: impossible = 0, even chance = 1/2, certain = 1. A probability
    can never be less than 0 or more than 1.
  - When all outcomes are EQUALLY LIKELY: P(event) = number of favourable
    outcomes ÷ total number of outcomes. On one die, P(even) = 3/6 = 1/2 because
    2, 4 and 6 are favourable out of 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6.
  - Write a probability as a fraction in simplest form, and also as a decimal or
    percent: 1/4 = 0.25 = 25%.
  - Coins, dice, spinners with equal parts, bags of coloured counters, number
    cards and a pack of 52 playing cards (4 suits of 13) are the usual
    experiments. An unequal spinner or a bag with more red counters makes some
    outcomes more likely.
  - COMPLEMENTARY events: P(not A) = 1 − P(A). If P(rain) = 3/10, then P(no
    rain) = 7/10. The probabilities of all the outcomes add up to 1.
  - For two coins or a coin and a die, LIST all the outcomes (HH, HT, TH, TT) or
    use a table before counting.
  - THEORETICAL probability is what we expect; EXPERIMENTAL probability is what
    happened: number of times it happened ÷ number of trials. With more trials
    the experimental result usually gets closer to the theoretical one. Expected
    number = probability × number of trials.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Use one of the words impossible, unlikely, even chance, likely or certain
    for each event. a) The sun will rise tomorrow. b) You roll a 7 on an
    ordinary die. c) A tossed coin lands heads. d) It rains in Mumbai on a day
    in July. e) It snows in Chennai next week.
    Answer: a) certain  b) impossible  c) even chance  d) likely  e) unlikely
    Teacher explains: An ordinary die has only the numbers 1 to 6, so a 7 can
    never come: impossible. A coin has two faces that are equally likely, so
    heads and tails each have an even chance, "fifty-fifty". The sun rising is
    certain. In July the monsoon brings rain to Mumbai on most days, so rain is
    likely (but not certain: some July days are dry). Snow in Chennai has never
    been seen, but we say "unlikely" rather than "impossible" for weather,
    because we cannot be completely sure. Ask the child to give a reason each
    time, not just a word. A common mistake is to call anything that is not
    certain "impossible". Now you try: choose a word for "picking a red counter
    from a bag of only red counters". (certain)

D2. Mark these on a probability scale from 0 to 1: impossible, even chance,
    certain, a probability of 1/4 and a probability of 3/4.
    0          1/4          1/2          3/4          1
    |-----------|------------|------------|------------|
    impossible  unlikely    even chance    likely    certain
    Answer: 0 = impossible, 1/2 = even chance, 1 = certain; 1/4 is unlikely
    (between 0 and 1/2) and 3/4 is likely (between 1/2 and 1).
    Teacher explains: Draw a line and write 0 at the left end and 1 at the right
    end. The middle is 1/2. Every probability sits somewhere on this line: the
    nearer to 0, the less likely; the nearer to 1, the more likely. Anything
    between 0 and 1/2 is unlikely and anything between 1/2 and 1 is likely.
    Decimals and percents go on the same line: 0.25 = 25% is the same point as
    1/4, and 0.9 = 90% is very likely. A probability of 5/4 or 1.2 or −1/2 is
    impossible to have, because nothing can be more than certain or less than
    impossible. Now you try: where does 0.1 go, and which word fits it? (near 0;
    unlikely)

D3. Riya tosses a fair coin. What is the probability of getting heads? What is
    the probability of getting tails?
    Answer: P(heads) = 1/2 and P(tails) = 1/2.
    Teacher explains: A fair coin has 2 equally likely outcomes: H (heads) and T
    (tails). Heads is 1 of the 2 outcomes, so P(heads) = number of favourable
    outcomes ÷ total number of outcomes = 1/2. Tails is the other 1 of the 2:
    P(tails) = 1/2. Check: 1/2 + 1/2 = 1, and the probabilities of all the
    outcomes always add up to 1. A common belief is that after three heads in a
    row, tails is "due". It is not: the coin has no memory, and the next toss is
    still 1/2 for each. Now you try: a coin lands heads 5 times in a row; what
    is P(heads) on the next toss? (1/2)

D4. Arjun rolls one ordinary die. Find the probability of rolling a) a 4 b) an
    even number c) a number greater than 4 d) a prime number e) a number less
    than 7.
    outcomes:  1   2   3   4   5   6
    even:          2       4       6
    > 4:                       5   6
    prime:         2   3       5
    Answer: a) 1/6  b) 3/6 = 1/2  c) 2/6 = 1/3  d) 3/6 = 1/2  e) 6/6 = 1
    Teacher explains: There are 6 equally likely outcomes: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6. For
    each event LIST the favourable outcomes, count them, and put the count over
    6. a) Only the 4: 1/6. b) 2, 4, 6: 3/6 = 1/2. c) "Greater than 4" means 5
    and 6 (not 4 itself): 2/6 = 1/3. d) The primes are 2, 3 and 5 (1 is NOT
    prime): 3/6 = 1/2. e) Every number on the die is less than 7, so it is
    certain: 6/6 = 1. Always simplify: divide the top and bottom by the same
    number. The two common mistakes are counting 1 as a prime and including 4 in
    "greater than 4". Now you try: P(a multiple of 3). (2/6 = 1/3)

D5. A spinner has 8 equal parts: 3 red (R), 2 blue (B), 2 green (G) and 1 yellow
    (Y). Find the probability of each colour. Which colour is most likely?
    +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    | R | R | R | B | B | G | G | Y |
    +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    Answer: P(red) = 3/8, P(blue) = 2/8 = 1/4, P(green) = 2/8 = 1/4,
    P(yellow) = 1/8. Red is the most likely.
    Teacher explains: The spinner has 8 EQUAL parts, so each part has
    probability 1/8. Red covers 3 parts, so P(red) = 3/8; blue and green 2 parts
    each, 2/8 = 1/4; yellow 1 part, 1/8. Check: 3/8 + 2/8 + 2/8 + 1/8 = 8/8 = 1.
    Red has the biggest fraction, so it is the most likely, and yellow the least
    likely. Blue and green are EQUALLY likely. Warn the child: this works only
    because the parts are equal. If one part were half the spinner, it would
    have probability 1/2 however many other parts there were. Now you try: P(not
    yellow). (7/8)

D6. A bag has 5 red, 3 blue and 2 green counters, all the same size. Meena picks
    one without looking. Find P(red), P(blue), P(green), P(not blue) and
    P(white).
    Answer: P(red) = 5/10 = 1/2, P(blue) = 3/10, P(green) = 2/10 = 1/5, P(not
    blue) = 7/10, P(white) = 0/10 = 0.
    Teacher explains: First find the TOTAL: 5 + 3 + 2 = 10 counters, each
    equally likely to be picked. P(red) = 5/10 = 1/2: an even chance.
    P(blue) = 3/10 and P(green) = 2/10 = 1/5. "Not blue" means red or green:
    5 + 2 = 7, so P(not blue) = 7/10. There are no white counters, so picking
    white is impossible: P(white) = 0. The most common mistake is to put the red
    counters over the OTHER counters (5/5) instead of over ALL the counters
    (5/10). Say: "favourable over total". Now you try: 2 more blue counters are
    added; what is P(blue) now? (5/12)

D7. The probability that Kiran's school bus is late is 1/8. What is the
    probability that it is NOT late? The probability of rolling a 6 on a die is
    1/6. What is P(not 6)?
    Answer: P(not late) = 1 − 1/8 = 7/8. P(not 6) = 1 − 1/6 = 5/6.
    Teacher explains: An event and its opposite (its COMPLEMENT) cannot both
    happen, and one of them must happen. So their probabilities add up to 1:
    P(A) + P(not A) = 1, which gives P(not A) = 1 − P(A). Write 1 as a fraction
    with the same denominator: 1 − 1/8 = 8/8 − 1/8 = 7/8. For the die, "not 6"
    is 1, 2, 3, 4 or 5: 5 outcomes, 5/6; and 1 − 1/6 = 5/6 gives the same
    answer. The rule is very useful when the "not" list is long. It works for
    decimals and percents too: if P(win) = 0.35 then P(not win) = 0.65; if
    P(rain) = 30% then P(no rain) = 70%. Now you try: P(A) = 0.2; find P(not A).
    (0.8)

D8. A pack of 52 playing cards has 4 suits: hearts and diamonds (red), clubs and
    spades (black). Each suit has 13 cards: A, 2 to 10, J, Q, K. One card is
    picked at random. Find P(a heart), P(a king), P(a red card) and P(the queen
    of spades).
    hearts   (red)    13 cards
    diamonds (red)    13 cards
    clubs    (black)  13 cards
    spades   (black)  13 cards
                      --------
             total    52 cards
    Answer: P(heart) = 13/52 = 1/4, P(king) = 4/52 = 1/13, P(red) = 26/52 = 1/2,
    P(queen of spades) = 1/52.
    Teacher explains: Total outcomes: 52 cards. Hearts: 13 of them, 13/52 = 1/4
    (each suit is a quarter of the pack). Kings: one in each suit, so 4 kings,
    4/52 = 1/13. Red cards: hearts and diamonds, 13 + 13 = 26, 26/52 = 1/2. The
    queen of spades is just one card: 1/52. To simplify 13/52, notice
    52 = 4 × 13; to simplify 4/52, divide both by 4. The picture cards J, Q, K
    are called FACE cards: 3 in each suit, 12 in all, so P(face
    card) = 12/52 = 3/13. Now you try: P(a black ace). (2/52 = 1/26)

D9. Sahil tossed a coin 50 times and got 28 heads. What is the experimental
    probability of heads? What is the theoretical probability? Why are they
    different?
    Answer: Experimental P(heads) = 28/50 = 14/25 = 0.56. Theoretical
    P(heads) = 1/2 = 0.5. They differ because of chance; with many more tosses
    the experimental result usually gets closer to 1/2.
    Teacher explains: EXPERIMENTAL probability = number of times the event
    happened ÷ number of trials = 28/50. Simplify:
    28/50 = 14/25 = 56/100 = 0.56. THEORETICAL probability comes from reasoning
    about equally likely outcomes: 1 out of 2, so 0.5. In 50 tosses we EXPECT
    about 50 × 1/2 = 25 heads, but 28 is quite normal; results jump around. If
    the whole class pools its results to make 1,000 tosses, the fraction of
    heads will usually be very close to 0.5. Do this experiment in class and
    record the results in a tally table. Now you try: a die is rolled 30 times
    and 6 comes up 7 times; experimental P(6)? (7/30)

D10. Two coins are tossed together. Neha says there are three outcomes (two
     heads, two tails, one of each), so P(one head and one tail) = 1/3. Is she
     right?
                2nd coin
                 H     T
     1st   H    HH    HT
     coin  T    TH    TT
     Answer: No. There are 4 equally likely outcomes: HH, HT, TH, TT. One head
     and one tail happens in 2 of them, so the probability is 2/4 = 1/2.
     Teacher explains: Make the outcomes EQUALLY LIKELY by keeping the two coins
     apart: call them the 1st coin and the 2nd coin (a ₹1 and a ₹2 coin, say).
     The table shows 4 outcomes. HT (first heads, second tails) and TH are
     DIFFERENT outcomes, and both give "one of each". So P(one head and one
     tail) = 2/4 = 1/2, P(two heads) = 1/4 and P(two tails) = 1/4. Check:
     1/2 + 1/4 + 1/4 = 1. Neha's three outcomes are not equally likely, so they
     cannot be counted as thirds. Toss two coins 40 times to see that "one of
     each" comes up about half the time. Now you try: P(at least one head).
     (3/4)

D11. In a game with one die, Riya scores a point when it shows 1 or 2 and Sam
     scores a point when it shows 3, 4, 5 or 6. Is the game fair? How can the
     rule be changed to make it fair?
     Answer: Not fair: P(Riya) = 2/6 = 1/3 but P(Sam) = 4/6 = 2/3. It is fair
     if, for example, Riya scores on 1, 2 or 3 and Sam on 4, 5 or 6 (or Riya on
     even numbers and Sam on odd numbers): 3/6 = 1/2 each.
     Teacher explains: A game is FAIR when each player has the same probability
     of winning. Count Riya's outcomes: 1 and 2, that is 2 out of 6. Sam has 4
     out of 6, twice as many, so Sam will usually win about twice as often. To
     make it fair, share the 6 outcomes 3 and 3. Other fair rules: Riya on
     primes (2, 3, 5), Sam on 1, 4, 6. An unfair rule stays unfair even if Riya
     wins one particular game; probability tells us what happens in the long
     run. Now you try: Riya wins on a 6 and Sam wins on a 1; nobody scores
     otherwise. Fair? (yes, 1/6 each)

D12. A spinner has P(red) = 1/4. It is spun 80 times. About how many times do we
     expect red? A die is rolled 120 times: about how many 6s do we expect?
     Write 1/4 as a decimal and a percent.
     Answer: About 1/4 × 80 = 20 reds. About 1/6 × 120 = 20 sixes.
     1/4 = 0.25 = 25%.
     Teacher explains: EXPECTED number = probability × number of trials. A
     quarter of the 80 spins: 80 ÷ 4 = 20. A sixth of the 120 rolls:
     120 ÷ 6 = 20. "About" is important: we might get 17 or 23 reds, but 20 is
     the best guess. To change a probability into a percent, make the
     denominator 100: 1/4 = 25/100 = 25%, which is also 0.25. So
     P(red) = 1/4 = 0.25 = 25%. Weather reports use percents: a "70% chance of
     rain" means P(rain) = 0.7 = 7/10, likely. Now you try: P(win) = 3/10; how
     many wins are expected in 50 games? (15)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write impossible, unlikely, even chance, likely or certain for each
    probability. If the number cannot be a probability, write "not a
    probability".
    a) 0      b) 1      c) 1/2    d) 1/10   e) 9/10   f) 3/4    g) 1/4
    h) 2/5    i) 3/5    j) 0.5    k) 0.2    l) 0.99   m) 7/7    n) 0/8
    o) 5/4    p) 50%    q) 80%    r) 5%     s) 1.5    t) 4/9    u) 5/9
    v) 6/12   w) 100%   x) 0.01
    Answers: a) impossible   b) certain   c) even chance   d) unlikely
             e) likely   f) likely   g) unlikely   h) unlikely   i) likely
             j) even chance   k) unlikely   l) likely   m) certain
             n) impossible   o) not a probability   p) even chance   q) likely
             r) unlikely   s) not a probability   t) unlikely   u) likely
             v) even chance   w) certain   x) unlikely

M2. One ordinary die (1 to 6) is rolled. Find each probability as a fraction in
    simplest form.
    a) P(1)               b) P(2)               c) P(3)
    d) P(4)               e) P(5)               f) P(6)
    g) P(even)            h) P(odd)             i) P(> 4)
    j) P(< 3)             k) P(≥ 5)             l) P(≤ 4)
    m) P(prime)           n) P(multiple of 3)   o) P(factor of 6)
    p) P(square)          q) P(composite)       r) P(not 6)
    s) P(7)               t) P(1 or 6)          u) P(≥ 1)
    v) P(odd prime)       w) P(> 6)             x) P(not prime)
    Answers: a) 1/6   b) 1/6   c) 1/6   d) 1/6   e) 1/6   f) 1/6   g) 3/6 = 1/2
             h) 3/6 = 1/2   i) 2/6 = 1/3   j) 2/6 = 1/3   k) 2/6 = 1/3
             l) 4/6 = 2/3   m) 3/6 = 1/2   n) 2/6 = 1/3   o) 4/6 = 2/3
             p) 2/6 = 1/3   q) 2/6 = 1/3   r) 5/6   s) 0/6 = 0   t) 2/6 = 1/3
             u) 6/6 = 1   v) 2/6 = 1/3   w) 0/6 = 0   x) 3/6 = 1/2

M3. A spinner has 8 equal parts numbered 1 to 8. Find each probability in
    simplest form.
    +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 |
    +---+---+---+---+---+---+---+---+
    a) P(3)               b) P(8)               c) P(even)
    d) P(odd)             e) P(> 5)             f) P(< 4)
    g) P(≥ 6)             h) P(≤ 2)             i) P(prime)
    j) P(multiple of 4)   k) P(multiple of 3)   l) P(factor of 8)
    m) P(square)          n) P(not 1)           o) P(9)
    p) P(1 to 8)          q) P(2 to 5)          r) P(composite)
    s) P(even or 1)       t) P(odd prime)
    Answers: a) 1/8   b) 1/8   c) 4/8 = 1/2   d) 4/8 = 1/2   e) 3/8   f) 3/8
             g) 3/8   h) 2/8 = 1/4   i) 4/8 = 1/2   j) 2/8 = 1/4   k) 2/8 = 1/4
             l) 4/8 = 1/2   m) 2/8 = 1/4   n) 7/8   o) 0/8 = 0   p) 8/8 = 1
             q) 4/8 = 1/2   r) 3/8   s) 5/8   t) 3/8

M4. One counter is picked at random from the bag. Find each probability in
    simplest form.
    Bag A: 3 red, 1 blue
    Bag B: 5 red, 3 blue, 2 green
    Bag C: 4 red, 4 blue, 4 green
    Bag D: 7 red, 2 yellow, 6 white
    Bag E: 9 blue, 1 green
    Bag F: 2 red, 5 blue, 8 green, 5 yellow
    a) A: P(red)             b) A: P(blue)            c) A: P(green)
    d) B: P(red)             e) B: P(green)           f) B: P(not blue)
    g) C: P(blue)            h) C: P(not green)       i) C: P(red or blue)
    j) D: P(red)             k) D: P(white)           l) D: P(yellow)
    m) D: P(not red)         n) E: P(blue)            o) E: P(green)
    p) E: P(blue or green)   q) F: P(green)           r) F: P(yellow)
    s) F: P(not blue)        t) F: P(red or yellow)   u) F: P(white)
    Answers: a) 3/4   b) 1/4   c) 0/4 = 0   d) 5/10 = 1/2   e) 2/10 = 1/5
             f) 7/10   g) 4/12 = 1/3   h) 8/12 = 2/3   i) 8/12 = 2/3   j) 7/15
             k) 6/15 = 2/5   l) 2/15   m) 8/15   n) 9/10   o) 1/10
             p) 10/10 = 1   q) 8/20 = 2/5   r) 5/20 = 1/4   s) 15/20 = 3/4
             t) 7/20   u) 0/20 = 0

M5. Complementary events: find P(not A).
    a) P(A) = 1/4     b) P(A) = 1/3     c) P(A) = 2/5     d) P(A) = 5/8
    e) P(A) = 1/6     f) P(A) = 7/10    g) P(A) = 0       h) P(A) = 1
    i) P(A) = 11/12   j) P(A) = 3/7     k) P(A) = 0.3     l) P(A) = 0.75
    m) P(A) = 0.05    n) P(A) = 0.62    o) P(A) = 0.9     p) P(A) = 45%
    q) P(A) = 8%      r) P(A) = 60%     s) P(A) = 99%     t) P(A) = 2/9
    Answers: a) 3/4   b) 2/3   c) 3/5   d) 3/8   e) 5/6   f) 3/10   g) 1   h) 0
             i) 1/12   j) 4/7   k) 0.7   l) 0.25   m) 0.95   n) 0.38   o) 0.1
             p) 55%   q) 92%   r) 40%   s) 1%   t) 7/9

M6. Twenty cards are numbered 1 to 20. One card is picked at random. Find each
    probability in simplest form.
    a) P(7)               b) P(even)            c) P(odd)
    d) P(prime)           e) P(multiple of 5)   f) P(multiple of 3)
    g) P(multiple of 4)   h) P(square)          i) P(> 15)
    j) P(< 6)             k) P(≥ 10)            l) P(≤ 12)
    m) P(factor of 20)    n) P(factor of 12)    o) P(1 to 10)
    p) P(11 to 20)        q) P(not 20)          r) P(21)
    s) P(composite)       t) P(even prime)      u) P(multiple of 7)
    v) P(not square)
    Answers: a) 1/20   b) 10/20 = 1/2   c) 10/20 = 1/2   d) 8/20 = 2/5
             e) 4/20 = 1/5   f) 6/20 = 3/10   g) 5/20 = 1/4   h) 4/20 = 1/5
             i) 5/20 = 1/4   j) 5/20 = 1/4   k) 11/20   l) 12/20 = 3/5
             m) 6/20 = 3/10   n) 6/20 = 3/10   o) 10/20 = 1/2   p) 10/20 = 1/2
             q) 19/20   r) 0/20 = 0   s) 11/20   t) 1/20   u) 2/20 = 1/10
             v) 16/20 = 4/5

M7. One card is picked at random from a pack of 52 playing cards (4 suits of 13:
    A, 2 to 10, J, Q, K; hearts and diamonds red, clubs and spades black; no
    jokers). Find each probability in simplest form.
    a) P(an ace)                  b) P(a heart)
    c) P(a red card)              d) P(a black card)
    e) P(the king of spades)      f) P(a face card)

    o) P(a red face card)         p) P(a joker)
    q) P(a club)                  r) P(any card)
    Answers: a) 4/52 = 1/13   b) 13/52 = 1/4   c) 26/52 = 1/2   d) 26/52 = 1/2

             m) 8/52 = 2/13   n) 48/52 = 12/13   o) 6/52 = 3/26   p) 0/52 = 0
             q) 13/52 = 1/4   r) 52/52 = 1

M8. Write each probability as a decimal and as a percent.

    d) 1/5 = __ = __%     e) 2/5 = __ = __%     f) 4/5 = __ = __%

M9. List or count the equally likely outcomes (2 coins: 4; 3 coins: 8; coin +
    die: 12; 2 dice: 36), then find each probability in simplest form.
    2 dice, sum:  +  1  2  3  4  5  6
                  1  2  3  4  5  6  7
                  2  3  4  5  6  7  8
                  3  4  5  6  7  8  9
                  4  5  6  7  8  9 10
                  5  6  7  8  9 10 11
                  6  7  8  9 10 11 12
    a) 2 coins: P(HH)              b) 2 coins: P(exactly 1 H)
    c) 2 coins: P(at least 1 H)    d) 2 coins: P(no H)
    e) 3 coins: P(HHH)             f) 3 coins: P(exactly 2 H)
    g) 3 coins: P(exactly 1 H)     h) 3 coins: P(at least 1 T)
    i) coin + die: P(H and 6)      j) coin + die: P(T and even)
    k) coin + die: P(H and > 4)    l) coin + die: P(H)
    m) 2 dice: P(sum 7)            n) 2 dice: P(sum 2)
    o) 2 dice: P(sum 12)           p) 2 dice: P(sum 6)
    q) 2 dice: P(sum 10)           r) 2 dice: P(double)
    s) 2 dice: P(sum > 9)          t) 2 dice: P(sum 1)
    Answers: a) 1/4   b) 2/4 = 1/2   c) 3/4   d) 1/4   e) 1/8   f) 3/8   g) 3/8
             h) 7/8   i) 1/12   j) 3/12 = 1/4   k) 2/12 = 1/6   l) 6/12 = 1/2
             m) 6/36 = 1/6   n) 1/36   o) 1/36   p) 5/36   q) 3/36 = 1/12
             r) 6/36 = 1/6   s) 6/36 = 1/6   t) 0/36 = 0

M10. A die was rolled 60 times. Find the experimental probabilities and compare
     them with the theoretical ones. Then find the experimental probabilities
     for the coin results.
     number   1    2    3    4    5    6   total
     times    8   11    9   12   10   10    60
     a) experimental P(1)
     b) experimental P(2)
     c) experimental P(3)

     e) experimental P(5)
     f) experimental P(6)
     g) experimental P(even)
     h) theoretical P(even)
     i) experimental P(> 4)

     k) expected number of 4s in 60 rolls
     l) 40 tosses, 18 heads: experimental P(H)
     m) 40 tosses, 18 heads: experimental P(T)
     n) 50 tosses, 31 heads: experimental P(H)
     o) 50 tosses, 31 heads: experimental P(T)
     p) 100 tosses, 47 heads: experimental P(H)
     q) 100 tosses, 47 heads: experimental P(T)

     s) 20 tosses, 13 heads: experimental P(T)
     Answers: a) 8/60 = 2/15   b) 11/60   c) 9/60 = 3/20   d) 12/60 = 1/5

              i) 20/60 = 1/3   j) 2/6 = 1/3   k) 1/6 × 60 = 10   l) 18/40 = 9/20
              m) 22/40 = 11/20   n) 31/50   o) 19/50   p) 47/100   q) 53/100
              r) 13/20   s) 7/20

M11. Expected number = probability × number of trials. Find the expected number
     of times the event happens.
     a) P = 1/2, 40 trials     b) P = 1/2, 90 trials     c) P = 1/4, 80 trials
     d) P = 1/4, 200 trials    e) P = 1/6, 60 trials     f) P = 1/6, 300 trials
     g) P = 1/3, 45 trials     h) P = 2/3, 60 trials     i) P = 3/4, 100 trials
     j) P = 1/10, 70 trials    k) P = 3/10, 50 trials    l) P = 2/5, 35 trials
     m) P = 1/8, 64 trials     n) P = 5/8, 40 trials     o) P = 1/13, 52 trials
     p) P = 1/52, 104 trials   q) P = 0.2, 150 trials    r) P = 0.35, 200 trials
     s) P = 25%, 36 trials     t) P = 60%, 25 trials
     Answers: a) 20   b) 45   c) 20   d) 50   e) 10   f) 50   g) 15   h) 40
              i) 75   j) 7   k) 15   l) 14   m) 8   n) 25   o) 4   p) 2   q) 30
              r) 70   s) 9   t) 15

M12. Bags of counters. Find the missing number.
     a) total 12, P(red) = 1/3; red = __
     b) total 20, P(red) = 1/4; red = __
     c) total 30, P(blue) = 2/5; blue = __
     d) total 24, P(green) = 5/8; green = __
     e) total 10, P(red) = 0; red = __
     f) total 18, P(red) = 1; red = __
     g) total 40, P(not red) = 3/4; red = __
     h) total 50, P(not blue) = 0.6; blue = __
     i) red 6, P(red) = 1/2; total = __
     j) red 6, P(red) = 2/5; total = __
     k) blue 4, P(blue) = 1/6; total = __
     l) green 9, P(green) = 3/10; total = __
     m) red 5, P(red) = 1/4; not red = __
     n) red 3 and blue 5 only; P(blue) = __
     o) red 7, blue 5, P(green) = 1/4; green = __
     p) red 2, blue 4, add __ red to make P(red) = 1/2
     Answers: a) 4   b) 5   c) 12   d) 15   e) 0   f) 18   g) 10   h) 20   i) 12
              j) 15   k) 24   l) 30   m) 15   n) 5/8   o) 4   p) 2

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use real coins, dice, spinners made from card and paper clips, and
  bags of coloured counters; always LIST the outcomes first and write
  "favourable over total"; stay with one die and one bag until the fractions are
  easy; use a class washing line labelled 0, 1/2 and 1 to peg event cards on.
  Harder: two dice (all 36 outcomes), designing spinners and bags for a given
  probability, pooling class experiments of 500 tosses to watch the experimental
  probability settle, deciding whether games are fair and changing the rules to
  make them fair.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.30 — PROFIT, LOSS, DISCOUNT (SIMPLE COMMERCIAL MATH)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - COST PRICE (CP) is the price at which a shopkeeper buys an article; SELLING
    PRICE (SP) is the price at which it is sold. Any extra money spent on
    transport or repair (OVERHEADS) is added to the CP.
  - If SP > CP there is a PROFIT: profit = SP − CP. If SP < CP there is a LOSS:
    loss = CP − SP. If SP = CP there is neither profit nor loss.
  - Profit and loss PERCENT are always worked out on the COST PRICE: profit % =
    profit ÷ CP × 100%, loss % = loss ÷ CP × 100%. Bought for ₹250 and sold for
    ₹300: profit ₹50, profit % = 50/250 × 100% = 20%.
  - Finding SP: SP = CP + profit, or SP = CP × (100 + profit %) ÷ 100; with a
    loss, SP = CP × (100 − loss %) ÷ 100. A 15% profit on ₹600 gives SP = ₹690.
  - Finding CP from SP: CP = SP × 100 ÷ (100 + profit %), or SP × 100 ÷ (100 −
    loss %). Never take the percent of the SP.
  - The MARKED PRICE (MP) or list price is the price on the tag. A DISCOUNT is a
    reduction on the MP: discount = MP − SP, discount % = discount ÷ MP × 100%,
    sale price SP = MP × (100 − discount %) ÷ 100.
  - Two discounts one after the other (SUCCESSIVE discounts): the second is
    taken on the new, lower price. 20% then 10% is a 28% discount, not 30%.
  - GST (Goods and Services Tax) is ADDED to the price: price with GST =
    price × (100 + GST %) ÷ 100. A shopkeeper can mark goods above the CP and
    still give a discount and make a profit.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Ramesh buys a pen for ₹40 and sells it for ₹50. Gita buys a lunch box for
    ₹180 and has to sell it for ₹165. Who made a profit and who made a loss? How
    much?
    Answer: Ramesh: profit = ₹50 − ₹40 = ₹10. Gita: loss = ₹180 − ₹165 = ₹15.
    Teacher explains: First name the two prices. The COST PRICE (CP) is what the
    seller paid; the SELLING PRICE (SP) is what the buyer paid the seller.
    Ramesh: CP ₹40, SP ₹50. SP is bigger, so he gains: profit = SP − CP = ₹10.
    Gita: CP ₹180, SP ₹165. SP is smaller, so she loses: loss = CP − SP = ₹15.
    Always subtract the smaller price from the bigger one, and then SAY whether
    it is a profit or a loss. A useful habit is a small table with the headings
    CP, SP, profit/loss. Now you try: CP ₹325, SP ₹400. (profit ₹75)

D2. A shopkeeper buys a school bag for ₹250 and sells it for ₹300. Find his
    profit percent.
    Answer: Profit = ₹50. Profit % = 50/250 × 100% = 20%.
    Teacher explains: Step 1: profit = SP − CP = ₹300 − ₹250 = ₹50. Step 2:
    compare the profit with the COST price: 50/250 of the CP. Step 3: turn it
    into a percent by multiplying by 100%: 50/250 × 100% = 5000/250 % = 20%. A
    quick way: 50/250 = 1/5 and 1/5 = 20%. Why the cost price? Because the
    shopkeeper wants to know how much he gained for every ₹100 he spent. A
    common mistake is to divide by the SP: 50/300 is about 17%, which is wrong.
    Now you try: CP ₹80, SP ₹100; profit %? (25%)

D3. Anil bought a bicycle for ₹3,500 and sold it after a year for ₹2,975. Find
    his loss and loss percent.
    Answer: Loss = ₹3,500 − ₹2,975 = ₹525. Loss % = 525/3,500 × 100% = 15%.
    Teacher explains: CP = ₹3,500, SP = ₹2,975. SP is less, so there is a loss
    of ₹3,500 − ₹2,975 = ₹525. Loss percent is also on the COST price:
    525/3,500 × 100% = 15%. To simplify, divide by 35 first: 3,500 ÷ 35 = 100,
    so 525/3,500 = 15/100 = 15%. Check: 15% of ₹3,500 is ₹525 and
    ₹3,500 − ₹525 = ₹2,975. Now you try: CP ₹800, SP ₹680; loss %? (15%)

D4. Farida buys an old sewing machine for ₹2,400, spends ₹600 on repairs, and
    sells it for ₹3,600. Find her profit percent.
    Answer: CP = ₹2,400 + ₹600 = ₹3,000. Profit = ₹3,600 − ₹3,000 = ₹600. Profit
    % = 600/3,000 × 100% = 20%.
    Teacher explains: Money spent on repairs, transport or cleaning before
    selling is called OVERHEADS, and it is part of what the article really cost
    her. So the true CP is ₹2,400 + ₹600 = ₹3,000. Then
    profit = ₹3,600 − ₹3,000 = ₹600, and profit % = 600/3,000 × 100% = 20%. The
    common mistake is to forget the repairs: 1,200/2,400 would give 50%, which
    is far too big. Now you try: bought for ₹900, transport ₹100, sold for
    ₹1,150; profit %? (15%)

D5. A shopkeeper buys a table fan for ₹1,200 and wants a profit of 15%. At what
    price should he sell it?
    Answer: Profit = 15% of ₹1,200 = ₹180. SP = ₹1,200 + ₹180 = ₹1,380.
    Teacher explains: Method 1: find the profit first. 15% of
    ₹1,200 = 15/100 × 1,200 = ₹180. Add it on: SP = ₹1,380. Method 2: think of
    the CP as 100%. With a 15% profit the SP is 115% of the CP:
    115/100 × 1,200 = ₹1,380. Both methods agree. Method 2 is quicker when there
    are many prices to find. Check: profit 180, and 180/1,200 × 100% = 15%. Now
    you try: CP ₹640 at 25% profit; SP? (₹800)

D6. A fruit seller buys a crate of mangoes for ₹1,500. Some mangoes go bad and
    he sells the crate at a loss of 8%. What is the selling price?
    Answer: Loss = 8% of ₹1,500 = ₹120. SP = ₹1,500 − ₹120 = ₹1,380.
    Teacher explains: With a loss we SUBTRACT: loss = 8/100 × 1,500 = ₹120, so
    SP = ₹1,500 − ₹120 = ₹1,380. Or: CP is 100%, and with an 8% loss the SP is
    92% of the CP: 92/100 × 1,500 = ₹1,380. Notice that the same CP with a 15%
    profit would give 115% of the CP, while a 15% loss gives 85%. Ask the child
    to say "profit means more than 100%, loss means less than 100%". Now you
    try: CP ₹2,500 at a loss of 12%; SP? (₹2,200)

D7. Sunita sold a saree for ₹1,320 and made a profit of 10%. What did she pay
    for it? Rahul says: "10% of ₹1,320 is ₹132, so the CP was ₹1,188." Is he
    right?
    Answer: CP = ₹1,200. Rahul is wrong: the 10% is on the CP, not on the SP.
    Teacher explains: The profit percent is always a percent of the COST price,
    and we do not know the CP yet. Think of the CP as 100 parts. A 10% profit
    means the SP is 110 parts. So 110% of CP = ₹1,320, then 1% of
    CP = 1,320 ÷ 110 = ₹12, and 100% of CP = ₹1,200. As one line:
    CP = 1,320 × 100 ÷ 110 = ₹1,200. Check: 10% of ₹1,200 = ₹120 and
    ₹1,200 + ₹120 = ₹1,320. Rahul's answer fails the check: 10% of ₹1,188 is
    about ₹119, not ₹132. For a loss, the SP is (100 − loss %) parts: sold for
    ₹855 at a 5% loss gives CP = 855 × 100 ÷ 95 = ₹900. Now you try: SP ₹690 at
    15% profit; CP? (₹600)

D8. A jacket has a marked price of ₹1,500. The shop gives a discount of 20%. How
    much is the discount and what does the customer pay?
    Answer: Discount = 20% of ₹1,500 = ₹300. Sale
    price = ₹1,500 − ₹300 = ₹1,200.
    Teacher explains: The MARKED PRICE (MP) is the price written on the tag. A
    DISCOUNT is always worked out on the MARKED price: 20/100 × 1,500 = ₹300.
    The customer pays the SELLING price (sale price) = MP − discount = ₹1,200.
    Quicker: a 20% discount means you pay 80% of the MP:
    80/100 × 1,500 = ₹1,200. Signs like "Flat 20% off" or "Sale: 20% discount"
    mean the same. Now you try: MP ₹850, discount 10%. (₹85 off; pay ₹765)

D9. In a Diwali sale, a toy marked ₹2,400 is sold for ₹1,800. What is the
    discount percent?
    Answer: Discount = ₹600. Discount % = 600/2,400 × 100% = 25%.
    Teacher explains: Discount = MP − SP = ₹2,400 − ₹1,800 = ₹600. Discount
    percent is compared with the MARKED price (not the sale price and not the
    cost price): 600/2,400 = 1/4 = 25%. Check: 25% of ₹2,400 = ₹600, and
    ₹2,400 − ₹600 = ₹1,800. When comparing sales in two shops, compare the final
    prices, not just the percents. Now you try: MP ₹500, sold for ₹425; discount
    %? (15%)

D10. A shop gives a discount of 20% on a ₹1,000 watch, and then another 10% off
     the sale price at the counter. Is that the same as a 30% discount?
     ₹1,000 --(−20%)--> ₹800 --(−10%)--> ₹720
     Answer: No. After 20% off: ₹800. After 10% off ₹800: ₹720. Total discount
     ₹280, which is 28%, not 30%.
     Teacher explains: In SUCCESSIVE discounts the second discount is taken on
     the NEW price. First: 20% of ₹1,000 = ₹200, price ₹800. Second: 10% of
     ₹800 = ₹80 (not ₹100), price ₹720. The customer saves ₹1,000 − ₹720 = ₹280,
     which is 280/1,000 × 100% = 28%. A single 30% discount would give ₹700,
     which is better for the customer. Using the "pay part": 80% then 90% means
     0.8 × 0.9 = 0.72 of the price, so ₹720. Now you try: ₹500 with 10% then
     10%. (₹405; total 19%)

D11. A pair of shoes costs ₹2,000 before tax. GST of 12% is added. What is the
     tax and what is the bill? Another item priced ₹800 has a 10% discount and
     then 5% GST on the sale price. What is its bill?
     Answer: Shoes: GST = 12% of ₹2,000 = ₹240; bill = ₹2,240. Second item:
     discount ₹80, sale price ₹720, GST 5% of ₹720 = ₹36, bill ₹756.
     Teacher explains: GST (Goods and Services Tax) is a tax paid to the
     government. The shop ADDS it to the price: 12/100 × 2,000 = ₹240, so the
     bill is ₹2,000 + ₹240 = ₹2,240, or 112% of ₹2,000. When there is a discount
     as well, the discount comes off first and the GST is charged on the price
     after discount: 10% of ₹800 = ₹80, ₹800 − ₹80 = ₹720; 5% of ₹720 = ₹36;
     bill ₹720 + ₹36 = ₹756. Different goods have different GST rates (for
     example 5%, 12%, 18%), and many bills show the GST in a separate line. Now
     you try: ₹1,500 plus 18% GST. (₹1,770)

D12. A shopkeeper buys a mixer for ₹4,000. He marks it 25% above the cost price
     and then gives a 10% discount. Find the marked price, the selling price and
     his profit percent.
     CP ₹4,000 --(+25%)--> MP ₹5,000 --(−10%)--> SP ₹4,500
     Answer: MP = ₹5,000. SP = ₹4,500. Profit ₹500, which is 12.5% of the CP.
     Teacher explains: Step 1: marked 25% above CP: 25% of ₹4,000 = ₹1,000, so
     MP = ₹5,000. Step 2: discount 10% of the MARKED price: ₹500, so
     SP = ₹4,500. Step 3: profit = SP − CP = ₹4,500 − ₹4,000 = ₹500, and profit
     % is on the COST price: 500/4,000 × 100% = 12.5%. Notice the three
     different prices and which one each percent belongs to: the mark-up is on
     the CP, the discount on the MP, and the profit on the CP. This is how shops
     can offer a "sale" and still make money. Now you try: CP ₹800, marked 50%
     above, discount 20%. (MP ₹1,200, SP ₹960, profit 20%)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Find the profit or loss. Write "profit ₹__", "loss ₹__" or "neither".
    a) CP ₹40, SP ₹55          b) CP ₹120, SP ₹100
    c) CP ₹75, SP ₹90          d) CP ₹500, SP ₹460
    e) CP ₹1,200, SP ₹1,350    f) CP ₹250, SP ₹250

    i) CP ₹65, SP ₹58          j) CP ₹8,000, SP ₹9,250

    s) CP ₹4,500, SP ₹4,499    t) CP ₹620, SP ₹855
    Answers: a) profit ₹15   b) loss ₹20   c) profit ₹15   d) loss ₹40
             e) profit ₹150   f) neither   g) profit ₹2   h) loss ₹450
             i) loss ₹7   j) profit ₹1,250   k) profit ₹6   l) loss ₹30
             m) profit ₹425   n) neither   o) loss ₹1,250   p) profit ₹42
             q) loss ₹150   r) profit ₹51   s) loss ₹1   t) profit ₹235

M2. Find the profit percent. (profit ÷ CP × 100%)
    a) CP ₹100, SP ₹120       b) CP ₹200, SP ₹250       c) CP ₹50, SP ₹60
    d) CP ₹80, SP ₹100        e) CP ₹400, SP ₹500       f) CP ₹250, SP ₹300
    g) CP ₹60, SP ₹75         h) CP ₹500, SP ₹525       i) CP ₹1,200, SP ₹1,500
    j) CP ₹900, SP ₹999       k) CP ₹40, SP ₹50         l) CP ₹750, SP ₹900
    m) CP ₹1,000, SP ₹1,125   n) CP ₹160, SP ₹200       o) CP ₹320, SP ₹400
    p) CP ₹2,500, SP ₹2,800   q) CP ₹5,000, SP ₹5,600   r) CP ₹125, SP ₹150
    s) CP ₹440, SP ₹462       t) CP ₹700, SP ₹770
    Answers: a) 20%   b) 25%   c) 20%   d) 25%   e) 25%   f) 20%   g) 25%
             h) 5%   i) 25%   j) 11%   k) 25%   l) 20%   m) 12.5%   n) 25%
             o) 25%   p) 12%   q) 12%   r) 20%   s) 5%   t) 10%

M3. Find the loss percent. (loss ÷ CP × 100%)
    a) CP ₹100, SP ₹90        b) CP ₹200, SP ₹150       c) CP ₹50, SP ₹45
    d) CP ₹80, SP ₹60         e) CP ₹400, SP ₹380       f) CP ₹250, SP ₹200
    g) CP ₹60, SP ₹51         h) CP ₹500, SP ₹350       i) CP ₹1,200, SP ₹1,080
    j) CP ₹900, SP ₹810       k) CP ₹40, SP ₹38         l) CP ₹750, SP ₹600
    m) CP ₹1,000, SP ₹875     n) CP ₹160, SP ₹120       o) CP ₹320, SP ₹304
    p) CP ₹2,500, SP ₹2,000   q) CP ₹5,000, SP ₹4,600   r) CP ₹125, SP ₹100
    s) CP ₹440, SP ₹396       t) CP ₹700, SP ₹434

             h) 30%   i) 10%   j) 10%   k) 5%   l) 20%   m) 12.5%   n) 25%
             o) 5%   p) 20%   q) 8%   r) 20%   s) 10%   t) 38%

M4. Find the profit or loss percent. Where overheads are given, add them to the

             i) CP ₹3,000, loss ₹1,200 = 40%   j) CP ₹800, profit ₹100 = 12.5%
             k) CP ₹2,000, loss ₹350 = 17.5%   l) loss ₹360 = 12%
             m) CP ₹15,000, loss ₹1,800 = 12%   n) CP ₹540, profit ₹108 = 20%
             o) CP ₹6,000, loss ₹1,200 = 20%   p) CP ₹900, profit ₹225 = 25%

M5. Find the selling price.
    a) CP ₹500, profit 10%     b) CP ₹800, profit 25%

             s) ₹1,050   t) ₹608

M6. Find the cost price. (CP = SP × 100 ÷ (100 + profit %) or SP × 100 ÷ (100 −
