M7. Is it a magic square (rows, columns and both diagonals with the same sum)?
    If yes, give the constant.
    a)                  b)                  c)                  d)
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    |  4 |  2 |  9 |    | 16 |  6 |  8 |    |  8 |  6 | 16 |    |  9 |  2 |  7 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    |  3 |  5 |  7 |    | 18 | 10 |  2 |    | 18 | 10 |  2 |    |  8 |  6 |  4 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    |  8 |  1 |  6 |    |  4 | 14 | 12 |    |  4 | 14 | 12 |    |  3 | 10 |  5 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+

    e)                  f)                  g)                  h)
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    | 12 | 16 | 17 |    | 30 |  5 | 40 |    | 12 | 17 | 16 |    |  4 |  9 |  2 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    | 19 | 15 | 11 |    | 15 | 25 | 35 |    | 19 | 15 | 11 |    |  3 |  5 |  7 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    | 14 | 13 | 18 |    | 20 | 45 | 10 |    | 14 | 13 | 18 |    |  8 |  1 |  6 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+

    i)                  j)                  k)                  l)
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    | 40 |  5 | 30 |    |  7 |  2 |  9 |    |  3 |  3 |  3 |    |  2 |  7 |  6 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    | 15 | 25 | 35 |    |  8 |  6 |  4 |    |  3 |  3 |  3 |    |  9 |  5 |  1 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    | 20 | 45 | 10 |    |  3 | 10 |  5 |    |  3 |  3 |  3 |    |  4 |  3 |  8 |
    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+    +----+----+----+
    Answers: a) no  b) no  c) yes, 30  d) no  e) no  f) no  g) yes, 45  h) yes,
             15  i) yes, 75  j) yes, 18  k) yes, 9  l) yes, 15

M8. Number riddles: who am I? (Each riddle has exactly one answer.)
    a) I am a 2-digit number; I am a multiple of 9; my digits add up to 9; my
       ones digit is 5.
    b) I am a 2-digit number; I am a square number; I am odd; my digits add up
       to 13.
    c) I am a 2-digit number; I am a prime number; my tens digit is 3 more than
       my ones digit.
    d) I am a 2-digit number; I am a factor of 90; I am odd; my digits add up to
       9.
    e) I am a 2-digit number; I am a multiple of 6; I am a multiple of 8; I am
       between 50 and 90.
    f) I am a 2-digit number; I am 1 more than a multiple of 5; I am a square
       number; I am even; I am between 20 and 50.
    g) I am a 3-digit number; I am a square number; my digits add up to 1.
    h) I am a 2-digit number; I am a prime number; my ones digit is 9; my digits
       add up to 16.
    i) I am a 2-digit number; I am a multiple of 11; I am even; I am a multiple
       of 4; my digits add up to 8.
    j) I am a 2-digit number; I am a factor of 60; my digits add up to 3; I am a
       multiple of 5.
    k) I am a 3-digit number; I am a multiple of 25; I am odd; my digits add up
       to 17; I am between 500 and 800.
    l) I am a 2-digit number; I am 1 more than a multiple of 7; I am 1 more than
       a multiple of 3; I am odd; I am between 20 and 60.
    m) I am a 2-digit number; I am a square number; my tens digit is 2 more than
       my ones digit.
    n) I am a 2-digit number; I am a prime number; my digits add up to 10; I am
       between 30 and 60.
    o) I am a 3-digit number; I am a multiple of 100; I am a square number; I am
       between 200 and 900.
    p) I am a 2-digit number; I am a multiple of 12; my digits add up to 15.
    Answers: a) 45   b) 49   c) 41   d) 45   e) 72   f) 36   g) 100   h) 79
             i) 44   j) 30   k) 575   l) 43   m) 64   n) 37   o) 400   p) 96

M9. Cryptarithms: each letter is a different digit and no number starts with 0.
    Find the digits. (When two equations are given, both must be true.)
    a) AA + AA = BBC              b) BC × C = AB
    c) AC × A = BBB               d) BA × B = ACB
    e) C + A = AB                 f) CB + B = BC
    g) BA + BA = CAA              h) AB + BA = 121; A − B = 5
    i) AB − BA = 45; A + B = 7    j) A + B = 10; A − B = 4
    k) AB × 2 = BA + 1            l) ABCD × 4 = DCBA
    Answers: a) A = 5, B = 1, C = 0   b) A = 8, B = 4, C = 2
             c) A = 3, B = 1, C = 7   d) A = 1, B = 4, C = 6
             e) A = 1, B = 0, C = 9   f) B = 9, C = 8   g) A = 0, B = 5, C = 1
             h) A = 8, B = 3   i) A = 6, B = 1   j) A = 7, B = 3
             k) A = 3, B = 7   l) A = 2, B = 1, C = 7, D = 8

M10. Matchstick equations (digits drawn as in D11; + has 2 sticks, − has 1, =
     has 2). Move exactly ONE stick to make each equation true. Each has one
     answer.
     a) 2 − 9 = 5   b) 4 − 1 = 6   c) 8 − 3 = 9   d) 7 − 5 = 4
     e) 9 + 7 = 9   f) 5 + 4 = 0   g) 6 − 9 = 6   h) 0 + 7 = 1
     i) 1 + 5 = 2   j) 4 + 2 = 9   k) 3 + 2 = 4   l) 4 − 5 = 8
     Answers: a) 2 + 3 = 5   b) 4 + 1 = 5   c) 6 + 3 = 9   d) 7 − 3 = 4
              e) 8 + 1 = 9   f) 5 + 4 = 9   g) 6 − 0 = 6   h) 8 − 7 = 1
              i) 7 − 5 = 2   j) 4 + 2 = 6   k) 2 + 2 = 4   l) 4 + 5 = 9

M11. Number pyramids: each brick is the sum of the two bricks under it. Fill in
     the missing bricks.
     a)                    b)
           [  15]                [ __ ]
        [ __ ][   7]          [  11][  13]
     [   3][ __ ][   2]    [   4][ __ ][ __ ]

     c)                    d)
           [  28]                [ __ ]
        [ __ ][  13]          [ __ ][ __ ]
     [  10][ __ ][ __ ]    [  12][   9][  15]

     e)                          f)
              [  20]                      [  34]
           [   8][  12]                [  17][  17]
        [   3][ __ ][ __ ]          [ __ ][ __ ][ __ ]
     [ __ ][   2][   3][ __ ]    [   5][ __ ][ __ ][   2]

     g)                          h)
              [  30]                      [ 100]
           [ __ ][ __ ]                [ __ ][ __ ]
        [  11][ __ ][   9]          [ __ ][ __ ][  20]
     [   7][   4][ __ ][ __ ]    [ __ ][  20][   5][ __ ]

     i)                          j)
              [ __ ]                      [ __ ]
           [ __ ][  23]                [ __ ][  18]
        [  11][ __ ][ __ ]          [   9][ __ ][ __ ]
     [ __ ][ __ ][   4][   6]    [ __ ][ __ ][   7][   3]

     k)                          l)
              [  42]                      [ __ ]
           [ __ ][ __ ]                [ __ ][  27]
        [ __ ][ __ ][ __ ]          [ __ ][ __ ][ __ ]
     [   6][ __ ][   3][   9]    [  11][ __ ][   9][   5]
     Answers:
       a)                    b)
             [  15]                [  24]
          [   8][   7]          [  11][  13]
       [   3][   5][   2]    [   4][   7][   6]

       c)                    d)
             [  28]                [  45]
          [  15][  13]          [  21][  24]
       [  10][   5][   8]    [  12][   9][  15]

       e)                          f)
                [  20]                      [  34]
             [   8][  12]                [  17][  17]
          [   3][   5][   7]          [   8][   9][   8]
       [   1][   2][   3][   4]    [   5][   3][   6][   2]

       g)                          h)
                [  30]                      [ 100]
             [  16][  14]                [  55][  45]
          [  11][   5][   9]          [  30][  25][  20]
       [   7][   4][   1][   8]    [  10][  20][   5][  15]

       i)                          j)
                [  47]                      [  35]
             [  24][  23]                [  17][  18]
          [  11][  13][  10]          [   9][   8][  10]
       [   2][   9][   4][   6]    [   8][   1][   7][   3]

       k)                          l)
                [  42]                      [  55]
             [  21][  21]                [  28][  27]
          [  12][   9][  12]          [  15][  13][  14]
       [   6][   6][   3][   9]    [  11][   4][   9][   5]

M12. Put +, −, × or ÷ in each box to make the sentence true (× and ÷ are done
     before + and −). Each has only one answer.
     a) 6 □ 3 □ 2 □ 8 = 3    b) 5 □ 5 □ 1 = 9        c) 6 □ 6 □ 8 □ 2 = 7
     d) 3 □ 9 □ 1 □ 9 = 19   e) 8 □ 6 □ 6 □ 2 = 14   f) 2 □ 7 □ 4 = 30
     g) 6 □ 3 □ 9 □ 9 = 8    h) 9 □ 2 □ 2 □ 4 = 6    i) 3 □ 7 □ 5 □ 7 = 9
     j) 4 □ 4 □ 6 □ 4 = 40   k) 2 □ 8 □ 5 □ 9 = 12   l) 5 □ 5 □ 4 = 5
     m) 4 □ 4 □ 2 □ 2 = 9    n) 2 □ 8 □ 8 = 24       o) 5 □ 6 □ 5 □ 5 = 12
     p) 9 □ 3 □ 9 □ 2 = 10
     Answers: a) 6 + 3 + 2 − 8 = 3   b) 5 + 5 − 1 = 9   c) 6 ÷ 6 + 8 − 2 = 7
              d) 3 × 9 + 1 − 9 = 19   e) 8 − 6 + 6 × 2 = 14   f) 2 + 7 × 4 = 30
              g) 6 + 3 − 9 ÷ 9 = 8   h) 9 + 2 ÷ 2 − 4 = 6   i) 3 × 7 − 5 − 7 = 9
              j) 4 × 4 + 6 × 4 = 40   k) 2 × 8 + 5 − 9 = 12   l) 5 ÷ 5 + 4 = 5
              m) 4 + 4 + 2 ÷ 2 = 9   n) 2 × 8 + 8 = 24   o) 5 + 6 + 5 ÷ 5 = 12
              p) 9 ÷ 3 + 9 − 2 = 10

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: begin with 4 × 4 sudokus with many clues and 3 × 3 KenKens, use
  number cards that can be moved around, solve puzzles in pairs and talk through
  each step aloud ("the 3 must go here because ..."), and use real matchsticks.
  Harder: harder 9 × 9 sudokus, 5 × 5 and 6 × 6 KenKens, making a 4 × 4 magic
  square from scratch, creating their own riddles and cryptarithms with exactly
  one answer, and SEND + MORE = MONEY.


================================================================================
TOPIC 5.35 — NUMBER BASES: BINARY AND OTHER BASES (PLACE VALUE REVISITED)
================================================================================
WHAT THE CHILD MUST LEARN
  - Our number system is BASE TEN: ten digits 0 to 9, and each place is worth 10
    times the place on its right (ones, tens, hundreds, ...). Other bases work
    the same way with a different "group size".
  - BINARY (base two) uses only the digits 0 and 1. The place values from the
    right are 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32, 64, 128, ... (each twice the one before). A
    small ₂ shows the base: 1011₂ = 8 + 2 + 1 = 11.
  - Binary to decimal: add the place values that have a 1. Decimal to binary:
    take away the biggest place value that fits, again and again
    (45 = 32 + 8 + 4 + 1 = 101101₂), or divide by 2 repeatedly and read the
    remainders from the bottom up.
  - Count in binary: 0, 1, 10, 11, 100, 101, 110, 111, 1000, ... Know every
    number from 0 to 31 (five binary digits) and numbers up to 255 (eight binary
    digits = one BYTE). Even numbers end in 0, odd numbers in 1.
  - Add in binary with the facts 0 + 1 = 1, 1 + 1 = 10₂ (write 0, carry 1) and
    1 + 1 + 1 = 11₂ (write 1, carry 1). Doubling a binary number puts a 0 on the
    end.
  - Computers use binary because a switch, a light or a tiny electric charge has
    two states, off (0) and on (1). One binary digit is a BIT; 8 bits make a
    BYTE, which can store 256 different values, 0 to 255.
  - BASE FIVE uses digits 0 to 4 and place values 1, 5, 25, 125; BASE EIGHT
    (octal) uses 0 to 7 and place values 1, 8, 64, 512. Each group of three
    binary digits makes one octal digit.
  - Base 60 lives on in time (60 seconds = 1 minute, 60 minutes = 1 hour) and
    angles, from the ancient Babylonians. Base 12 lives on in dozens (12) and
    gross (144), and in 12 months and 12 hours on a clock.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE
--------------------
D1. Write 3,572 and 1101₂ in place-value charts. What is each place worth in
    base ten and in base two?
    base ten:  thousands  hundreds  tens  ones
                 1,000       100      10     1
                   3          5        7     2

    base two:  eights  fours  twos  ones
                 8       4      2     1
                 1       1      0     1
    Answer: 3,572 = 3,000 + 500 + 70 + 2. 1101₂ = 8 + 4 + 0 + 1 = 13.
    Teacher explains: In base ten each place is 10 times the place on its right:
    1, 10, 100, 1,000, ... because we make a new group every time we reach TEN.
    In base two we make a new group every time we reach TWO, so each place is 2
    times the place on its right: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, ... The idea of place value is
    exactly the same; only the group size changes. A digit in base two can only
    be 0 or 1, just as a digit in base ten can only be 0 to 9. Read 1101₂ as
    "one one zero one, base two", NOT "one thousand one hundred and one". Now
    you try: what is 111₂? (4 + 2 + 1 = 7)

D2. Change 101101₂ into a base-ten number.
    place value:  32  16   8   4   2   1
    digit:         1   0   1   1   0   1
    value:        32   0   8   4   0   1
    Answer: 101101₂ = 32 + 8 + 4 + 1 = 45.
    Teacher explains: Write the place values above the digits, STARTING FROM THE
    RIGHT: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32. Every place with a 1 counts its place value;
    every 0 counts nothing. Add: 32 + 8 + 4 + 1 = 45. The common mistake is to
    start the place values from the left, which gives the wrong answer for any
    number that is not a mirror image. Check with the ones digit: it is 1, so
    the number is odd, and 45 is odd ✓. Now you try: 11010₂. (16 + 8 + 2 = 26)

D3. Change 45 into binary by dividing by 2 again and again.
    45 ÷ 2 = 22 remainder 1   ↑
    22 ÷ 2 = 11 remainder 0   |
    11 ÷ 2 =  5 remainder 1   |  read the
     5 ÷ 2 =  2 remainder 1   |  remainders
     2 ÷ 2 =  1 remainder 0   |  upwards
     1 ÷ 2 =  0 remainder 1   |
    Answer: 45 = 101101₂.
    Teacher explains: Each division by 2 makes pairs; the remainder (0 or 1) is
    the digit that is left over in that place. The FIRST remainder is the ones
    digit, so read the remainders from the BOTTOM UP: 1, 0, 1, 1, 0, 1
    → 101101₂. Stop when the quotient reaches 0. Check by changing back:
    32 + 8 + 4 + 1 = 45 ✓ (this is D2 the other way round). Reading the
    remainders top-down gives 101101 here only by luck, because this number is
    the same both ways; try 6: remainders 0, 1, 1 give 110₂, not 011. Now you
    try: 19 in binary. (10011₂)

D4. Change 200 into binary using the place values 128, 64, 32, 16, 8, 4, 2, 1.
    128  64  32  16   8   4   2   1
      1   1   0   0   1   0   0   0
    Answer: 200 = 128 + 64 + 8 = 11001000₂.
    Teacher explains: Take away the biggest place value that fits, and put a 1
    there. 128 fits in 200: 200 − 128 = 72. 64 fits in 72: 72 − 64 = 8. 32 does
    not fit in 8, nor does 16: write 0s. 8 fits: 8 − 8 = 0. The rest are 0s. So
    200 = 128 + 64 + 8 = 11001000₂. Do not forget the zeros in the middle and at
    the end: they hold the places, just as the 0 does in 305. Now you try: 100
    in binary. (64 + 32 + 4 = 1100100₂)

D5. Count from 0 to 16 in binary. What patterns do you see?
    0  0      4  100     8  1000    12  1100    16  10000
    1  1      5  101     9  1001    13  1101
    2  10     6  110    10  1010    14  1110
    3  11     7  111    11  1011    15  1111
    Answer: See the table. The last digit goes 0, 1, 0, 1, ...; a new place
    starts at 2, 4, 8 and 16.
    Teacher explains: Counting in binary is like counting in base ten, but each
    place "fills up" at 1 instead of at 9. 1 + 1 makes 10 (one two and no ones),
    just as 9 + 1 makes 10 in base ten. The ones digit alternates 0, 1, 0, 1:
    even numbers end in 0 and odd numbers end in 1. The twos digit goes 0, 0, 1,
    1, 0, 0, 1, 1; the fours digit 0, 0, 0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1. Numbers made of all
    1s (1, 11, 111, 1111) are one less than a power of 2: 1, 3, 7, 15. The
    powers of 2 themselves are 1 followed by 0s. Now you try: what comes after
    10111₂? (11000₂)

D6. Add 1011₂ + 110₂ in binary. Check in base ten.
      carries:  1 1
        1 0 1 1
      +   1 1 0
      ---------
      1 0 0 0 1
    Answer: 1011₂ + 110₂ = 10001₂. Check: 11 + 6 = 17 = 16 + 1 ✓.
    Teacher explains: Line up the digits on the right, as in column addition.
    The binary facts are 0 + 0 = 0, 0 + 1 = 1, 1 + 1 = 10₂ (write 0, carry 1),
    and 1 + 1 + 1 = 11₂ (write 1, carry 1). Ones: 1 + 0 = 1. Twos: 1 + 1 = 10₂,
    write 0 carry 1. Fours: 0 + 1 plus the carried 1 makes 10₂, write 0 carry 1.
    Eights: 1 plus the carried 1 makes 10₂, write 0 carry 1, into a new sixteens
    place. Answer 10001₂. The common mistake is writing "2", which is not a
    binary digit. Now you try: 111₂ + 1₂. (1000₂)

D7. Why do computers use binary? How many different values can one byte (8 bits)
    store?
    Answer: A computer is built from millions of tiny switches that are either
    off (0) or on (1). One byte stores 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 × 2 = 256
    values, from 00000000₂ = 0 to 11111111₂ = 255.
    Teacher explains: A switch, a transistor, a magnetic spot or a pit on a disc
    has two clear states that are easy to tell apart, even when the signal is
    weak: off and on, 0 and 1. Each binary digit is a BIT. With 1 bit we have 2
    patterns (0, 1); with 2 bits 4 (00, 01, 10, 11); every extra bit doubles the
    number of patterns. So 8 bits give 2 multiplied by itself 8 times = 256
    patterns. The biggest is 128 + 64 + 32 + 16 + 8 + 4 + 2 + 1 = 255. Letters,
    colours and sounds are all stored as numbers in binary: in the common code
    for text, capital A is 65 = 1000001₂. Now you try: how many patterns do 4
    bits give? (16)

D8. In base five we group in fives. Change 243₅ to base ten, and change 38 to
    base five.
    place value:  25   5   1
    243₅:          2   4   3   = 2 × 25 + 4 × 5 + 3 = 73
    38:            1   2   3   = 1 × 25 + 2 × 5 + 3 = 38
    Answer: 243₅ = 73. 38 = 123₅.
    Teacher explains: Base five has the digits 0, 1, 2, 3, 4 and the place
    values 1, 5, 25, 125 (each 5 times the one before). 243₅ means 2
    twenty-fives, 4 fives and 3 ones: 50 + 20 + 3 = 73. For 38: how many 25s?
    One, leaving 13. How many 5s in 13? Two, leaving 3. So
    38 = 1 × 25 + 2 × 5 + 3 = 123₅. Or divide by 5: 38 ÷ 5 = 7 remainder 3,
    7 ÷ 5 = 1 remainder 2, 1 ÷ 5 = 0 remainder 1; read upwards: 123₅. A digit 5
    or more can never appear in base five. Think of tally marks in groups of
    five, or ₹5 and ₹25 notes. Now you try: 34₅ in base ten. (19)

D9. Change 157₈ to base ten, and 100 to base eight. Then change 101110₂ to base
    eight using groups of three.
    Answer: 157₈ = 111. 100 = 144₈. 101110₂ = 56₈ (= 46).
    Teacher explains: Base eight (OCTAL) has the digits 0 to 7 and place values
    1, 8, 64, 512. 157₈ = 1 × 64 + 5 × 8 + 7 = 64 + 40 + 7 = 111. For 100: one
    64 leaves 36; four 8s make 32, leaving 4: 100 = 144₈. Because 8 = 2 × 2 × 2,
    every octal digit is exactly three binary digits: split 101110₂ into groups
    of three from the right: 101 | 110 = 5 | 6, so 56₈. Check: 5 × 8 + 6 = 46,
    and 101110₂ = 32 + 8 + 4 + 2 = 46 ✓. Programmers use octal (and base 16) as
    a short way to write long binary numbers. Now you try: 77₈ in base ten. (63)

D10. The Babylonians counted in base sixty. We still do for time. Write
     2 h 15 min 30 s in seconds. Write 10,000 seconds in hours, minutes and
     seconds.
     Answer: 2 h 15 min 30 s = 7,200 + 900 + 30 = 8,130 s.
     10,000 s = 2 h 46 min 40 s.
     Teacher explains: Time is a base-60 system: 60 seconds make 1 minute and
     60 minutes make 1 hour, so the "place values" are 1 s, 60 s and
     60 × 60 = 3,600 s. 2 h = 2 × 3,600 = 7,200 s; 15 min = 15 × 60 = 900 s; add
     30 s: 8,130 s. Going the other way, divide by 3,600: 10,000 ÷ 3,600 = 2
     remainder 2,800, so 2 h; then 2,800 ÷ 60 = 46 remainder 40, so 46 min 40 s.
     Angles also use 60: 1 degree = 60 minutes of arc. The Babylonians liked 60
     because it can be divided exactly by 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 10, 12, 15, 20 and 30.
     Now you try: 5,000 s in hours, minutes and seconds. (1 h 23 min 20 s)

D11. A shopkeeper has 3 gross, 5 dozen and 7 single pencils. How many pencils is
     that? What is it in base twelve?
     Answer: 3 × 144 + 5 × 12 + 7 = 432 + 60 + 7 = 499 pencils = 357₁₂.
     Teacher explains: A DOZEN is 12 and a GROSS is a dozen dozens,
     12 × 12 = 144. These are the place values of BASE TWELVE: 1, 12, 144. So 3
     gross 5 dozen 7 is the base-twelve number 357₁₂ = 499. Base twelve needs
     twelve digits, so we add A for ten and B for eleven: 10 = A₁₂, 11 = B₁₂,
     12 = 10₁₂, 23 = 1B₁₂. Twelve is handy because it can be divided by 2, 3, 4
     and 6; that is why eggs come in dozens and a clock has 12 hours. Now you
     try: 2 gross and 1 dozen. (2 × 144 + 12 = 300 = 210₁₂)

D12. A magic trick uses five cards. A friend chooses a number from 1 to 31 and
     tells you which cards it is on. You add the FIRST number on each of those
     cards. The friend says "cards 1, 2 and 4" — what is the number? Why does
     the trick work?
     card 1 starts: 1, 3, 5, 7, ...    (ones place is 1)
     card 2 starts: 2, 3, 6, 7, ...    (twos place is 1)
     card 3 starts: 4, 5, 6, 7, ...    (fours place is 1)
     card 4 starts: 8, 9, 10, 11, ...  (eights place is 1)
     card 5 starts: 16, 17, 18, 19, ... (sixteens place is 1)
     Answer: 1 + 2 + 8 = 11. It works because the cards are the binary place
     values: 11 = 1011₂.
     Teacher explains: Card 1 holds every number whose ONES binary digit is 1,
     card 2 every number whose TWOS digit is 1, card 3 the fours, card 4 the
     eights and card 5 the sixteens. The first number on each card is its place
     value: 1, 2, 4, 8, 16. "Cards 1, 2 and 4" means the binary digits for 1, 2
     and 8 are 1 and the others are 0: 01011₂ = 11. Adding the first numbers is
     just changing binary to decimal! Make the five cards: list the numbers 1 to
     31 in binary and copy each number onto the cards where it has a 1. Every
     number from 1 to 31 has a different set of cards. Now you try: cards 1, 3
     and 5. (1 + 4 + 16 = 21)

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY
--------------------------
M1. Write every number from 0 to 31 in binary.
     0 = ______        8 = ______       16 = ______       24 = ______
     1 = ______        9 = ______       17 = ______       25 = ______
     2 = ______       10 = ______       18 = ______       26 = ______
     3 = ______       11 = ______       19 = ______       27 = ______
     4 = ______       12 = ______       20 = ______       28 = ______
     5 = ______       13 = ______       21 = ______       29 = ______
     6 = ______       14 = ______       22 = ______       30 = ______
     7 = ______       15 = ______       23 = ______       31 = ______
    Answers:
       0 = 0₂            8 = 1000₂        16 = 10000₂       24 = 11000₂
       1 = 1₂            9 = 1001₂        17 = 10001₂       25 = 11001₂
       2 = 10₂          10 = 1010₂        18 = 10010₂       26 = 11010₂
       3 = 11₂          11 = 1011₂        19 = 10011₂       27 = 11011₂
       4 = 100₂         12 = 1100₂        20 = 10100₂       28 = 11100₂
       5 = 101₂         13 = 1101₂        21 = 10101₂       29 = 11101₂
       6 = 110₂         14 = 1110₂        22 = 10110₂       30 = 11110₂
       7 = 111₂         15 = 1111₂        23 = 10111₂       31 = 11111₂

M2. Write each binary number in base ten (all 32 five-digit binary numbers,
    mixed up).
    01011₂ = __       01010₂ = __       11000₂ = __       11100₂ = __
    01000₂ = __       11101₂ = __       10001₂ = __       10101₂ = __
    10111₂ = __       00000₂ = __       01110₂ = __       10010₂ = __
    00010₂ = __       10100₂ = __       00101₂ = __       10110₂ = __
    00110₂ = __       10000₂ = __       11110₂ = __       01100₂ = __
    01111₂ = __       11001₂ = __       00111₂ = __       01101₂ = __
    00001₂ = __       11010₂ = __       00011₂ = __       11011₂ = __
    11111₂ = __       10011₂ = __       00100₂ = __       01001₂ = __
    Answers:
      01011₂ = 11       01010₂ = 10       11000₂ = 24       11100₂ = 28
      01000₂ = 8        11101₂ = 29       10001₂ = 17       10101₂ = 21
      10111₂ = 23       00000₂ = 0        01110₂ = 14       10010₂ = 18
      00010₂ = 2        10100₂ = 20       00101₂ = 5        10110₂ = 22
      00110₂ = 6        10000₂ = 16       11110₂ = 30       01100₂ = 12
      01111₂ = 15       11001₂ = 25       00111₂ = 7        01101₂ = 13
      00001₂ = 1        11010₂ = 26       00011₂ = 3        11011₂ = 27
      11111₂ = 31       10011₂ = 19       00100₂ = 4        01001₂ = 9

     to p): write in hours, minutes and seconds.

M11. Base twelve (A = ten, B = eleven; place values 144, 12, 1). a) to f): how
     many single items? g) to p): write in base twelve.
     a) 1 gross 0 dozen 0     b) 0 gross 3 dozen 5     c) 2 gross 6 dozen 0

M12. a) to j): write <, > or =. k) to m): double each binary number (in binary).
     n) to p): odd or even?
     a) 1011₂ __ 11        b) 1000₂ __ 7         c) 10100₂ __ 21
     d) 34₅ __ 19          e) 77₈ __ 111111₂     f) 1100100₂ __ 99
     g) 101₅ __ 11010₂     h) 100₁₂ __ 144       i) 11111111₂ __ 256
     j) 11₈ __ 14₅         k) 2 × 101₂           l) 2 × 1101₂
     m) 2 × 10110₂         n) 101101₂            o) 1100100₂
     p) 11111111₂
     Answers: a) =   b) >   c) <   d) =   e) =   f) >   g) =   h) =   i) <
              j) =   k) 1010₂   l) 11010₂   m) 101100₂   n) odd   o) even
              p) odd

TEACHING TIPS
  Struggling: use cups of counters to trade two ones for one "two", two twos for
  one "four" and so on; make binary cards (1, 2, 4, 8, 16) face up (1) or face
  down (0); count on fingers in binary (up = 1, down = 0); always write the
  place values above the digits first. Harder: count to 1,023 on ten fingers,
  base 16 (hexadecimal) with digits 0 to F, subtracting and multiplying in
  binary, the binary magic card trick for 1 to 63, and finding which bases make
  "10 + 10 = 100" true.


================================================================================
MIXED REVISION TEST — STAGE 5
================================================================================
Two parts with the same number of questions: Part A is descriptive (word
problems and "explain" questions, written with working), Part B is
mathematical only. 1 mark each, 60 marks in all. Spread it over a week.

PART A — DESCRIPTIVE, 30 marks
  1.  The population of a state is 7,25,48,316. Write it in words in the
      Indian system and with international commas.
  2.  Explain why 10^5 means 100,000 and not 50.
  3.  A packet of biscuits weighs 0.235 kg. How many grams is that? How
      much do 1000 packets weigh in kg?
  4.  Two bells ring every 12 and 18 minutes. They ring together at 9:00.
      When do they next ring together? Explain using LCM.
  5.  Riya ate 2/3 of a cake and Aman ate 1/4 of the same cake. What
      fraction was eaten altogether? What fraction is left?
  6.  Draw an area model to show 3/4 × 2/5. Explain the answer.
  7.  How many 3/8 m pieces can be cut from 3/4 m of ribbon? Explain
      with a picture and with keep-change-flip.
  8.  A pen costs ₹12.60. How much do 3 pens cost? How much change from
      ₹50?
  9.  Explain why 1/3 = 0.333... never ends, but 1/8 = 0.125 does.
 10.  In a class of 40, 15% of children wear glasses. How many is that?
 11.  A recipe uses flour and sugar in the ratio 5 : 2. How much sugar is
      needed with 750 g of flour?
 12.  Share ₹360 between Asha and Ravi in the ratio 3 : 5. Show a bar
      model.
 13.  If 8 notebooks cost ₹240, how much do 13 notebooks cost?
 14.  The temperature at night was −5 °C. By noon it rose by 12 °C. What
      was the temperature at noon?
 15.  Explain, using a number line, why −3 − (−7) = 4.
 16.  Write an expression for "5 more than three times a number n".
      Evaluate it when n = 7.
 17.  A taxi charges ₹50 plus ₹15 per km. Write a formula for the fare
      F for d km. Find the fare for 12 km.
 18.  Solve 3x − 4 = 17 using the balance idea, and check your answer.
 19.  Matchsticks: 1 square uses 4 sticks, 2 squares in a row use 7, 3
      use 10. Find the rule for n squares. How many for 20 squares?
 20.  Plot A(2, 3), B(−2, 3) and C(−2, −1). Where must D be to make a
      rectangle ABCD? Which quadrant is D in?
 21.  Two angles on a straight line are in the ratio 2 : 7. Find them.
 22.  A triangle has angles 47° and 68°. Find the third angle and say what
      type of triangle it is.
 23.  Describe the steps to construct a 60° angle with ruler and compass.
 24.  A trapezium has parallel sides 9 cm and 15 cm and height 6 cm. Find
      its area.
 25.  A fish tank is 50 cm long, 30 cm wide and 40 cm high. How many litres
      of water does it hold when full?
 26.  Five friends scored 12, 15, 18, 11 and 14 runs. Find the mean and the
      median. What score would a sixth friend need to make the mean 15?
 27.  A bag has 3 red, 5 blue and 2 green marbles. What is the probability
      of picking a blue marble? Of not picking red?
 28.  A shopkeeper buys a fan for ₹900 and sells it for ₹1,200. Find the
      profit and the profit percent.
 29.  A train travels 150 km in 2 h 30 min. Find its average speed in km/h
      and in m/s.
 30.  Work backwards: I think of a number, double it, add 6 and get 30.
      What was my number? Explain.

PART B — MATHEMATICAL ONLY, 30 marks
 31.  1 crore = __ million        3.5 billion = __ crore
 32.  10^4 × 10^3 = 10^__         2^5 = __       3^4 = __
 33.  0.035 × 1000 = __           42.7 ÷ 100 = __
 34.  HCF and LCM of 84 and 126 = __ , __
 35.  Use Euclid's method: HCF(1071, 462) = __
 36.  2/3 + 3/4 = __     5/6 − 3/8 = __     3 1/4 − 1 2/3 = __
 37.  3/4 × 2/5 = __     2 1/2 × 1 1/3 = __
 38.  3/4 ÷ 3/8 = __     4 1/2 ÷ 1 1/2 = __     3 ÷ 1/4 = __
 39.  4.56 × 2.3 = __    12.6 ÷ 0.3 = __        7.2 − 3.456 = __
 40.  Write as decimals: 3/8, 7/20, 2/3 (recurring)
 41.  Write as percents: 0.45, 3/5, 1/8
 42.  15% of 640 = __    18 is __% of 24
 43.  Simplify the ratios: 36 : 48,  50 cm : 2 m
 44.  Fill the ratio table:  2 : 5 = 6 : __ = __ : 35
 45.  Order: −4, 3, −9, 0, −1, 7
 46.  −7 + 12 = __   −5 − (−9) = __   (−6) × (−4) = __   36 ÷ (−9) = __
 47.  Simplify: 4a + 3b − a + 5b − 2
 48.  If x = 2, find 5x³ and 2(x + 4) + 3
 49.  Solve: x + 13 = 21,  x/4 = 9,  2x + 7 = 25
 50.  nth term of 5, 9, 13, 17, ... = __ ; the 50th term = __
 51.  Quadrant of (−3, 5)? Reflect (4, −2) in the x-axis.
 52.  Complement of 37° = __ ; supplement of 118° = __
 53.  Fourth angle of a quadrilateral with 85°, 110°, 75° = __
 54.  Radius 7.5 cm, diameter = __ ; diameter 26 m, radius = __
 55.  Area of a triangle with base 14 cm, height 9 cm = __
 56.  Volume and surface area of a cube with side 6 cm = __ , __
 57.  Mode and range of 4, 7, 7, 2, 9, 7, 4 = __ , __
 58.  Marked price ₹800, discount 15%. Sale price = __
 59.  Simple interest on ₹600 at 5% per year for 3 years = __
 60.  101101 (base 2) = __ (base 10);  23 (base 10) = __ (base 2)

ANSWER KEY
  Part A (accept any clear, correct method)
   1. seven crore twenty-five lakh forty-eight thousand three hundred
      sixteen; 72,548,316
   2. 10^5 = 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 × 10 (five tens multiplied), not 10 × 5
   3. 235 g; 235 kg
   4. at 9:36 (LCM of 12 and 18 is 36)
   5. 11/12 eaten; 1/12 left
   6. 3/4 × 2/5 = 6/20 = 3/10 (6 of the 20 small rectangles are shaded
      twice)
   7. 2 pieces (3/4 ÷ 3/8 = 3/4 × 8/3 = 2)
   8. ₹37.80; ₹12.20 change
   9. 3 has a factor other than 2 or 5, so the division never finishes;
      8 = 2 × 2 × 2, so 1/8 = 125/1000 exactly
  10. 6 children
  11. 300 g of sugar
  12. Asha ₹135, Ravi ₹225 (8 parts; 1 part = ₹45)
  13. ₹390 (1 notebook = ₹30)
  14. 7 °C
  15. taking away −7 is the same as adding 7: −3 + 7 = 4
  16. 3n + 5; 26
  17. F = 50 + 15d; ₹230
  18. x = 7 (add 4 to both sides: 3x = 21; divide by 3); check
      3 × 7 − 4 = 17
  19. 3n + 1 sticks; 61 sticks
  20. D(2, −1); fourth quadrant
  21. 40° and 140°
  22. 65°; acute-angled (all angles less than 90°), scalene
  23. draw a ray; with centre at its end draw an arc cutting it; with the
      same radius and centre at that cut, draw an arc crossing the first;
      join the end to the crossing point
  24. 72 cm²
  25. 60 L (60,000 cm³)
  26. mean 14, median 14; 20 runs (6 × 15 = 90, 90 − 70 = 20)
  27. 5/10 = 1/2; 7/10
  28. profit ₹300; 33 1/3 %
  29. 60 km/h; 16 2/3 m/s
  30. 12 (undo: 30 − 6 = 24, 24 ÷ 2 = 12)
  Part B
  31. 10 ; 350                       32. 7 ; 32 ; 81
  33. 35 ; 0.427                     34. 42 ; 252
  35. 21                            36. 17/12 = 1 5/12 ; 11/24 ; 1 7/12
  37. 3/10 ; 3 1/3                  38. 2 ; 3 ; 12
  39. 10.488 ; 42 ; 3.744           40. 0.375 ; 0.35 ; 0.666...
  41. 45% ; 60% ; 12.5%             42. 96 ; 75%
  43. 3 : 4 ; 1 : 4                 44. 15 ; 14
  45. −9, −4, −1, 0, 3, 7           46. 5 ; 4 ; 24 ; −4
  47. 3a + 8b − 2                   48. 40 ; 15
  49. 8 ; 36 ; 9                    50. 4n + 1 ; 201
  51. second quadrant ; (4, 2)      52. 53° ; 62°
  53. 90°                           54. 15 cm ; 13 m
  55. 63 cm²                        56. 216 cm³ ; 216 cm²
  57. 7 ; 7                         58. ₹680
  59. ₹90                           60. 45 ; 10111

Scoring guide (out of 60):
  51-60  Ready for Stage 6.
  39-50  Nearly there — practise the topics of the missed questions for
         two weeks, then try those questions again.
  0-38   Keep working through Stage 5 (revise Stage 4 if needed); test
         again in a month.
  Compare the two parts: strong in B but weak in A means the child needs
  more word problems, diagrams and explaining; strong in A but weak in B
  means more drill practice for speed and accuracy.


================================================================================
END OF STAGE 5 — READINESS CHECKLIST FOR STAGE 6
================================================================================
The child is ready for Stage 6 (Grades 7-8) when they can usually:
  [ ] Read, write and compare very large numbers in both the Indian and
      international systems, and convert between them
  [ ] Use powers of 10 and exponent notation
  [ ] Multiply and divide decimals by 10, 100 and 1000
  [ ] Find HCF and LCM by listing, prime factors and Euclid's method
  [ ] Add, subtract, multiply and divide fractions and mixed numbers
  [ ] Carry out all four operations with decimals
  [ ] Convert between fractions, decimals and percentages, and know which
      fractions give recurring decimals
  [ ] Find percentages of amounts and express one amount as a percentage
      of another
  [ ] Simplify ratios, use ratio tables and share in a given ratio
  [ ] Use unit rates and the unitary method
  [ ] Order integers and carry out all four operations with them
  [ ] Write, simplify and evaluate algebraic expressions and formulas
  [ ] Solve one-step and two-step equations and check the solution
  [ ] Find and use the nth term of a simple sequence
  [ ] Plot and read points in all four quadrants
  [ ] Use angle facts (straight line, point, vertically opposite,
      complementary, supplementary, triangle and quadrilateral sums)
  [ ] Do basic ruler-and-compass constructions
  [ ] Name the parts of a circle and use d = 2r
  [ ] Find areas of triangles, parallelograms and trapezia
  [ ] Find volume and surface area of cubes and cuboids; recognise nets
  [ ] Find mean, median, mode and range and choose a sensible average
  [ ] Describe and calculate simple probabilities
  [ ] Solve problems on profit, loss, discount and simple interest
  [ ] Use speed = distance ÷ time and convert km/h to m/s
  [ ] Plan a simple budget and read bills and receipts
  [ ] Choose and use problem-solving strategies, and solve logic puzzles
  [ ] Convert between binary and decimal numbers

Rational numbers, algebraic identities, linear equations and graphs,
Pythagoras' theorem, congruence and similarity are the main new work of
Stage 6.
================================================================================
